## Description

Nathan Lambert and Sebastian Raschka are machine learning researchers, engineers, and educators. Nathan is the post-training lead at the Allen Institute for AI (Ai2) and the author of The RLHF Book. Sebastian Raschka is the author of Build a Large Language Model (From Scratch) and Build a Reasoning Model (From Scratch).

## Transcript

00:00:00 --> 00:00:05
- The following is a conversation all about the state-of-the-art in artificial

00:00:04 --> 00:00:08
intelligence, including some of the exciting technical breakthroughs and

00:00:07 --> 00:00:12
evelopments in AI that happened over the past year, and

00:00:11 --> 00:00:16
some of the interesting things we think might happen this upcoming

00:00:15 --> 00:00:20
year. At times, it does get super technical,

00:00:19 --> 00:00:24
but we do try to make sure that it remains accessible to folks

00:00:23 --> 00:00:28
outside the field without ever dumbing it down. It

00:00:27 --> 00:00:31
is a great honor and pleasure to be able to do this kind of

00:00:31 --> 00:00:35
episode with two of my favorite people in the AI

00:00:34 --> 00:00:38
community, Sebastian Raschka and Nathan

00:00:38 --> 00:00:42
Lambert. They are both widely respected machine

00:00:41 --> 00:00:46
learning researchers and engineers who also happen to be great

00:00:45 --> 00:00:50
communicators, educators, writers, and X posters.

00:00:49 --> 00:00:54
Sebastian is the author of two books

00:00:53 --> 00:00:58
I highly recommend for beginners and experts alike. First is

00:00:57 --> 00:01:01
Build a Large Language Model from Scratch

00:01:01 --> 00:01:06
and Build a Reasoning Model from Scratch. I

00:01:05 --> 00:01:10
truly believe in the machine learning world, the

00:01:09 --> 00:01:13
best way to learn and understand something is to build it

00:01:13 --> 00:01:17
yourself from scratch. Nathan is

00:01:17 --> 00:01:21
the post-training lead at the Allen Institute for AI,

00:01:21 --> 00:01:26
author of the definitive book on Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback.

00:01:26 --> 00:01:31
Both of them have great X accounts, great Substacks.

00:01:30 --> 00:01:35
Sebastian has courses on YouTube, Nathan has a podcast.

00:01:34 --> 00:01:38
And everyone should absolutely follow all of those.

00:01:37 --> 00:01:42
This is the Lex Fridman podcast. To support it, please

00:01:41 --> 00:01:46
check out our sponsors in the description, where you can also find

00:01:45 --> 00:01:50
links to contact me, ask questions, get feedback, and so

00:01:49 --> 00:01:57
n. And now, dear friends, here's Sebastian Raschka and Nathan Lambert.

00:01:57 --> 00:02:02
So I think one useful lens to look at all this through is

00:02:01 --> 00:02:06
the so-called DeepSeek moment. This happened about

00:02:05 --> 00:02:10
a year ago in January 2025, when the open-weight Chinese

00:02:09 --> 00:02:13
company DeepSeek released DeepSeek R1, that I

00:02:13 --> 00:02:17
think it's fair to say surprised everyone with near-state-of-the-art

00:02:16 --> 00:02:25
performance, with allegedly much less compute for much cheaper. And from then

00:02:24 --> 00:02:29
to today, the AI competition has gotten insane,

00:02:28 --> 00:02:33
both on the research and product level. It's just been accelerating.

00:02:32 --> 00:02:37
discuss all of this today, and maybe let's start with some spicy

00:02:36 --> 00:02:38
questions if we can.

00:02:38 --> 00:02:43
Who's winning at the international level? Would you say it's the set

00:02:42 --> 00:02:47
of companies in China or the set of companies in the United States?

00:02:46 --> 00:02:51
And Sebastian, Nathan, it's good to see you guys.

00:02:50 --> 00:02:54
So Sebastian, who do you think is winning?

00:02:53 --> 00:02:58
- Winning is a very broad term.

00:02:57 --> 00:03:02
I would say you mentioned the DeepSeek moment, and I think DeepSeek is winning

00:03:01 --> 00:03:06
the hearts of the people who work on open-weight models because they share

00:03:05 --> 00:03:10
these as open models. Winning, I think, has multiple

00:03:09 --> 00:03:13
timescales to it. We have today, we have next year, we have in 10

00:03:12 --> 00:03:17
years. One thing I know for sure is that I don't

00:03:16 --> 00:03:21
hink nowadays, in 2026, that there will be any

00:03:20 --> 00:03:25
company that has access to technology that no other

00:03:24 --> 00:03:29
company has access to. That is mainly because researchers

00:03:28 --> 00:03:33
are frequently changing jobs and labs.

00:03:32 --> 00:03:37
They rotate. I don't think there will be a clear winner in terms of

00:03:36 --> 00:03:40
technology access. However, I do think there will be,

00:03:39 --> 00:03:44
The differentiating factor will be budget and hardware constraints.

00:03:43 --> 00:03:47
I don't think the ideas will be proprietary,

00:03:46 --> 00:03:54
but rather the resources needed to implement them. I don't see

00:03:53 --> 00:03:59
currently a winner-take-all scenario. I can't see that. At the moment.

00:03:59 --> 00:04:01
- Nathan, what do you think?

00:04:00 --> 00:04:05
- You see the labs put different energy into what they're trying to do, and

00:04:04 --> 00:04:09
I think to demarcate the point in time when we're recording this, the hype

00:04:08 --> 00:04:12
over Anthropic's Claude Opus 4.5 model has been

00:04:12 --> 00:04:16
absolutely insane, which is just... I mean, I've used it and built stuff

00:04:16 --> 00:04:21
in the last few weeks, and it's... it's almost gotten to the point where it feels like a bit of

00:04:20 --> 00:04:22
a meme in terms of the hype. And it's

00:04:22 --> 00:04:27
kind of funny because this is very organic, and then if we go back a few months

00:04:26 --> 00:04:31
ago, we can see the release date and the notes, as Gemini 3 from Google got

00:04:30 --> 00:04:33
released, and it seemed like the

00:04:33 --> 00:04:37
marketing and just, like, wow factor of that release was super

00:04:36 --> 00:04:41
high. But then at the end of November, Claude Opus 4.5 was released and

00:04:40 --> 00:04:45
the hype has been growing, but Gemini 3 was before this. And it kind of feels

00:04:44 --> 00:04:49
like people don't really talk about it as much, even though when it came out, everybody was like, this

00:04:48 --> 00:04:53
Gemini's moment to retake Google's

00:04:52 --> 00:04:57
tructural advantages in AI. And Gemini 3 is a fantastic model, and I still use it.

00:04:56 --> 00:05:01
It's just kind of differentiation is lower. And I

00:05:00 --> 00:05:05
agree with Sebastian; what you're saying with all these, the idea space is

00:05:04 --> 00:05:09
very fluid, but culturally Anthropic is known for betting very

00:05:08 --> 00:05:13
hard on code, which is the Claude Code thing, is working out for them right now. So I

00:05:12 --> 00:05:17
think that even if the ideas flow pretty freely, so much of this is

00:05:16 --> 00:05:21
bottlenecked by human effort and the culture of organizations, where Anthropic

00:05:20 --> 00:05:25
seems to at least be presenting as the least chaotic. It's a

00:05:24 --> 00:05:29
bit of an advantage, if they can keep doing that for a while. But on the other

00:05:28 --> 00:05:33
side of things, there's a lot of ominous technology from China where

00:05:32 --> 00:05:36
there's way more labs than DeepSeek. So DeepSeek kicked off

00:05:35 --> 00:05:40
a movement within China, I say kind of similar to how

00:05:39 --> 00:05:44
ChatGPT kicked off a movement in the US where everything had a chatbot. There's now

00:05:44 --> 00:05:49
tons of tech companies in China that are releasing very strong frontier open-weight

00:05:48 --> 00:05:53
models, to the point where I would say that DeepSeek is kind of losing its crown as the

00:05:52 --> 00:05:57
preeminent open model maker in China, and the likes of

00:05:56 --> 00:06:01
Z.ai with their GLM models, Minimax's models,

00:06:00 --> 00:06:05
Kimi Moonshot, especially in the last few months, has shown more

00:06:04 --> 00:06:09
brightly. The new DeepSeek models are still very strong, but that's kind of

00:06:08 --> 00:06:12
a... it could look back as a big narrative point where in 2025

00:06:11 --> 00:06:16
DeepSeek came and it provided this platform for way more Chinese

00:06:15 --> 00:06:20
companies that are releasing these fantastic models to kind of have this new

00:06:19 --> 00:06:24
type of operation. So these models from these Chinese companies are open-weights, and

00:06:24 --> 00:06:28
epending on this trajectory of business models that these American companies are

00:06:27 --> 00:06:32
doing, they could be at risk. But currently, a lot of people are paying

00:06:31 --> 00:06:36
for AI software in the US, and historically in China and other

00:06:35 --> 00:06:38
parts of the world, people don't pay a lot for software.

00:06:37 --> 00:06:42
- So some of these models like DeepSeek have the love of the people because

00:06:41 --> 00:06:46
they are open-weight. How long do you think the Chinese companies keep

00:06:45 --> 00:06:48
releasing open-weight models?

00:06:47 --> 00:06:52
- I would say for a few years. I think that, like in the US, there's not a

00:06:51 --> 00:06:56
clear business model for it. I have been writing about open models for a while,

00:06:55 --> 00:07:00
and these Chinese companies have realized it. So I get inbound from some of them.

00:06:59 --> 00:07:03
And they're smart and realize the same constraints: a lot of top US tech

00:07:02 --> 00:07:07
companies and other IT companies won't pay for an API subscription to

00:07:07 --> 00:07:11
Chinese companies for security concerns. This has been a long-standing

00:07:10 --> 00:07:15
habit in tech, and the people at these companies then see open

00:07:14 --> 00:07:19
weight models as an ability to influence and take part of a huge growing

00:07:18 --> 00:07:23
AI expenditure market in the US. And they're very realistic about this,

00:07:23 --> 00:07:27
and it's working for them. I think that the government will see that that is

00:07:27 --> 00:07:32
building a lot of influence internationally in terms of uptake of the technology,

00:07:31 --> 00:07:36
so there's going to be a lot of incentives to keep it going. But building

00:07:35 --> 00:07:39
these models and doing the research is very expensive, so at some point, I expect

00:07:39 --> 00:07:44
consolidation. But I don't expect that to be a story of 2026, where there will be

00:07:44 --> 00:07:49
more open model builders throughout 2026 than there were in 2025. And a

00:07:48 --> 00:07:51
lot of the notable ones will be in China.

00:07:50 --> 00:07:52
- You were going to say something?

00:07:51 --> 00:07:58
- Yes. You mentioned DeepSeek losing its crown. I do think to some extent, yes, but

00:07:58 --> 00:08:03
we also have to consider though, they are still, I would say, slightly ahead. And

00:08:02 --> 00:08:07
the other ones—it's not that DeepSeek got worse, it's just that the other ones

00:08:06 --> 00:08:11
are using the ideas from DeepSeek. For example, you mentioned Kimi—same

00:08:10 --> 00:08:14
architecture, they're training it. And then again, we have this leapfrogging

00:08:13 --> 00:08:18
where they might be at some point in time a bit better because they have the more recent

00:08:17 --> 00:08:22
model. And I think this comes back to the fact that there won't be

00:08:21 --> 00:08:26
a clear winner. It will just be like that: one person releases

00:08:25 --> 00:08:30
omething, the other one comes in, and the most recent model is probably always the

00:08:29 --> 00:08:31
best model.

00:08:30 --> 00:08:34
- Yeah. We'll also see the Chinese companies have different incentives. Like,

00:08:33 --> 00:08:38
DeepSeek is very secretive, whereas some of these startups are

00:08:37 --> 00:08:42
like the MiniMaxs and Z.ais of the world. Those two literally have filed

00:08:41 --> 00:08:46
IPO paperwork, and they're trying to get Western

00:08:45 --> 00:08:50
mindshare and do a lot of outreach there. So I don't know if these incentives will change the

00:08:49 --> 00:08:54
model development, because DeepSeek famously is built by a hedge fund,

00:08:53 --> 00:08:58
Highflyer Capital, and we don't know exactly what they use the

00:08:57 --> 00:08:59
models for or if they care about this.

00:08:59 --> 00:09:04
- They're secretive in terms of communication; they're not secretive in terms of the technical reports that

00:09:03 --> 00:09:07
describe how their models work. They're still open on that front. And we should also

00:09:06 --> 00:09:13
say, on the Claude Opus 4.5 hype, there's the layer of something

00:09:13 --> 00:09:18
being the darling of the X echo chamber, on the

00:09:18 --> 00:09:23
Twitter echo chamber, and the actual amount of people that are using the

00:09:22 --> 00:09:26
model. I think it's probably fair to say that ChatGPT and

00:09:25 --> 00:09:30
Gemini are focused on the broad user base that just

00:09:29 --> 00:09:34
want to solve problems in their daily lives, and that user base

00:09:33 --> 00:09:38
is gigantic. So the hype about the coding may not be

00:09:37 --> 00:09:39
representative of the actual use.

00:09:38 --> 00:09:43
- I would say also a lot of the usage patterns are,

00:09:43 --> 00:09:47
like you said, name recognition, brand and stuff, but also

00:09:47 --> 00:09:51
muscle memory almost, where, you know, ChatGPT has been around

00:09:50 --> 00:09:55
for a long time. People just got used to using it, and it's almost like a flywheel:

00:09:54 --> 00:09:59
they recommend it to other users and that stuff. One interesting point is also

00:09:58 --> 00:10:03
the customization of LLMs. For example, ChatGPT has a

00:10:02 --> 00:10:07
memory feature, right? And so you may have a subscription and you

00:10:06 --> 00:10:11
se it for personal stuff, but I don't know if you want to use that same thing at work.

00:10:10 --> 00:10:15
Because it's a boundary between private and work. If you're working at a company, they might not

00:10:14 --> 00:10:19
allow that or you may not want that. And I think that's also an interesting point

00:10:18 --> 00:10:23
where you might have multiple subscriptions. One is just clean code.

00:10:22 --> 00:10:27
It has nothing of your personal images or hobby

00:10:26 --> 00:10:31
projects in there. It's just like the work thing. And then the other one is your personal thing.

00:10:30 --> 00:10:34
So I think that's also something where there are two different use cases, and it doesn't mean

00:10:34 --> 00:10:39
you only have to have one. I think the future is also multiple ones.

00:10:38 --> 00:10:44
- What model do you think won 2025, and what model do you think is going to win '26?

00:10:43 --> 00:10:48
- I think in the context of consumer chatbots, it's a question of: are you willing to

00:10:47 --> 00:10:51
bet on Gemini over ChatGPT?

00:10:50 --> 00:10:55
Which I would say, in my gut, feels like a bit of a risky bet

00:10:54 --> 00:10:59
because OpenAI has been the incumbent, and there are so many benefits to that in tech.

00:10:58 --> 00:11:01
I think the

00:11:01 --> 00:11:06
momentum, if you look at 2025, was on Gemini's side, but they were starting from

00:11:05 --> 00:11:13
such a low point. And RIP Bard and these earlier attempts at getting started.

00:11:13 --> 00:11:17
Huge credit to them for powering through the organizational chaos to make that happen.

00:11:17 --> 00:11:23
But also it's hard to bet against OpenAI because they always come off as

00:11:22 --> 00:11:27
o chaotic, but they're very good at landing things. And I think,

00:11:26 --> 00:11:31
personally, I have very mixed reviews of GPT-5, but it must have

00:11:30 --> 00:11:35
saved them so much money with the high-line feature being a router where

00:11:34 --> 00:11:39
most users are no longer charging their GPU costs as much.

00:11:38 --> 00:11:43
So I think it's very hard to dissociate

00:11:42 --> 00:11:46
the things that I like out of models versus the things that are going to

00:11:45 --> 00:11:51
actually be a general public differentiator.

00:11:50 --> 00:11:53
- What do you think about 2026? Who's going to win?

00:11:52 --> 00:11:57
- I'll say something, even though it's risky. I think Gemini will continue to make progress on ChatGPT.

00:11:56 --> 00:12:00
I think Google's scale, when both of these are

00:11:59 --> 00:12:04
operating at such extreme scales—and Google has the

00:12:03 --> 00:12:08
ability to separate research and product a bit better, whereas you hear so much

00:12:07 --> 00:12:12
about OpenAI being chaotic operationally and chasing the high-impact thing,

00:12:11 --> 00:12:16
which is a very startup culture. And then on the software and enterprise side,

00:12:15 --> 00:12:20
I think Anthropic will have continued success, as they've again and again been set up for that.

00:12:19 --> 00:12:24
And obviously Google Cloud has a lot of offerings,

00:12:23 --> 00:12:28
but I think this kind of Gemini name brand is important for them to build.

00:12:27 --> 00:12:32
Google Cloud will continue to do well, but

00:12:31 --> 00:12:35
hat's a more complex thing to explain in the

00:12:34 --> 00:12:39
cosystem, because that's competing with the likes of Azure and AWS rather than

00:12:38 --> 00:12:41
on the model provider side.

00:12:40 --> 00:12:46
- So in infrastructure, you think TPU is giving an advantage?

00:12:45 --> 00:12:50
- Largely because the margin on NVIDIA chips is insane, and

00:12:49 --> 00:12:54
Google can develop everything from top to bottom to fit their stack and not have

00:12:53 --> 00:12:58
to pay this margin. And they've had a head start in building data

00:12:57 --> 00:13:02
centers. So all of these things that have both high lead times and very hard margins on

00:13:01 --> 00:13:05
high costs, Google has a just kind of historical advantage there.

00:13:05 --> 00:13:10
And if there's going to be a new paradigm, it's most likely to come from OpenAI where

00:13:09 --> 00:13:13
their research division again and again has shown

00:13:13 --> 00:13:18
this ability to land a new research idea or a product. Like Deep

00:13:17 --> 00:13:22
Research, Sora, o1 thinking models—all these

00:13:21 --> 00:13:25
definitional things have come from OpenAI, and that's got to be one of their

00:13:25 --> 00:13:30
top traits as an organization. So it's kind of hard to bet against

00:13:29 --> 00:13:33
hat, but I think a lot of this year will be about scale and

00:13:33 --> 00:13:37
optimizing what could be described as low-hanging fruit in models.

00:13:37 --> 00:13:41
- And clearly there's a trade-off between intelligence and

00:13:40 --> 00:13:45
speed. This is what ChatGPT-5 was trying

00:13:44 --> 00:13:49
to solve behind the scenes. It's like, do people actually

00:13:48 --> 00:13:53
want intelligence, the broad public, or do they want speed?

00:13:52 --> 00:13:56
- I think it's a nice variety, or the option to have a toggle there.

00:13:56 --> 00:14:01
I mean, for my personal usage, most of the time when I look

00:14:00 --> 00:14:04
something up, I use ChatGPT to ask a quick question, get the information I wanted

00:14:03 --> 00:14:08
fast. For most daily tasks, I use the quick

00:14:07 --> 00:14:12
model. Nowadays, I think the auto mode is pretty good where you don't have to specifically

00:14:11 --> 00:14:15
say thinking or non-thinking. Then again, I also

00:14:14 --> 00:14:19
metimes want the pro mode. Very often what I do is, when I have something

00:14:18 --> 00:14:22
written, I put it into ChatGPT and say, "Hey, do a

00:14:22 --> 00:14:26
very thorough check. Are all my references correct? Are all my thoughts

00:14:25 --> 00:14:30
correct? Did I make any formatting mistakes and are the figure

00:14:29 --> 00:14:34
numbers wrong?" Or something like that. And I don't need that right

00:14:33 --> 00:14:38
away. I finish my stuff, maybe have dinner, let it run, come back

00:14:37 --> 00:14:42
and go through this. I think this is where it's important to

00:14:41 --> 00:14:46
have this option. I would go crazy if for each query I would have to wait 30 minutes or

00:14:45 --> 00:14:47
10 minutes even.

00:14:46 --> 00:14:48
- That's me.

00:14:48 --> 00:14:53
I'm sitting over here losing my mind that you use the router and the non-thinking model.

00:14:52 --> 00:14:56
I'm like, "How do you live with that?"

00:14:55 --> 00:15:01
That's like my reaction. I've been heavily on ChatGPT for a while.

00:15:01 --> 00:15:06
I never touched ChatGPT-5 non-thinking. I find its tone and then

00:15:05 --> 00:15:10
its propensity for errors—it has a higher likelihood of errors. Some of this

00:15:09 --> 00:15:14
from back when OpenAI released o3, which was the first model to

00:15:13 --> 00:15:18
do this deep search and find many sources and integrate them for you.

00:15:17 --> 00:15:22
I became habituated with that. So I will only use GPT-5.2 Thinking

00:15:21 --> 00:15:26
or Pro when I'm finding any sort of information query for work,

00:15:25 --> 00:15:29
whether that's a paper or some code reference that I

00:15:28 --> 00:15:33
found. And I will regularly have like five Pro

00:15:32 --> 00:15:37
queries going simultaneously, each looking for one specific paper or

00:15:36 --> 00:15:38
feedback on an equation or something.

00:15:38 --> 00:15:42
- I have a fun example where I needed the answer as fast as possible

00:15:41 --> 00:15:46
for this podcast before I was going on the trip.

00:15:46 --> 00:15:50
like a local GPU running at home and I wanted to run a long RL

00:15:49 --> 00:15:54
experiment. And usually I also unplug things because you never know if

00:15:53 --> 00:15:58
you're not at home, you don't want things plugged in. And I accidentally unplugged the

00:15:57 --> 00:16:02
GPU. My wife was already in the car and it's like, "Oh dang."

00:16:01 --> 00:16:06
Then basically I wanted as fast as possible a Bash

00:16:05 --> 00:16:10
script that runs my different experiments and the evaluation. And

00:16:09 --> 00:16:14
it's something I know, I learned how to use the Bash

00:16:13 --> 00:16:18
interface or Bash terminal, but in that moment I just needed

00:16:17 --> 00:16:19
like 10 seconds, give me the command.

00:16:18 --> 00:16:22
- This is a hilarious situation but yeah, so what did you use?

00:16:21 --> 00:16:26
- So I did the non-thinking fastest model. It gave me the Bash

00:16:25 --> 00:16:30
command to chain different scripts to each other and

00:16:29 --> 00:16:34
then the thing is like you have the tee thing where you want to route this

00:16:33 --> 00:16:38
to a log file. Top of my head I was just like in a hurry, I could have thought

00:16:37 --> 00:16:38
about it myself.

00:16:37 --> 00:16:41
- By the way I don't know if there's a representative case, wife waiting in the car- ...

00:16:40 --> 00:16:45
you have to run, you know, unplug the GPU. You have to generate a Bash script. This sounds like

00:16:44 --> 00:16:47
a movie, like- Mission Impossible.

00:16:46 --> 00:16:51
- I use Gemini for that. So I use thinking for all the information stuff and then Gemini for

00:16:50 --> 00:16:55
fast things or stuff that I could sometimes Google, which is like it's good at

00:16:54 --> 00:16:59
explaining things and I trust that it has this kind of background of knowledge and

00:16:58 --> 00:17:02
it's simple. And the Gemini app has gotten a lot better and-

00:17:01 --> 00:17:06
It's good for those sorts of things. And then for code and any sort of philosophical

00:17:05 --> 00:17:09
discussion, I use Claude Opus 4.5. Also always with

00:17:08 --> 00:17:13
extended thinking. Extended thinking and inference time scaling is just a way to make the

00:17:12 --> 00:17:17
models marginally smarter. And I will always

00:17:16 --> 00:17:21
err on that side when the progress is very high because you don't know when that'll unlock a new

00:17:20 --> 00:17:25
use case. And then sometimes use Grok for real-time

00:17:24 --> 00:17:29
information or finding something on AI Twitter that I knew I saw and I need

00:17:28 --> 00:17:32
to dig up and I just fixated on. Although when

00:17:31 --> 00:17:36
Grok 4 came out, the Grok 4 SuperGrok Heavy, which

00:17:35 --> 00:17:40
was like their pro variant was actually very good and I was pretty impressed with it, and then it

00:17:39 --> 00:17:44
just kind of like muscle memory lost track of it with having the ChatGPT

00:17:43 --> 00:17:46
app open. So I use many different things.

00:17:45 --> 00:17:50
- Yeah. I actually do use Grok 4 Heavy

00:17:49 --> 00:17:54
for debugging. For like hardcore debugging that the other ones

00:17:53 --> 00:17:58
can't solve, I find that it's the best at. And... it's interesting

00:17:57 --> 00:18:02
'cause you say ChatGPT is the best interface. For

00:18:01 --> 00:18:05
me, for that same reason, but this could be just momentum-

00:18:04 --> 00:18:10
Gemini is the better interface for me. I think because I fell in love with

00:18:09 --> 00:18:14
eir best needle in the haystack. If I ever put

00:18:13 --> 00:18:18
something that has a lot of context but I'm looking for very specific kinds of information to

00:18:17 --> 00:18:22
make sure it tracks all of it, I find at least that

00:18:21 --> 00:18:25
Gemini for me has been the best. So it's

00:18:25 --> 00:18:29
funny with some of these models, if they win your heart over-

00:18:28 --> 00:18:33
for one particular feature on one particular day, for

00:18:32 --> 00:18:36
that particular query, that prompt, you're like, "This

00:18:35 --> 00:18:40
model's better." And so you'll just stick with it for a bit until

00:18:40 --> 00:18:44
it does something really dumb. There's like a threshold effect. Some smart

00:18:43 --> 00:18:48
hing and then you fall in love with it and then it does some dumb thing and you're like, "You know what?

00:18:47 --> 00:18:51
I'm gonna switch and try Claude or ChatGPT." And all that kind of stuff.

00:18:51 --> 00:18:56
- This is exactly it: you use it until it breaks, until you have a problem, and

00:18:55 --> 00:18:59
then you change the LLM. And I think it's

00:18:59 --> 00:19:03
the same as how we use anything, like our favorite text editor,

00:19:02 --> 00:19:07
operating systems, or the browser. I mean, there are many options:

00:19:06 --> 00:19:11
Safari, Firefox, Chrome. They're relatively similar, but then

00:19:10 --> 00:19:15
there are edge cases, extensions you want, and then you switch.

00:19:14 --> 00:19:20
But I don't think anyone types the same thing into different

00:19:19 --> 00:19:24
browsers and compares them. You only do that when something breaks.

00:19:23 --> 00:19:29
So that's a good point. You use it until it breaks, then you explore other options.

00:19:28 --> 00:19:33
- On the long context thing, I was also a Gemini user, but the GPT-5.2

00:19:32 --> 00:19:37
release blog had crazy long context scores. People were like, "Did they

00:19:36 --> 00:19:41
just figure out some algorithmic change?" It went from 30% to

00:19:40 --> 00:19:45
70% in this minor model update. It's very hard to

00:19:44 --> 00:19:48
keep track of all of these things, but now I look more

00:19:47 --> 00:19:52
favorably at GPT-5.2's long context. So it's just like,

00:19:51 --> 00:19:58
"How do I actually get to testing this?" It's a never-ending battle.

00:19:57 --> 00:20:01
- Well, it's interesting that none of us talked about the Chinese models

00:20:01 --> 00:20:06
from a usage perspective. What does that say? Does it mean the

00:20:05 --> 00:20:12
Chinese models are not as good, or are we just very biased and US-focused?

00:20:11 --> 00:20:16
- I think currently there's a discrepancy between the model and the platform.

00:20:15 --> 00:20:20
The open models are more known for the open weights, not the platform yet.

00:20:19 --> 00:20:22
known for the open weights, not their platform yet.

00:20:21 --> 00:20:26
- Many companies will sell you open-model inference at a very low cost.

00:20:25 --> 00:20:30
With OpenRouter, it's easy to look at multi-model things.

00:20:29 --> 00:20:34
You can run DeepSeek on Perplexity. Sitting here, we're like,

00:20:33 --> 00:20:40
"We use OpenAI GPT-5 Pro consistently." We're all willing to pay for the marginal

00:20:39 --> 00:20:43
intelligence gain. These models from the US are better

00:20:43 --> 00:20:48
in terms of the outputs. I think the question is,

00:20:47 --> 00:20:52
will they stay better for this year and for years to come?

00:20:51 --> 00:20:55
As long as they're better, I'm gonna pay for them.

00:20:55 --> 00:21:00
There's also analysis showing that the way

00:20:59 --> 00:21:03
the Chinese models are served—you could argue this is due to export controls—

00:21:02 --> 00:21:07
is that they use fewer GPUs per replica, which makes them slower

00:21:06 --> 00:21:11
and have different errors. If speed and intelligence are in your favor as a user,

00:21:10 --> 00:21:15
in the US, a lot of users will go for this. And I think that will spur these

00:21:14 --> 00:21:19
Chinese companies to want to compete in other ways,

00:21:18 --> 00:21:23
whether it's free or substantially lower costs, or it'll breed

00:21:22 --> 00:21:27
creativity in terms of offerings, which is good for the ecosystem.

00:21:26 --> 00:21:31
But the simple thing is: the US models are currently better, and we use them.

00:21:30 --> 00:21:35
I tried these other open models, and I'm like, "Fun, but I don't go back."

00:21:34 --> 00:21:39
models, and I'm like, "Fun, but not gonna... I don't go back to it."

00:21:38 --> 00:21:43
- We didn't really mention programming. That's another use case

00:21:42 --> 00:21:47
that a lot of people deeply care about. I use basically

00:21:46 --> 00:21:51
half-and-half Cursor and Claude Code, because they're... I

00:21:50 --> 00:21:54
fundamentally different experiences and both are useful.

00:21:54 --> 00:21:57
What do you guys... You program quite a bit,

00:21:56 --> 00:22:00
so what do you use? What's the current vibe?

00:21:59 --> 00:22:04
- So, I use the Codeium plugin for VS Code. You know, it's very

00:22:03 --> 00:22:07
convenient. It's just like a plugin, and then it's a chat interface that has access to your

00:22:06 --> 00:22:11
epository. I know that Claude Code is, I think, a bit different. It

00:22:10 --> 00:22:15
is a bit more agentic. It touches more things. It does the whole project for you. I'm not quite

00:22:14 --> 00:22:19
there yet where I'm comfortable with that because maybe I'm a

00:22:18 --> 00:22:23
control freak, but I still would like to see a bit what's going on. And

00:22:22 --> 00:22:27
Codeium is kind of, right now, for me, the sweet spot where it is

00:22:26 --> 00:22:30
helping me, but it is not taking completely over.

00:22:29 --> 00:22:34
- I should mention, one of the reasons I do use Claude Code is to build the

00:22:33 --> 00:22:37
skill of programming with English. I mean, the experience is fundamentally

00:22:37 --> 00:22:41
different. You're... As opposed to micromanaging the

00:22:40 --> 00:22:45
details of the process of the generation of the code, and

00:22:44 --> 00:22:49
looking at the diff, which you can in Cursor if that's the IDE you

00:22:48 --> 00:22:53
se, and in changing, altering.

00:22:52 --> 00:22:56
Looking and reading the code and understanding the code deeply as you

00:22:55 --> 00:23:04
progress, versus just thinking in this design space and just guiding it

00:23:03 --> 00:23:07
at this macro level, which I think

00:23:07 --> 00:23:11
is another way of thinking about the programming process.

00:23:10 --> 00:23:15
Also, we should say that Claude Code just seems to

00:23:14 --> 00:23:19
be somehow a better utilization of Claude Opus 4.5.

00:23:18 --> 00:23:23
- It's a good side-by-side for people to do. You can have Claude Code open, you can have Cursor

00:23:22 --> 00:23:27
open, you can have VS Code open, and you can select the same models on all of them—

00:23:26 --> 00:23:31
...and ask questions, and it's very interesting. Claude Code is way better

00:23:30 --> 00:23:33
in that domain. It's remarkable.

00:23:32 --> 00:23:37
- All right, we should say that both of you are legit on multiple fronts:

00:23:36 --> 00:23:42
researchers, programmers, educators, Tweeters.

00:23:43 --> 00:23:47
And on the book front, too. So Nathan, at some

00:23:46 --> 00:23:51
point soon, hopefully has an RLHF book coming out.

00:23:50 --> 00:23:55
- It's available for preorder, and there's a full digital preprint. I'm

00:23:54 --> 00:23:59
just making it pretty and better organized for the physical thing, which is a lot of why I do

00:23:58 --> 00:24:03
it, because it's fun to create things that you think are excellent in the

00:24:02 --> 00:24:06
physical form when so much of our life is digital.

00:24:05 --> 00:24:09
- I should say, going to Perplexity here, Sebastian Raschka is a machine learning

00:24:08 --> 00:24:13
researcher and author known for several influential books. A couple of them that I

00:24:12 --> 00:24:17
wanted to mention—which is a book I highly recommend—Build a Large

00:24:16 --> 00:24:21
Language Model from Scratch, and the new one, Build a

00:24:20 --> 00:24:24
Reasoning Model from Scratch. So, I'm really excited about

00:24:23 --> 00:24:28
hat. Building stuff from scratch is one of the most powerful ways of learning.

00:24:27 --> 00:24:32
- Honestly, building an LLM from scratch is a lot of fun. It's also a lot to learn.

00:24:31 --> 00:24:35
And like you said, it's probably the best way to learn how something really works,

00:24:35 --> 00:24:40
'cause you can look at figures, but figures can have mistakes. You can look

00:24:39 --> 00:24:44
at concepts and explanations, but you might misunderstand them.

00:24:43 --> 00:24:48
But if there is code, and the code

00:24:47 --> 00:24:51
works, you know it's correct. I mean, there's no misunderstanding. It's precise.

00:24:50 --> 00:24:55
Otherwise, it wouldn't work. And I think that's the beauty behind coding.

00:24:54 --> 00:24:59
It doesn't lie. It's math, basically. So,

00:24:58 --> 00:25:03
even though with math, I think you can have mistakes in a book you would never notice.

00:25:02 --> 00:25:07
Because you are not running the math when you are reading the book, you can't verify this.

00:25:06 --> 00:25:09
And with code, what's nice is you can verify it.

00:25:09 --> 00:25:13
- Yeah, I agree with you about the Build an LLM from Scratch book. It's nice to

00:25:13 --> 00:25:17
tune out everything else, the internet and so on, and just focus on the book.

00:25:16 --> 00:25:24
But, you know, I read several history books. It's just less

00:25:23 --> 00:25:28
lonely somehow. It's really more fun. Like for example, on the

00:25:27 --> 00:25:32
programming front, I think it's genuinely more fun to program with an LLM.

00:25:31 --> 00:25:37
And I think it's genuinely more fun to read with an LLM.

00:25:36 --> 00:25:41
But you're right. That distraction should be minimized.

00:25:40 --> 00:25:49
So you use the LLM to basically enrich the experience, maybe add more context.

00:25:48 --> 00:25:55
I just find the rate of aha moments for me is really high with LLMs.

00:25:54 --> 00:25:59
- 100%. I also want to correct myself: I'm not suggesting not to use

00:25:58 --> 00:26:03
LLMs. I suggest doing it in multiple passes. Like, one pass

00:26:02 --> 00:26:07
just offline, focus mode, and then after that... I mean, I also

00:26:06 --> 00:26:10
take notes, but I, I try to resist the urge to

00:26:10 --> 00:26:15
immediately look things up. I do a second pass. It's just more

00:26:14 --> 00:26:19
structured this way. Sometimes things are answered in the

00:26:18 --> 00:26:22
chapter, but sometimes also it just helps to let it sink in and think

00:26:21 --> 00:26:26
about it. Other people have different preferences. I highly recommend using LLMs when

00:26:25 --> 00:26:30
reading books. For me, it's not the first thing to do; it's the second pass.

00:26:29 --> 00:26:36
- My recommendation is the opposite. I like to use the LLM at the beginning

00:26:35 --> 00:26:40
to lay out the full context of what is this world that I'm

00:26:39 --> 00:26:44
now stepping into? But I try to avoid clicking out of

00:26:43 --> 00:26:48
the LLM into the world of Twitter and

00:26:47 --> 00:26:51
blogs, because then you're down this rabbit hole. You're reading

00:26:50 --> 00:26:55
somebody's opinion. There's a flame war about a particular topic and all of a

00:26:54 --> 00:26:59
sudden you're in the realm of the internet and Reddit

00:26:58 --> 00:27:03
and so on. But if you're purely letting the

00:27:02 --> 00:27:07
LLM give you the context of why this matters, what are the big picture

00:27:06 --> 00:27:13
ideas... sometimes books are good at doing that, but not always.

00:27:12 --> 00:27:16
- This is why I like the ChatGPT app, because it gives the AI a home on your

00:27:15 --> 00:27:20
computer where you can focus on it, rather than just being another tab in

00:27:19 --> 00:27:24
my mess of internet options. And I think Claude Code

00:27:23 --> 00:27:28
does a good job of making that a joy, where it seems

00:27:27 --> 00:27:32
very engaging as a product design to be an interface

00:27:31 --> 00:27:36
that your AI will then go out into the world. It's something that is

00:27:35 --> 00:27:40
intangible between it and Codex; it just feels warm

00:27:39 --> 00:27:44
and engaging, where Codex can often be as good from OpenAI, but it just,

00:27:43 --> 00:27:48
feel a little bit rough around the edges. Whereas Claude Code

00:27:47 --> 00:27:52
makes it fun to build things from scratch, where you just

00:27:51 --> 00:27:55
rust that it'll make something. Obviously this is good for

00:27:55 --> 00:27:59
websites and kind of refreshing tooling and stuff like

00:27:58 --> 00:28:03
this, which I use it for, or data analysis. For my

00:28:02 --> 00:28:07
On my blog, we scrape Hugging Face so we keep download numbers for every dataset and model.

00:28:06 --> 00:28:11
over time, so we have them. And Claude was just like, "Yeah, I've made use of that

00:28:10 --> 00:28:14
data, no problem." And I was like, "That would've taken me days."

00:28:13 --> 00:28:18
And then I have enough situational awareness to be like, "Okay, these trends obviously make sense."

00:28:17 --> 00:28:22
You can check things. But that's just a wonderful interface where you can have an

00:28:21 --> 00:28:26
intermediary and not have to do the kind of awful low-level work

00:28:25 --> 00:28:30
that you would have to do to maintain different web projects.

00:28:29 --> 00:28:33
- All right. So we just talked about a bunch of the

00:28:32 --> 00:28:37
closed-weight models. Let's talk about the

00:28:36 --> 00:28:40
open ones. Tell me about the landscape of open LLM

00:28:39 --> 00:28:44
models. Which are interesting? Which stand out to you and why? We

00:28:43 --> 00:28:45
already mentioned DeepSeek R1.

00:28:44 --> 00:28:48
- Do you wanna see how many we can name off the top of our head?

00:28:47 --> 00:28:49
- Yeah, without looking at notes.

00:28:48 --> 00:28:57
- DeepSeek, Kimi, MiniMax, Z.ai, Moonshot. We're just going Chinese.

00:28:57 --> 00:29:02
- Let's throw in Mistral AI, Gemma...

00:29:01 --> 00:29:06
gpt-oss, the open weight model by OpenAI. Actually,

00:29:05 --> 00:29:10
NVIDIA had a really cool one, Nemotron 3. There,

00:29:09 --> 00:29:13
there's a lot of stuff especially at the end of the year. Qwen might be the one—

00:29:12 --> 00:29:16
- Oh, yeah. Qwen was the obvious name I was gonna say.

00:29:15 --> 00:29:19
You can get at least 10 Chinese and at least 10 Western. I think

00:29:18 --> 00:29:23
that OpenAI released their first open model—

00:29:22 --> 00:29:27
...since GPT-2. When I was writing about

00:29:26 --> 00:29:30
OpenAI's open model release, they were like, "Don't forget about GPT-2," which I thought was

00:29:29 --> 00:29:34
really funny 'cause it's just such a different time. But gpt-oss-120b is

00:29:33 --> 00:29:38
actually a very strong model and does some things that other models

00:29:38 --> 00:29:45
don't do very well. Selfishly, I'll promote a bunch of Western companies in the US

00:29:44 --> 00:29:49
and Europe that have these fully open models. I work at the Allen Institute for AI,

00:29:48 --> 00:29:53
where we've been building OLMo, which releases data and code. And now

00:29:52 --> 00:29:57
e have actual competition for people that are trying to release everything

00:29:56 --> 00:30:00
so that others can train these models. There's the Institute for Foundation

00:29:59 --> 00:30:04
Models/LM360, which has had their K2

00:30:03 --> 00:30:08
models of various types. Apertus is a Swiss research

00:30:07 --> 00:30:12
consortium. Hugging Face has SmolLM, which

00:30:11 --> 00:30:15
is very popular. And NVIDIA's Nemotron 3 has started releasing data as

00:30:14 --> 00:30:19
well. And then Stanford's Martini Community Project, which is kind

00:30:18 --> 00:30:23
of making it so there's a pipeline for people to open a GitHub issue and implement a new

00:30:22 --> 00:30:27
idea and then have it run in a stable language modeling stack.

00:30:26 --> 00:30:32
This space, that list was way smaller in 2024—

00:30:31 --> 00:30:35
...so I think it was just AI2. So it's a great thing for more people to get

00:30:34 --> 00:30:39
involved and to understand language models, which doesn't really have a

00:30:38 --> 00:30:43
Chinese analog. While I'm talking, I'll

00:30:42 --> 00:30:47
say that the Chinese open language models tend to be

00:30:46 --> 00:30:51
much bigger, and that gives them higher peak performance as MoEs, where a lot of

00:30:50 --> 00:30:55
these things that we like a lot, whether it was Gemma and

00:30:54 --> 00:30:59
Nemotron, have tended to be smaller models from the US, which is starting

00:30:58 --> 00:31:02
to change from the US and Europe. Mistral Large 3 came out, which was a

00:31:01 --> 00:31:06
giant MoE model, very similar to DeepSeek architecture in

00:31:05 --> 00:31:13
December. And then a startup, RCAI, and both Nemotron and NVIDIA have teased MoE

00:31:12 --> 00:31:17
models way bigger than 100 billion parameters-

00:31:16 --> 00:31:20
like this 400 billion parameter range coming in this Q1

00:31:19 --> 00:31:24
2026 timeline. So I think this kind of balance is set to change

00:31:23 --> 00:31:28
this year in terms of what people are using the Chinese versus US open models

00:31:27 --> 00:31:33
for, which I'm personally going to be very excited to watch.

00:31:32 --> 00:31:37
- First of all, huge props for being able to name so many of

00:31:36 --> 00:31:39
these. Did you actually name LLaMA?

00:31:38 --> 00:31:40
- No.

00:31:39 --> 00:31:41
- I feel like ...

00:31:40 --> 00:31:42
- RIP.

00:31:41 --> 00:31:43
- This was not on purpose.

00:31:43 --> 00:31:46
- RIP LLaMA.

00:31:45 --> 00:31:49
All right. Can you mention some interesting models that stand out? You mentioned

00:31:48 --> 00:31:52
Qwen 3 is obviously a standout.

00:31:51 --> 00:31:56
- So I would say the year's almost bookended by both DeepSeek

00:31:55 --> 00:31:59
V3 and R1. And then on the other hand, in December, DeepSeek-V3.2.

00:31:59 --> 00:32:03
Because what I like about those is they always have an interesting

00:32:02 --> 00:32:04
architecture tweak

00:32:03 --> 00:32:08
that others don't have. But otherwise, if you want to go with the

00:32:07 --> 00:32:12
familiar but really good performance, Qwen 3 and, like

00:32:11 --> 00:32:16
Nathan said, also gpt-oss-120b. And I think what's

00:32:15 --> 00:32:19
interesting about it is it's kind of like the first public or open

00:32:19 --> 00:32:24
weight model that was really trained with tool use in mind, which

00:32:23 --> 00:32:28
I do think is kind of a paradigm shift where the ecosystem was not quite

00:32:27 --> 00:32:32
ready for it. By tool use, I mean that the LLM is able to do a web

00:32:31 --> 00:32:34
search or to call a Python interpreter.

00:32:33 --> 00:32:37
And I do think it's a standout because it's a huge

00:32:36 --> 00:32:41
unlock. Because one of the most common complaints

00:32:40 --> 00:32:44
about LLMs are, for example, hallucinations, right?

00:32:43 --> 00:32:48
And so, in my opinion, one of the best ways to solve hallucinations is to

00:32:47 --> 00:32:52
not try to always remember information or make things up.

00:32:51 --> 00:32:55
For math, why not use a calculator app or Python?

00:32:54 --> 00:32:58
If I ask the LLM, "Who won the soccer World Cup in

00:32:58 --> 00:33:02
1998?" instead of just trying to memorize, it could go

00:33:02 --> 00:33:06
do a search. I think mostly it's still a Google search.

00:33:06 --> 00:33:10
So ChatGPT and gpt-oss-120b, they would do a tool call to

00:33:09 --> 00:33:13
Google, maybe find the FIFA website. Find, okay, it was

00:33:12 --> 00:33:17
France. It would get you that information reliably instead of just trying to

00:33:16 --> 00:33:21
memorize it. So I think it's a huge unlock which right now

00:33:20 --> 00:33:24
is not fully utilized yet by the open-source, open-weight

00:33:24 --> 00:33:28
ecosystem. A lot of people don't use tool call modes

00:33:27 --> 00:33:32
because I think, first, it's a trust thing. You don't want to run this on your computer

00:33:31 --> 00:33:36
where it has access to tools, could wipe your hard drive or whatever. So you want to maybe

00:33:35 --> 00:33:40
containerize that. But I do think that is like

00:33:39 --> 00:33:45
a really important step for the upcoming years to have this ability.

00:33:44 --> 00:33:48
- So a few quick things. First of all, thank you

00:33:47 --> 00:33:52
for defining what you mean by tool use. I think that's a great thing to do in general

00:33:51 --> 00:33:55
for the concepts we're talking about. Even things as sort of

00:33:54 --> 00:33:58
well-established as MoEs.

00:33:57 --> 00:34:02
You have to say that means mixture of experts, and you kind of

00:34:01 --> 00:34:06
have to build up an intuition for people what that means, how it's actually utilized, what

00:34:05 --> 00:34:10
are the different flavors. So what does it mean that there's just such an

00:34:09 --> 00:34:14
explosion of open models? What's your intuition?

00:34:13 --> 00:34:18
- If you're releasing an open model, you want people to use it, is the first and foremost thing.

00:34:17 --> 00:34:22
And then after that comes things like transparency and trust. I think when

00:34:21 --> 00:34:26
you look at China, the biggest reason is that they want people around the

00:34:25 --> 00:34:30
world to use these models, and I think a lot of people will not. If you look outside

00:34:29 --> 00:34:34
of the US, a lot of people will not pay for software, but they might have computing resources where you can put a model on

00:34:33 --> 00:34:38
it and run it. I think there can also be data that you don't want to send to the

00:34:37 --> 00:34:42
cloud. So the number one thing is getting people to use models, use

00:34:41 --> 00:34:46
AI, or use your AI that might not be able to do it without having access

00:34:45 --> 00:34:46
to the model.

00:34:46 --> 00:34:50
- I guess we should state explicitly, so we've been talking about these Chinese models

00:34:50 --> 00:34:55
and open weight models. Oftentimes, the way they're run is

00:34:54 --> 00:35:02
locally. So it's not like you're sending your data to China or to whoever developed

00:35:01 --> 00:35:04
Silicon Valley, or whoever developed the model.

00:35:04 --> 00:35:08
- A lot of American startups make money by hosting-

00:35:07 --> 00:35:11
...these models from China and selling them. It's called selling

00:35:10 --> 00:35:16
tokens, which means somebody will call the model to do some piece of work.

00:35:15 --> 00:35:20
I think the other reason is for US companies like OpenAI. They are so GPU

00:35:19 --> 00:35:24
deprived. They're at the limits of the GPUs. Whenever they make a release,

00:35:23 --> 00:35:27
they're always talking about like, "Our GPUs are hurting." And

00:35:27 --> 00:35:31
I think during one of these gpt-oss-120b release sessions, Sam

00:35:31 --> 00:35:35
Altman said, "Oh, we're releasing this because we can use your GPUs. We don't have to

00:35:34 --> 00:35:39
use our GPUs, and OpenAI can still get distribution out

00:35:38 --> 00:35:44
of this," which is another very real thing, because it doesn't cost them anything.

00:35:43 --> 00:35:48
- And for the user, I think also, there are users who just use the

00:35:47 --> 00:35:52
model locally how they would use ChatGPT. But also for companies I think it's a huge

00:35:51 --> 00:35:56
unlock to have these models because you can customize them, you can train them, you

00:35:55 --> 00:35:59
can add post-training, add more data. Like,

00:35:58 --> 00:36:03
specialize them into, let's say, law, medical models, whatever you

00:36:02 --> 00:36:07
have. And the appeal, you mentioned Llama, the appeal of the open-weight

00:36:06 --> 00:36:11
models from China is that the open-weight models' licenses

00:36:10 --> 00:36:14
are even friendlier. I think they are just unrestricted open source licenses

00:36:13 --> 00:36:18
where if we use something like Llama or Gemma, there are some strings

00:36:17 --> 00:36:22
attached. I think it's like an upper limit in terms of how many users you have. And then if

00:36:21 --> 00:36:25
you exceed, I don't know, so and so many million users, you have to report your

00:36:24 --> 00:36:29
financial situation to, let's say, Meta or something like that.

00:36:28 --> 00:36:33
And I think while it is a free model, there are strings

00:36:32 --> 00:36:37
attached, and people do like things where strings are not attached. So

00:36:36 --> 00:36:41
I think that's also one of the reasons, besides performance, why the

00:36:40 --> 00:36:44
open-weight models from China are so popular, because you can just use them.

00:36:43 --> 00:36:47
There's no catch in that sense.

00:36:46 --> 00:36:51
- The ecosystem has gotten better on that front, but mostly downstream of these new

00:36:50 --> 00:36:54
providers providing such open licenses. That was funny when you pulled up Perplexity and said,

00:36:53 --> 00:36:58
"Kimi K2 Thinking hosted in the US." Which is just like an exact... I've never seen this, but

00:36:57 --> 00:37:02
it's an exact example of what we're talking about where people are sensitive to this.

00:37:01 --> 00:37:06
But Kimi K2 Thinking and Kimi K2 is a model that is very popular. People

00:37:05 --> 00:37:10
say that has very good creative writing and also in doing some software

00:37:09 --> 00:37:14
things. So it's just these little quirks that people pick up on with different models that they

00:37:13 --> 00:37:14
like.

00:37:14 --> 00:37:19
- What are some interesting ideas that some of these models have

00:37:18 --> 00:37:22
xplored that you can speak to, that are particularly interesting to you?

00:37:21 --> 00:37:26
- Maybe we can go chronologically. I mean, there was, of course, DeepSeek. DeepSeek

00:37:25 --> 00:37:30
R1 that came out in January of 2025, if we just focus on 2025. However,

00:37:29 --> 00:37:34
this was based on DeepSeek-V3, which came out the year before

00:37:33 --> 00:37:38
in December 2024. There are multiple things on the architecture

00:37:37 --> 00:37:41
side. What is fascinating is... I mean, that's what I do with my

00:37:41 --> 00:37:45
from-scratch coding projects. You can still start with GPT-2, and you can

00:37:44 --> 00:37:49
add things to that model to make it into this other model. So it's all

00:37:48 --> 00:37:53
still kind of like the same lineage. It is a very close

00:37:52 --> 00:37:56
relationship between those. But top of my head, DeepSeek—what was

00:37:55 --> 00:38:00
unique there is the Mixture of Experts. Not that they were inventing Mixture of

00:37:59 --> 00:38:03
Experts—we can maybe talk a bit more about what Mixture of Experts

00:38:02 --> 00:38:07
means—but just to list these things first before we

00:38:06 --> 00:38:11
dive into detail. Mixture of Experts, but then they also had Multi-head

00:38:10 --> 00:38:15
Latent Attention, which is a tweak to the attention mechanism, where

00:38:14 --> 00:38:19
this was, I would say, the main distinguishing

00:38:19 --> 00:38:23
factor between these open-weight models. Different

00:38:23 --> 00:38:28
weaks to make inference or KV cache size... We can also define

00:38:27 --> 00:38:32
KV cache in a few moments, but to kind of make it more

00:38:31 --> 00:38:36
conomical to have long context, to shrink the KV cache size. So what are

00:38:35 --> 00:38:39
tweaks that we can do? And most of them focused on the attention mechanism. There

00:38:38 --> 00:38:43
is Multi-head Latent Attention in DeepSeek. There is Group

00:38:43 --> 00:38:47
Query Attention, which is still very popular. It's not invented by any of those models.

00:38:46 --> 00:38:50
It goes back a few years. But that would be the other option.

00:38:50 --> 00:38:54
Sliding window attention—I think OLMo 3 uses it, if I remember

00:38:53 --> 00:38:58
correctly. So there are these different tweaks that make the models

00:38:57 --> 00:39:02
different. Otherwise, I put them all together in an

00:39:01 --> 00:39:05
article once where I just compared them. They are very,

00:39:04 --> 00:39:09
surprisingly similar. It's just different numbers in terms of how many repetitions

00:39:08 --> 00:39:13
of the transformer block you have in the center. And, like,

00:39:12 --> 00:39:17
just little knobs that people tune. But what's so nice about it is

00:39:16 --> 00:39:21
it works no matter what. You can tweak things. You can move the

00:39:20 --> 00:39:25
normalization layers around to get some performance gains. And OLMo is always very

00:39:24 --> 00:39:29
good in ablation studies, showing what it actually

00:39:28 --> 00:39:33
does to the model if you move something around. Ablation studies: does it make it better or

00:39:32 --> 00:39:36
worse? But there are so many, let's say, ways you can implement a transformer and make it still

00:39:36 --> 00:39:40
work. The big ideas that are still prevalent is Mixture of

00:39:39 --> 00:39:44
Experts, multi-head latent attention, sliding window attention, group

00:39:43 --> 00:39:48
query attention. And then at the end of the year, we saw a focus

00:39:47 --> 00:39:52
on making the attention mechanism scale linearly with inference token

00:39:51 --> 00:39:56
prediction. So there was Qwen2-VL, for example,

00:39:55 --> 00:40:00
which added a gated delta net. It's kind of inspired by

00:39:59 --> 00:40:04
State space models, where you have a fixed state that you keep updating. But it

00:40:03 --> 00:40:05
makes essentially this attention

00:40:05 --> 00:40:09
cheaper, or it replaces attention with a cheaper operation.

00:40:08 --> 00:40:13
- And it may be useful to step back and talk about transformer

00:40:12 --> 00:40:14
architecture in general.

00:40:13 --> 00:40:18
- Yeah, so maybe we should start with the GPT-2 architecture. The

00:40:17 --> 00:40:22
transformer that was derived from the "Attention Is All You Need" paper.

00:40:21 --> 00:40:26
The "Attention Is All You Need" paper had a transformer architecture that had two

00:40:25 --> 00:40:30
parts, an encoder and a decoder. And GPT went

00:40:30 --> 00:40:35
just focusing in on the decoder part. It is essentially still a

00:40:34 --> 00:40:38
neural network and it has this attention mechanism

00:40:37 --> 00:40:42
inside. And you predict one token at a time. You

00:40:41 --> 00:40:46
pass it through an embedding layer. There's the transformer block. The transformer block

00:40:45 --> 00:40:50
has attention modules and a fully connected layer. And there are some

00:40:49 --> 00:40:53
normalization layers in between. But it's essentially neural network layers with this attention

00:40:52 --> 00:40:57
mechanism. So coming from GPT-2 when we move on

00:40:56 --> 00:41:01
to gpt-oss-120b, there is, for example, the Mixture of Experts

00:41:00 --> 00:41:05
layer. It's not invented by gpt-oss-120b. It's a few years old.

00:41:04 --> 00:41:12
But it is essentially a tweak to make the model larger without consuming more

00:41:11 --> 00:41:16
compute in each forward pass. So there is this fully connected layer,

00:41:15 --> 00:41:19
and if listeners are familiar with multi-layer

00:41:18 --> 00:41:23
perceptrons, you can think of a mini multi-layer perceptron, a

00:41:22 --> 00:41:27
fully connected neural network layer inside the transformer. And it's very

00:41:26 --> 00:41:30
expensive, because it's fully connected. If you have a thousand inputs and a thousand outputs,

00:41:30 --> 00:41:35
that's like one million connections. And it's a very expensive part in

00:41:34 --> 00:41:38
this transformer. And the idea is to kind of expand that

00:41:37 --> 00:41:42
into multiple feedforward networks. So instead of having

00:41:41 --> 00:41:46
one, let's say you have 256, but it would make it way more expensive,

00:41:45 --> 00:41:50
because now you have 256, but you don't use all of them at the same time. So you

00:41:49 --> 00:41:54
now have a router that says, "Okay, based on this input token, it would be

00:41:53 --> 00:41:58
useful to use this fully connected network." And in that

00:41:57 --> 00:42:02
context, it's called an expert. So a Mixture of Experts means you have multiple

00:42:01 --> 00:42:06
xperts. And depending on what your input is, let's say it's more

00:42:05 --> 00:42:09
math-heavy, it would use different experts, compared to, let's say,

00:42:09 --> 00:42:13
translating input text from English to Spanish. It would maybe consult different

00:42:12 --> 00:42:17
experts. It's not quite clear, I mean, not as clear-cut to say, "Okay, this

00:42:16 --> 00:42:21
only an expert for math and for Spanish." It's a bit more

00:42:20 --> 00:42:24
fuzzy. But the idea is essentially that you pack more

00:42:24 --> 00:42:28
knowledge into the network, but not all the knowledge is used all the time.

00:42:27 --> 00:42:32
That would be very wasteful. So, during the token

00:42:31 --> 00:42:35
generation, you are more selective. There's a router that selects which

00:42:34 --> 00:42:39
tokens should go to which expert. It adds more complexity. It's

00:42:38 --> 00:42:43
harder to train. There's a lot that can go wrong, like collapse and

00:42:42 --> 00:42:47
everything. So I think that's why OLMo 3 still uses dense... I mean, you

00:42:46 --> 00:42:51
have OLMo models with Mixture of Experts, but dense models, where dense

00:42:50 --> 00:42:55
means... So also, it's jargon. There's a distinction

00:42:54 --> 00:42:59
between dense and sparse. So Mixture of Experts is considered sparse,

00:42:58 --> 00:43:02
because we have a lot of experts, but only a few of them are active. So that's called

00:43:01 --> 00:43:06
sparse. And then dense would be the opposite, where you only have one fully

00:43:05 --> 00:43:09
connected module, and it's always utilized.

00:43:08 --> 00:43:12
- So maybe this is a good place to also talk about KV cache.

00:43:11 --> 00:43:15
But actually, before that, even zooming out, like

00:43:14 --> 00:43:23
fundamentally, how many new ideas have been implemented from GPT-2 to today?

00:43:22 --> 00:43:26
Like, how different really are these architectures?

00:43:25 --> 00:43:29
- Take the Mixture of Experts. The attention mechanism in

00:43:28 --> 00:43:33
gpt-oss-120b, that would be the Group Query Attention mechanism. So it's a

00:43:32 --> 00:43:37
slight tweak from Multi-Head Attention to Group Query Attention. So that we have

00:43:36 --> 00:43:40
too... I think they replaced LayerNorm by

00:43:39 --> 00:43:44
RMSNorm, but it's just like a different normalization there and not a big change.

00:43:43 --> 00:43:47
It's just like a tweak. The nonlinear activation function—

00:43:47 --> 00:43:51
people familiar with deep neural networks, I mean, it's the same as

00:43:51 --> 00:43:55
changing sigmoid with ReLU. It's not changing the network fundamentally.

00:43:55 --> 00:43:59
It's just a little tweak. And that's about it, I

00:43:58 --> 00:44:03
would say. It's not really fundamentally that different. It's still the same

00:44:02 --> 00:44:07
architecture. So you can go from

00:44:06 --> 00:44:10
one into the other by just adding these changes basically.

00:44:09 --> 00:44:13
- It fundamentally is still the same architecture.

00:44:12 --> 00:44:17
- Yep. For example, you mentioned my book earlier. That's a GPT-2 model in the

00:44:16 --> 00:44:20
book because it's simple and it's very small, so 124

00:44:19 --> 00:44:24
million parameters approximately. But in the bonus materials, I do have

00:44:24 --> 00:44:29
OLMo from scratch, Gemini 3 from scratch, and other types of from-scratch

00:44:28 --> 00:44:32
models. And I always start it with my GPT-2 model and just tweak the—well,

00:44:31 --> 00:44:36
add different components and you get from one to the other. It's kind of like

00:44:35 --> 00:44:38
a lineage in a sense.

00:44:37 --> 00:44:42
- Can you build up an intuition for people? Because when you zoom out,

00:44:41 --> 00:44:46
you look at it, there's so much rapid advancement in the AI world.

00:44:46 --> 00:44:52
And at the same time, fundamentally the architectures have not changed.

00:44:51 --> 00:44:59
So where is all the turbulence, the turmoil of the advancement happening?

00:44:58 --> 00:45:01
Where are the gains to be had?

00:45:01 --> 00:45:05
- So there are different stages where you develop the network or train the

00:45:04 --> 00:45:09
network. You have the pre-training. Now back in the day, it was just

00:45:08 --> 00:45:13
pre-training with GPT-2. Now you have pre-training, mid-training, and

00:45:12 --> 00:45:17
post-training. So I think right now we are in the

00:45:16 --> 00:45:21
post-training focus stage. Pre-training still gives you

00:45:20 --> 00:45:25
advantages if you scale it up with better, higher quality data. But then

00:45:24 --> 00:45:29
we have capability unlocks that were not there with GPT-2, for

00:45:28 --> 00:45:33
For example, ChatGPT is basically a GPT-3

00:45:32 --> 00:45:37
model. And GPT-3 is the same as GPT-2 in terms of architecture.

00:45:36 --> 00:45:41
What was new was adding supervised fine-tuning and

00:45:40 --> 00:45:45
reinforcement learning with human feedback. So it's more on the algorithmic side than the

00:45:44 --> 00:45:45
architecture.

00:45:44 --> 00:45:49
- I would say that the systems also change a lot. If you listen to NVIDIA's announcements,

00:45:48 --> 00:45:53
they talk about things like, "You now do FP8, you can now do FP4."

00:45:53 --> 00:45:58
What's happening is these labs are figuring out how to utilize more compute to put

00:45:57 --> 00:46:01
it into one model, which lets them train faster and put more

00:46:00 --> 00:46:05
data in. And then you can find better configurations faster by doing

00:46:04 --> 00:46:09
this. So you can look at, essentially, tokens per second per GPU

00:46:09 --> 00:46:13
as a metric that you look at when you're doing large-scale training.

00:46:12 --> 00:46:17
You can go from 10k to 13k by turning on FP8 training,

00:46:16 --> 00:46:21
which means you're using less memory per parameter in the

00:46:20 --> 00:46:24
model. By saving less information, you do less communication and train

00:46:23 --> 00:46:28
faster. So all of these system things underpin way

00:46:27 --> 00:46:35
faster experimentation on data and algorithms.

00:46:35 --> 00:46:40
It's a loop that keeps going where it's hard to describe when you look at architectures and they're

00:46:39 --> 00:46:45
xactly the same, but the code base used to train these models is vastly different-

00:46:44 --> 00:46:49
and you could probably... the GPUs are different but you probably

00:46:48 --> 00:46:55
train gpt-oss-20b way faster in wall-clock time than GPT-2 was trained at the time.

00:46:54 --> 00:46:58
- Yeah. Like you said, they had, for example, in Mixture of Experts this

00:46:57 --> 00:47:02
FP4 optimization where you get more throughput. But I do

00:47:01 --> 00:47:06
think, for speed this is true, but it

00:47:05 --> 00:47:10
doesn't give the model new capabilities. It's just: how much can we

00:47:09 --> 00:47:14
make the computation coarser without suffering in terms

00:47:13 --> 00:47:18
of model performance degradation? But I do think- I mean,

00:47:17 --> 00:47:22
there are alternatives popping up to the transformer. Text diffusion models,

00:47:21 --> 00:47:26
a completely different paradigm. And there is also... I mean, although

00:47:25 --> 00:47:29
text diffusion models might use transformer architectures, it's not an

00:47:28 --> 00:47:33
autoregressive transformer. And also Mamba models.

00:47:32 --> 00:47:37
It's a state space model. But they do have trade-offs, and

00:47:36 --> 00:47:41
nothing has yet replaced the autoregressive

00:47:40 --> 00:47:45
transformer as the state-of-the-art model. For state-of-the-art, you would still

00:47:44 --> 00:47:49
go with that, but there are now alternatives for the cheaper

00:47:48 --> 00:47:52
end—alternatives that are kind of making

00:47:51 --> 00:47:56
compromises. It's not just one architecture anymore. There are

00:47:56 --> 00:48:01
little ones coming up. But if we talk about the state-of-the-art, it's pretty much still

00:48:00 --> 00:48:07
the transformer architecture, autoregressive, derived from GPT-2 essentially.

00:48:06 --> 00:48:11
- I guess the big question here is, we talked quite a bit about the architecture behind

00:48:10 --> 00:48:17
the pre-training. Are the scaling laws holding strong across pre-training,

00:48:16 --> 00:48:21
post-training, inference, context size, data, and synthetic data?

00:48:20 --> 00:48:24
- I'd like to start with the technical definition of a scaling law-

00:48:23 --> 00:48:27
which informs all of this. The scaling law is the power law relationship

00:48:26 --> 00:48:31
between... You can think of the x-axis, so kind of what you are scaling as a

00:48:30 --> 00:48:35
combination of compute and data, which are kind of similar,

00:48:34 --> 00:48:38
and then the y-axis is like the held-out prediction accuracy over next tokens.

00:48:38 --> 00:48:43
We talked about models being autoregressive. It's like if you keep a set of

00:48:44 --> 00:48:48
text that the model has not seen, how accurate will it get when you train? And

00:48:47 --> 00:48:52
the idea of scaling laws came when people figured out that that

00:48:51 --> 00:48:56
was a very predictable relationship. And I think that

00:48:55 --> 00:49:00
technical term is continuing, and then the question is, what

00:48:59 --> 00:49:04
do users get out of it? Then there are more types of scaling where,

00:49:03 --> 00:49:08
OpenAI's o1 was famous for introducing inference time scaling. And I think

00:49:07 --> 00:49:12
less famously for also showing that you can scale reinforcement learning training

00:49:11 --> 00:49:16
and get kind of this log x-axis and then a

00:49:15 --> 00:49:19
linear increase in performance on y-axis. So there's kind of these three axes now

00:49:19 --> 00:49:24
here the traditional scaling laws are talked about for pre-training, which is how big

00:49:23 --> 00:49:27
your model is and how big your dataset is, and then scaling reinforcement learning,

00:49:26 --> 00:49:31
which is like how long can you do this trial and error learning that we'll talk about.

00:49:30 --> 00:49:35
We'll define more of this, and then this inference time compute, which is just letting the

00:49:34 --> 00:49:39
model generate more tokens on a specific problem. So I'm kind of bullish,

00:49:39 --> 00:49:43
but they're all really still working, but the low-hanging fruit has mostly been taken,

00:49:43 --> 00:49:47
especially in the last year on reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards,

00:49:46 --> 00:49:51
which is this RLVR, and then inference time scaling,

00:49:50 --> 00:49:54
which is just why these models feel so different to use, where previously

00:49:53 --> 00:49:58
ou would get that first token immediately. And now they'll go off for seconds,

00:49:57 --> 00:50:02
minutes, or even hours, generating these hidden thoughts

00:50:01 --> 00:50:06
before giving you the first word of your answer. And that's all about this

00:50:05 --> 00:50:07
inference time scaling, which is

00:50:07 --> 00:50:12
uch a wonderful kind of step function in terms of how the models change abilities.

00:50:11 --> 00:50:15
They kind of enabled this tool use stuff and enabled this much better

00:50:14 --> 00:50:18
software engineering that we were talking about. And this,

00:50:18 --> 00:50:22
when we say enabled, is almost entirely downstream of the fact that this

00:50:21 --> 00:50:26
reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards training just kind of let the models

00:50:25 --> 00:50:30
pick up these skills very easily. So let the models learn,

00:50:29 --> 00:50:34
so if you look at the reasoning process when the models are generating

00:50:33 --> 00:50:38
a lot of tokens, what it'll often be doing is: it tries a tool, it looks at what it gets back.

00:50:37 --> 00:50:42
It tries another API, it sees what it gets back and if it solves the problem.

00:50:41 --> 00:50:45
So the models, when you're training them, very quickly learn to do this.

00:50:45 --> 00:50:49
And then at the end of the day, that gives this kind of general foundation

00:50:48 --> 00:50:53
where the model can use CLI commands very nicely in your repo

00:50:52 --> 00:50:57
and handle Git for you and move things around and organize things

00:50:56 --> 00:51:00
or search to find more information, which if we were sitting in these chairs a year ago

00:51:00 --> 00:51:05
is something that we didn't really think of the models doing. So this is just

00:51:04 --> 00:51:08
kind of something that has happened this year and has totally transformed how

00:51:07 --> 00:51:12
we think of using AI which

00:51:11 --> 00:51:16
evolution and just unlocks so much value. But it's like,

00:51:15 --> 00:51:20
just so- pr- unlocks so much value. But it's- it's like,

00:51:19 --> 00:51:24
it's not clear what the next avenue will be in terms of unlocking stuff like this.

00:51:23 --> 00:51:28
I think there's... we'll get to continual learning later, but there's a lot of buzz

00:51:27 --> 00:51:32
around certain areas of AI, but no one knows when the next step function

00:51:31 --> 00:51:32
will really come.

00:51:31 --> 00:51:35
- So you've actually said quite a lot of things there,

00:51:35 --> 00:51:40
and said profound things quickly. It would be nice to

00:51:39 --> 00:51:44
unpack them a little bit. You say you're bullish basically on every version of

00:51:43 --> 00:51:49
scaling. So can we just even start at the beginning? Pre-training,

00:51:49 --> 00:51:54
are we kind of implying that the low- hanging fruit on pre-training

00:51:53 --> 00:51:58
scaling has been picked? Has pre-training

00:51:57 --> 00:52:02
hit a plateau, or is even pre-training still something you're bullish on?

00:52:01 --> 00:52:05
- Pre-training has gotten extremely expensive. I think to scale up

00:52:04 --> 00:52:09
re-training, it's also implying that you're gonna serve a very

00:52:08 --> 00:52:13
large model to the users. So I think that it's been

00:52:12 --> 00:52:17
loosely established the likes of GPT-4 and similar models were around one

00:52:16 --> 00:52:21
trillion parameters at the biggest size. There's a lot of rumors that they've

00:52:20 --> 00:52:25
actually gotten smaller as training has gotten more efficient. You want to make

00:52:24 --> 00:52:29
the model smaller because then your costs of serving go down proportionately.

00:52:28 --> 00:52:33
These models, the cost of training them is really low relative to the

00:52:32 --> 00:52:37
cost of serving them to hundreds of millions of users. I think DeepSeek had this famous number

00:52:36 --> 00:52:41
of about five million dollars for pre-training at cloud market rates.

00:52:40 --> 00:52:45
In OLMo 3, section 2.4 in the paper, we just

00:52:44 --> 00:52:48
detailed how long we had the GPU clusters sitting around for training which

00:52:47 --> 00:52:52
includes engineering issues, multiple seeds,

00:52:51 --> 00:52:56
and it was like about two million dollars to rent the cluster to deal with all the headaches of

00:52:55 --> 00:52:59
training a model. So these models are pretty—

00:52:59 --> 00:53:03
like, a lot of people could get one to 10 million dollars to train a model,

00:53:02 --> 00:53:07
but the recurring costs of serving millions of users is

00:53:06 --> 00:53:11
really billions of dollars of compute. I think that you can look at a

00:53:10 --> 00:53:15
thousand GPU rental you can pay 100 grand a day for. And these

00:53:15 --> 00:53:19
companies could have millions of GPUs. Like you can look at how much these things

00:53:18 --> 00:53:21
cost to sit around. So that's kind of a big

00:53:21 --> 00:53:26
thing, and then it's like, if scaling is actually giving you a better model,

00:53:25 --> 00:53:30
is it gonna be financially worth it? And I think we'll slowly push it

00:53:29 --> 00:53:34
out as AI solves more compelling tasks, so like the likes of Claude Opus 4.5,

00:53:33 --> 00:53:38
making Claude Code just work for things. I—

00:53:37 --> 00:53:42
I launched this project called the ATOM project, which is American Truly

00:53:41 --> 00:53:45
Open Models in July, and that was like a true vibe

00:53:45 --> 00:53:49
coded website, and like, I have a job to make

00:53:49 --> 00:53:53
plots and stuff. And then I came back to refresh it in the last few weeks and it's like

00:53:52 --> 00:53:57
Claude Opus 4.5 versus whatever model at the time was like, just crushed all the

00:53:56 --> 00:54:01
issues that it had from building in June and July and like, it might be a

00:54:00 --> 00:54:05
bigger model. There's a lot of things that go into this, but there's still progress coming.

00:54:04 --> 00:54:08
- So what you're speaking to is the nuance of the y-axis of the scaling

00:54:07 --> 00:54:12
laws—the way it's experienced versus on a benchmark, the

00:54:11 --> 00:54:16
actual intelligence might be different. But still, your intuition

00:54:15 --> 00:54:20
about pre-training, if you scale the size of compute,

00:54:20 --> 00:54:25
will the models get better? Not whether it's financially viable but

00:54:24 --> 00:54:29
just from the law aspect of it, do you think the models will get smarter?

00:54:28 --> 00:54:33
- Yeah. And I think that there's... And this sometimes comes off as

00:54:32 --> 00:54:36
almost like disillusionment from people, leadership at AI

00:54:35 --> 00:54:40
companies saying this, but they're like, "It's held for 13 orders of magnitude

00:54:39 --> 00:54:44
of compute, why would it ever end?" So I think fundamentally it

00:54:43 --> 00:54:48
is pretty unlikely to stop, it's just eventually we're not even gonna be able to

00:54:47 --> 00:54:51
test the bigger scales because of all the problems that come with more compute.

00:54:50 --> 00:54:55
I think that there's a lot of talk on how 2026

00:54:54 --> 00:54:59
is a year when very large Blackwell compute clusters,

00:54:58 --> 00:55:03
like gigawatt-scale facilities at hyperscalers, are coming online.

00:55:03 --> 00:55:08
These were all contracts for power and data centers that

00:55:07 --> 00:55:12
were signed and sought out in 2022 and 2023.

00:55:11 --> 00:55:16
So before or right after ChatGPT. It took this two-to-three-year

00:55:15 --> 00:55:20
lead time to build these bigger clusters to train the models. While there's obviously

00:55:19 --> 00:55:24
immense interest in building even more data centers than that. So that is the crux

00:55:23 --> 00:55:28
that people are saying: these new clusters are coming. The labs are gonna have

00:55:27 --> 00:55:31
more compute for training. They're going to utilize this,

00:55:30 --> 00:55:35
but it's not a given. I've seen so much progress that I expect it,

00:55:34 --> 00:55:39
and I expect a little bit bigger models, and I expect...

00:55:39 --> 00:55:44
I would say it's more like we'll see a $2,000 subscription this year. We've seen

00:55:43 --> 00:55:47
$200 subscriptions. That could 10X again, and these are the kind of things that

00:55:47 --> 00:55:51
could come, and they're all downstream of this bigger model

00:55:50 --> 00:55:54
that offers just a little bit more cutting edge.

00:55:53 --> 00:55:58
- So, you know, it's reported that xAI is gonna hit that one-gigawatt scale

00:55:57 --> 00:56:01
arly '26, and a

00:56:01 --> 00:56:06
full two gigawatts by year end. How do you think they'll utilize that

00:56:06 --> 00:56:09
in the context of scaling laws?

00:56:09 --> 00:56:13
Is a lot of that inference? Is a lot of that training?

00:56:12 --> 00:56:17
- It ends up being all of the above. So I think

00:56:16 --> 00:56:21
that all of your decisions when you're training a model come back to

00:56:20 --> 00:56:24
pre-training. So if you're going to scale RL on a model, you still need to

00:56:23 --> 00:56:28
decide on your architecture that enables this. We were talking about other

00:56:27 --> 00:56:32
architectures and using different types of attention, or a mixture of experts models.

00:56:31 --> 00:56:36
The sparse nature of MoE models makes it

00:56:35 --> 00:56:40
much more efficient to do generation, which becomes a big part

00:56:39 --> 00:56:44
of post-training, and you need to have your architecture ready so

00:56:43 --> 00:56:48
that you can actually scale up this compute. I still think most of the

00:56:47 --> 00:56:51
compute is going in at pre-training. Because you can still make a model

00:56:51 --> 00:56:55
better, you still want to go and revisit this. You still want the best base model

00:56:54 --> 00:56:59
you can. And in a few years that'll saturate and the RL compute will just

00:56:59 --> 00:57:01
go longer.

00:57:00 --> 00:57:06
- Are there people who disagree with you and say pre-training is dead?

00:57:06 --> 00:57:10
It's all about scaling inference, scaling post-training,

00:57:09 --> 00:57:16
scaling context, continual learning, scaling data, synthetic data?

00:57:15 --> 00:57:20
- People vibe that way and describe it in that way, but I think it's not the practice

00:57:19 --> 00:57:20
that is happening.

00:57:19 --> 00:57:22
- It's just the general vibe of people saying this thing is dead-

00:57:21 --> 00:57:25
The excitement is elsewhere. So the low-hanging fruit-

00:57:24 --> 00:57:29
...in RL is elsewhere. For example, we released our model in November...

00:57:28 --> 00:57:32
Every company has deadlines. Our deadline was November 20th, and

00:57:31 --> 00:57:36
for that, our run was five days, which compared to 2024

00:57:36 --> 00:57:40
is a very long time to just be doing post- training at a model of 30 billion

00:57:39 --> 00:57:44
parameters. It's not a big model. And then in December, we had another release,

00:57:43 --> 00:57:48
where we let the RL run for another three and a half weeks,

00:57:47 --> 00:57:52
and the model got notably better, so we released it. And that's a

00:57:51 --> 00:57:56
to just allocate to something that is going to be your peak-

00:57:55 --> 00:57:58
...for the year. So it's like-

00:57:57 --> 00:57:59
The reasoning is-

00:57:58 --> 00:58:02
There's these types of decisions when training a model where they just...

00:58:01 --> 00:58:06
