They can't leave it forever. You have to keep pulling

00:58:05 --> 00:58:09
in the improvements from researchers. So you redo

00:58:08 --> 00:58:13
pre-training, you'll do this post-training for a month, but then you

00:58:12 --> 00:58:17
need to give it to your users. You need to do safety testing. So it's

00:58:16 --> 00:58:21
just... I think there's a lot in place that reinforces this cycle of

00:58:20 --> 00:58:25
updating the models. Things improve. You get a new compute cluster

00:58:24 --> 00:58:29
that lets you do something more stably or faster. It's like

00:58:28 --> 00:58:32
you hear a lot about Blackwell having rollout issues, where at

00:58:31 --> 00:58:36
AI2, most of the models we're pre-training are on 1,000 to 2,000

00:58:35 --> 00:58:40
GPUs. But when pre-training on 10,000 or 100,000 GPUs, you hit

00:58:39 --> 00:58:44
very different failures. GPUs break in weird ways, and on a 100,000

00:58:43 --> 00:58:48
GPU run, you're pretty much guaranteed to have one GPU

00:58:47 --> 00:58:51
that is down. Your training code must handle that redundancy, which is a

00:58:50 --> 00:58:52
very different problem.

00:58:51 --> 00:58:56
Whereas what we're doing, like playing with post-training on a cluster,

00:58:55 --> 00:59:00
or for people learning ML, what they're battling to

00:58:59 --> 00:59:02
train these biggest models is just-

00:59:02 --> 00:59:06
...mass distributed scale, and it's very different.

00:59:05 --> 00:59:12
But that's somewhat different than- That's a systems problem-

00:59:11 --> 00:59:16
...in order to enable scaling laws, especially at pre-training.

00:59:15 --> 00:59:20
You need all these GPUs at once. When we shift to RL, it actually

00:59:19 --> 00:59:24
lends itself to heterogeneous compute because you have many copies of the

00:59:23 --> 00:59:28
model. To do a primer for

00:59:27 --> 00:59:32
language model reinforcement learning, what you're doing is having two sets of

00:59:31 --> 00:59:35
GPUs. One you can call the actor, and one you call the learner.

00:59:34 --> 00:59:39
The learner is where your actual reinforcement learning updates happen.

00:59:38 --> 00:59:43
These are traditionally policy gradient algorithms.

00:59:42 --> 00:59:47
Proximal Policy Optimization, PPO, and Group Relative Policy

00:59:46 --> 00:59:51
Optimization, GRPO, are the two popular classes.

00:59:50 --> 00:59:54
And on the other side you have actors which are generating

00:59:53 --> 00:59:58
completions, and these completions are what you're going to grade.

00:59:57 --> 01:00:02
Reinforcement learning is all about optimizing reward. In practice,

01:00:01 --> 01:00:06
you can have a lot of different actors in different parts of the world

01:00:05 --> 01:00:09
oing different types of problems, and then you send it back to

01:00:08 --> 01:00:13
this highly networked compute cluster to do this actual learning

01:00:12 --> 01:00:17
where you take the gradients. You need to have a

01:00:16 --> 01:00:21
tightly meshed network to do different types of parallelism and

01:00:20 --> 01:00:25
spread out your model for efficient training. Every different type of

01:00:24 --> 01:00:29
training and serving has these considerations to scale.

01:00:28 --> 01:00:33
We talked about pre-training and RL, and then inference time scaling-

01:00:32 --> 01:00:36
how do you serve a model that's thinking for an hour to 100 million users?

01:00:35 --> 01:00:40
I don't know about that, but I know that's a hard problem. In order

01:00:39 --> 01:00:44
to give people this intelligence, there's all these systems problems, and we need

01:00:43 --> 01:00:47
more compute and you need more stable compute to do it."

01:00:46 --> 01:00:51
- But you're bullish on all of these kinds of scaling is what I'm hearing. On the

01:00:50 --> 01:00:55
inference, on the reasoning, even on the pre-training?

01:00:54 --> 01:00:59
- Yeah, so that's a big can of worms here, but there are basically two...

01:00:58 --> 01:01:02
The knobs are the training and the inference scaling where you can

01:01:02 --> 01:01:06
get gains. In a world where we had, let's say, infinite

01:01:05 --> 01:01:10
compute resources, you want to do all of them. So you have training, you have inference

01:01:09 --> 01:01:14
scaling, and training is like a hierarchy: it's pre-training, mid-training,

01:01:13 --> 01:01:18
post-training. Changing the model size, more training data, training a bigger

01:01:17 --> 01:01:23
model gives you more knowledge in the model. Then the model, let's say, has a

01:01:22 --> 01:01:27
better base model. Back in the day, or still, we call it a foundation

01:01:26 --> 01:01:31
model, and it unlocks... But you don't, let's say, have

01:01:30 --> 01:01:35
the model be able to solve your most complex tasks

01:01:34 --> 01:01:39
during pre-training or after pre-training. You still have these other unlock

01:01:38 --> 01:01:42
phases where you have mid-training or, for example, post-training with

01:01:41 --> 01:01:46
RL that unlocks capabilities that the model has in terms of knowledge in the pre-training.

01:01:45 --> 01:01:50
And I think, sure, if you

01:01:49 --> 01:01:54
do more pre-training, you get a better base model that you can unlock

01:01:53 --> 01:01:58
later. But like Nathan said, it just becomes too expensive. We don't have infinite compute,

01:01:57 --> 01:02:02
so you have to decide, do I want to spend that compute more on making the model

01:02:01 --> 01:02:05
arger? It's like a trade-off. In an ideal world, you want to do

01:02:05 --> 01:02:09
all of them. And I think in that sense, scaling is still pretty much alive. You would

01:02:08 --> 01:02:13
still get a better model, but like we saw with Claude Opus 4.5, it's just not worth it.

01:02:12 --> 01:02:17
Because you can unlock more

01:02:16 --> 01:02:21
performance with other techniques at that current moment, especially if you

01:02:20 --> 01:02:25
look at inference scaling. That's one of the biggest gains this year with o1,

01:02:24 --> 01:02:29
where it took a smaller model further than

01:02:29 --> 01:02:33
pre-training a larger model like Claude Opus 4.5. So I wouldn't say

01:02:32 --> 01:02:37
pre-training scaling is dead, it's just that there are other more attractive ways to scale

01:02:36 --> 01:02:41
right now. But at some point, you will still want to make some

01:02:40 --> 01:02:44
progress on the pre-training. The thing also to consider

01:02:44 --> 01:02:49
is where you want to spend your money. If you spend it more

01:02:48 --> 01:02:53
on the pre-training, it's like a fixed cost. You train the model, and then it has this

01:02:52 --> 01:02:57
capability forever. You can always use it.

01:02:56 --> 01:03:00
With inference scaling, you don't spend money during training, you spend money

01:03:00 --> 01:03:04
later per query, and then it's also like math. How long is my model gonna be

01:03:04 --> 01:03:08
on the market if I replace it in half a year? Maybe it's not worth

01:03:07 --> 01:03:12
spending $5 million, $10 million, $100 million on training

01:03:11 --> 01:03:16
it longer. Maybe I will just do more

01:03:15 --> 01:03:20
inference scaling and get performance there. It maybe costs me $2 million in terms

01:03:19 --> 01:03:23
of user queries. It becomes a question of how many users you have and doing the

01:03:22 --> 01:03:27
math, and I think that's also where it's interesting where ChatGPT is in a

01:03:26 --> 01:03:31
position. I think they have a lot of users where they need to go a bit cheaper, where they have that

01:03:30 --> 01:03:34
GPT-5 model that is a bit smaller. Other companies that

01:03:33 --> 01:03:38
have... Let's say, if your customers have other

01:03:37 --> 01:03:42
trade-offs. For example, there was also the Math Olympiad or some

01:03:41 --> 01:03:44
of these math problems where

01:03:44 --> 01:03:49
ChatGPT or they had a proprietary model, and I'm pretty sure it's just

01:03:48 --> 01:03:53
like a model that has been fine-tuned a little bit more, but most of it

01:03:52 --> 01:03:57
was during inference scaling to achieve peak performance in certain tasks.

01:03:56 --> 01:04:00
need that all the time. But yeah, long story short, I do

01:03:59 --> 01:04:04
think all of these pre-training, mid-training, post-training,

01:04:03 --> 01:04:08
inference scaling, they are all still things you want to do. It's just

01:04:07 --> 01:04:12
finding—at the moment, in this year, it's finding the right ratio that gives you the best

01:04:11 --> 01:04:13
bang for the buck, basically.

01:04:13 --> 01:04:17
- I think this might be a good place to define pre-training, mid-training, and

01:04:16 --> 01:04:19
post-training.

01:04:18 --> 01:04:22
- So, pre-training is the classic training one next token prediction at a time.

01:04:21 --> 01:04:26
You have a big corpus of data. And Nathan probably also has very interesting

01:04:26 --> 01:04:30
insights there because of OLMo 3. A big portion of the paper focuses on the

01:04:29 --> 01:04:34
right data mix. So, pre-training is essentially just, you know, training

01:04:33 --> 01:04:38
cross entropy loss, training on next token prediction on a

01:04:37 --> 01:04:42
vast corpus of internet data, books, papers and so forth. It

01:04:41 --> 01:04:46
has changed a little bit over the years in the sense people used to throw in

01:04:45 --> 01:04:49
everything they can. Now, it's not just raw data. It's

01:04:48 --> 01:04:53
also synthetic data where people, let's say,

01:04:52 --> 01:04:57
rephrase certain things. So synthetic data doesn't necessarily mean

01:04:56 --> 01:05:01
purely AI-made data. It's also taking

01:05:00 --> 01:05:05
something from an article, a Wikipedia article, and then rephrasing it as

01:05:04 --> 01:05:12
a Q&A question or summarizing it, rewording it, and making better data that way.

01:05:12 --> 01:05:17
Because I think of it also like with humans. If someone,

01:05:16 --> 01:05:21
let's say, reads a book compared to a messy—no offense, but like—Reddit post

01:05:20 --> 01:05:26
or something like that, I do think you learn—

01:05:25 --> 01:05:29
- There's going to be a post about this, Sebastian.

01:05:28 --> 01:05:32
- Some Reddit data is very coveted and excellent for training.

01:05:31 --> 01:05:33
You just have to filter it.

01:05:33 --> 01:05:38
- And I think that's the idea. I think it's like if someone

01:05:37 --> 01:05:43
took that and rephrased it in a, let's say, more concise and structured way,

01:05:42 --> 01:05:46
I think it's higher quality data that gets the LLM there faster.

01:05:46 --> 01:05:51
You get the same LLM out of it at the end, but it trains faster because

01:05:50 --> 01:05:55
if the grammar and the punctuation are

01:05:54 --> 01:05:59
correct, it already learns the correct way, versus getting

01:05:58 --> 01:06:03
information from a messy source and then learning later how to correct that.

01:06:02 --> 01:06:10
So, I think that is how pre-training evolved and why scaling still works.

01:06:09 --> 01:06:14
It's not just about the amount of data, it's also

01:06:13 --> 01:06:18
the tricks to make that data better for you, in a sense. And

01:06:17 --> 01:06:22
then mid-training is... I mean, it used to be called pre-training.

01:06:21 --> 01:06:26
I think it's called mid-training because it was awkward to have pre-training and post-training but nothing in the

01:06:25 --> 01:06:30
middle, right? It sounds a bit weird. You have pre-training and post-training, but what's the actual training?

01:06:29 --> 01:06:34
So, the mid-training is usually similar to pre-training,

01:06:33 --> 01:06:38
but it's a bit more specialized. It's the same

01:06:37 --> 01:06:41
algorithm, but what you do is you focus, for example, on

01:06:40 --> 01:06:45
long-context documents. The reason you

01:06:44 --> 01:06:49
don't do that during pre-training is because you don't have that many long

01:06:48 --> 01:06:53
context documents. We have a specific phase. And one problem of LLMs is

01:06:52 --> 01:06:56
till that it's a neural network. It has the problem of catastrophic

01:06:55 --> 01:07:00
forgetting. So, you teach it something, it forgets other things. And you

01:06:59 --> 01:07:04
wanna... I mean, it's not 100% forgetting, but it's like "no free lunch."

01:07:03 --> 01:07:08
It's the same with humans. If you ask me some math I learned 10 years

01:07:07 --> 01:07:10
ago, I would have to look at it again.

01:07:09 --> 01:07:14
- Nathan was actually saying that he's consuming so much content that there's a catastrophic

01:07:13 --> 01:07:15
forgetting issue.

01:07:14 --> 01:07:18
- Yeah, I'm trying to learn so much about AI, and it's like I was learning about

01:07:18 --> 01:07:23
pre-training parallelism. I'm like, "I lost something and I don't know what it was."

01:07:22 --> 01:07:26
- I don't want to anthropomorphize LLMs, but it's the same kind of thing

01:07:25 --> 01:07:30
in how humans learn. I mean, quantity is not always better

01:07:29 --> 01:07:34
because you have to be selective. And mid-training is being

01:07:33 --> 01:07:38
selective in terms of quality content at the end. So the last thing the

01:07:37 --> 01:07:41
LLM has seen is the quality stuff. And then post-training is all

01:07:40 --> 01:07:45
the fine-tuning, supervised fine-tuning,

01:07:44 --> 01:07:49
DPO, Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR),

01:07:49 --> 01:07:54
with human feedback, and so forth. So the refinement stages. And it's also

01:07:53 --> 01:07:58
interesting, it's a cost thing. You spend a lot of money on pre-training right now.

01:07:57 --> 01:08:02
RL a bit less. With RL, you don't really

01:08:01 --> 01:08:05
teach it knowledge. It's more like unlocking the knowledge; it's more like a skill

01:08:05 --> 01:08:09
earning, like how to solve problems with the knowledge that it has from pre-training.

01:08:09 --> 01:08:13
There are actually three papers this year, or last year, 2025,

01:08:12 --> 01:08:18
on RL for pre-training. But I don't think anyone does that in production.

01:08:17 --> 01:08:19
- Toy examples for now.

01:08:18 --> 01:08:23
- Toy examples, right? But to generalize, RL post-training is more like

01:08:22 --> 01:08:27
the skill unlock, where pre-training is like soaking up the knowledge.

01:08:26 --> 01:08:31
- A few things that could be helpful. A lot of people

01:08:31 --> 01:08:36
think of synthetic data as being bad for training the models. You mentioned

01:08:35 --> 01:08:37
how DeepSeek got almost...

01:08:37 --> 01:08:42
OCR, which is Optical Character Recognition. A lot of labs did it.

01:08:41 --> 01:08:45
Ai2 had one, Meta had multiple.

01:08:44 --> 01:08:49
And the reason each of these labs has these is because there are vast

01:08:48 --> 01:08:53
amounts of PDFs and other digital documents on the web that aren't in formats

01:08:52 --> 01:08:57
that are encoded with text easily. So you use these Almost-OCR,

01:08:56 --> 01:09:01
DeepSeek OCR, or what we called our Almost-OCR, to extract trillions of

01:09:00 --> 01:09:05
tokens of candidate data for pre-training.

01:09:04 --> 01:09:08
Pre-training dataset size is measured in trillions of tokens.

01:09:08 --> 01:09:12
Smaller models from researchers can be something like five to 10 trillion.

01:09:11 --> 01:09:16
Um,

01:09:15 --> 01:09:20
Qwen is documented going up to 50 trillion, and there are rumors that these closed

01:09:19 --> 01:09:24
labs can go to 100 trillion tokens. Getting this potential data to put

01:09:23 --> 01:09:28
in—they have a very big funnel, and the data you actually train on is

01:09:27 --> 01:09:32
a small percentage of this. This character recognition data

01:09:31 --> 01:09:36
would be described as synthetic data for pre-training in a lab. And then there's

01:09:35 --> 01:09:40
also the fact that ChatGPT now gives wonderful answers, and you

01:09:39 --> 01:09:43
can train on those best answers, and that's synthetic data. It's very different than

01:09:42 --> 01:09:47
early ChatGPT with lots of hallucination data.

01:09:46 --> 01:09:49
when people became grounded in synthetic data.

01:09:48 --> 01:09:53
- One interesting question is, if I recall correctly, OLMo 3 was trained with less

01:09:52 --> 01:09:57
data than specifically some other open-weight models, maybe even

01:09:56 --> 01:10:01
OLMo 2. But you still got better performance, and that might be one example of how

01:10:00 --> 01:10:02
the data helped.

01:10:01 --> 01:10:03
- It's mostly down to data quality.

01:10:02 --> 01:10:07
I think if we had more compute, we would train for longer. I think we'd ultimately see

01:10:06 --> 01:10:11
that as something we would want to do. And especially with big

01:10:10 --> 01:10:15
models, you need more compute, because we talked about having more parameters and

01:10:14 --> 01:10:19
we talked about knowledge. Essentially, there's a ratio where big models can absorb

01:10:18 --> 01:10:23
more from data, and then you get more benefit out of this.

01:10:22 --> 01:10:27
Any logarithmic graph in your mind is like a small model will level off

01:10:26 --> 01:10:30
sooner if you're measuring tons of tokens, and bigger models need

01:10:29 --> 01:10:34
more. But mostly, we aren't training that big of models right now at

01:10:33 --> 01:10:39
AI2, and getting the highest quality data we can is the natural starting point.

01:10:38 --> 01:10:43
- Is there something to be said about the topic of data quality? Is there some

01:10:42 --> 01:10:47
low-hanging fruit there still where the quality could be improved?

01:10:46 --> 01:10:50
- It's like turning the crank. Historically, in the open, there's been a

01:10:49 --> 01:10:54
canonical best pre-training dataset that has

01:10:53 --> 01:10:58
moved around between who has the most recent one or the best recent effort. Like

01:10:57 --> 01:11:01
AI2's Dolma was very early with the first OLMo, and Hugging Face had

01:11:00 --> 01:11:05
FineWeb. And there's a DCLM project, which has been kind of like a,

01:11:04 --> 01:11:09
which stands for Data Comp Language Model. There's been Data Comp

01:11:08 --> 01:11:13
for other machine learning projects, and they had a very strong

01:11:12 --> 01:11:17
dataset. And a lot of it is the internet is becoming

01:11:16 --> 01:11:21
fairly closed off, so we have Common Crawl, which is hundreds of trillions of tokens,

01:11:20 --> 01:11:24
and you filter it. It looks like scientific work where

01:11:24 --> 01:11:28
you're training classifiers and making decisions based on how you

01:11:27 --> 01:11:32
prune down this dataset into the highest quality stuff and the stuff

01:11:31 --> 01:11:36
that suits your tasks. Previously, language models were tested a lot more

01:11:35 --> 01:11:40
on knowledge and conversational things, but now they're expected

01:11:39 --> 01:11:44
to do math and code. To train a reasoning model, you need to remix your whole

01:11:43 --> 01:11:48
dataset. And there's a lot of wonderful scientific methods here where you can,

01:11:47 --> 01:11:52
you can take your gigantic dataset, sample really tiny things from

01:11:51 --> 01:11:56
different sources, such as GitHub, Stack Exchange, Reddit,

01:11:55 --> 01:12:00
Wikipedia. You can sample small things from them, and train small models on each of these

01:11:59 --> 01:12:04
mixes and measure their performance on your evaluations. You can just do basic linear

01:12:03 --> 01:12:08
egression, and it's like, "Here's your optimal dataset." But if your evaluations change, your

01:12:07 --> 01:12:12
dataset changes a lot. So a lot of OLMo 3 was new sources for

01:12:11 --> 01:12:16
easoning to be better at math and code, and then you do this mixing

01:12:15 --> 01:12:19
procedure and it gives you the answer. I think that's happened at labs this

01:12:19 --> 01:12:23
year; there's new hot things, whether it's coding environments or web

01:12:22 --> 01:12:27
navigation, and you need to bring in new data, change your whole pre-training so that your

01:12:26 --> 01:12:30
post-training can work better. And that's like the constant

01:12:30 --> 01:12:36
evolution and the redetermining of what they care about for their models.

01:12:35 --> 01:12:40
- Are there fun anecdotes of what sources of data are

01:12:39 --> 01:12:43
particularly high quality that we wouldn't expect? You mentioned Reddit

01:12:42 --> 01:12:46
sometimes can be a source.

01:12:45 --> 01:12:52
- Reddit was very useful. I think PDFs is definitely one.

01:12:51 --> 01:12:53
- Oh, especially arXiv.

01:12:52 --> 01:12:58
- Yeah, so AI2 has run Semantic Scholar for a long time, which is

01:12:58 --> 01:13:02
what you can say is a competitor to Google Scholar with a lot more features.

01:13:01 --> 01:13:06
And to do this, AI2 has found and scraped a lot of PDFs for

01:13:05 --> 01:13:10
openly accessible papers that might not be behind the

01:13:09 --> 01:13:13
closed walled garden of a certain publisher. So, truly open

01:13:12 --> 01:13:17
scientific PDFs. And if you sit on all of these and you process

01:13:16 --> 01:13:19
it, you can get value out of it. And I think that

01:13:18 --> 01:13:23
like, a lot of that style of work has been done by

01:13:22 --> 01:13:26
the frontier labs did much earlier. You just need to have

01:13:25 --> 01:13:30
a pretty skilled researcher that understands how things change

01:13:29 --> 01:13:34
models; they bring it in, clean it, and it's a lot of labor.

01:13:33 --> 01:13:38
When frontier labs scale researchers, a lot more goes

01:13:37 --> 01:13:42
into data. If you join a frontier lab and you want to

01:13:41 --> 01:13:45
have impact, the best way to do it is just find new data that's

01:13:44 --> 01:13:49
better. And then, the fancy, glamorous algorithmic things like

01:13:49 --> 01:13:53
figuring out how to make o1 is like the sexiest thought of a scientist. It's

01:13:52 --> 01:13:57
like, "Oh, I figured out how to scale RL." There's a group that did that, but most of the

01:13:56 --> 01:13:59
contribution is like—

01:13:58 --> 01:13:59
- On the dataset

01:13:58 --> 01:14:03
- ..."I'm gonna make the data better," or, "I'm gonna make the infrastructure better so everyone on

01:14:02 --> 01:14:05
my team can run experiments 5% faster."

01:14:04 --> 01:14:09
- At the same time, I think it's also one of the closest guarded secrets, what your training data is,

01:14:08 --> 01:14:13
for legal reasons. And so there's also, I think, a lot of work that goes into hiding

01:14:12 --> 01:14:17
what your training data was essentially. Like training the model

01:14:16 --> 01:14:20
to not give away the sources because you have legal reasons.

01:14:19 --> 01:14:23
- The other thing, to be complete, is that some people are trying to train on only

01:14:22 --> 01:14:27
licensed data, whereas Common Crawl is a scrape of the whole internet.

01:14:26 --> 01:14:30
So if I host multiple websites,

01:14:29 --> 01:14:34
I'm happy to have them train language models, but I'm not explicitly

01:14:33 --> 01:14:38
licensing what governs it. And therefore, Common Crawl is

01:14:37 --> 01:14:42
largely unlicensed, which means that your consent really hasn't been

01:14:41 --> 01:14:46
provided for how to use the data. There's another idea where you can train language models only

01:14:45 --> 01:14:50
on data that has been licensed explicitly, so that the kind of governing

01:14:49 --> 01:14:54
contract is provided, and I'm not sure if Apertus is the copyright thing or the

01:14:53 --> 01:14:54
license thing.

01:14:53 --> 01:14:58
I know that the reason that they did it was for an EU compliance thing, where they wanted to make

01:14:57 --> 01:15:06
sure that their model fit one of those checks.

01:15:05 --> 01:15:11
- On that note, there's also the distinction in licensing. Some people

01:15:11 --> 01:15:15
just purchase the license. Let's say they buy an Amazon

01:15:15 --> 01:15:20
Kindle book, or a Manning book, and then use that in training. That is a gray

01:15:19 --> 01:15:24
zone 'cause you paid for the content and you might want to train on it. But then there

01:15:23 --> 01:15:27
are also restrictions where even that shouldn't be allowed. And so that is

01:15:26 --> 01:15:31
where it gets a bit fuzzy. And yeah, I think that is

01:15:30 --> 01:15:35
right now still a hot topic. Big companies like OpenAI

01:15:34 --> 01:15:39
approached private companies for their proprietary data and

01:15:38 --> 01:15:43
private companies, they become more and more, let's say,

01:15:42 --> 01:15:47
protective of their data because they know, "Okay, this is going to be my moat

01:15:46 --> 01:15:52
in a few years." And I do think that's like the interesting question, where

01:15:52 --> 01:15:57
if LLMs become more commoditized, and I think a lot of people learn about LLMs, there

01:15:56 --> 01:16:01
will be a lot more people able to train LLMs. Of course, there are infrastructure challenges.

01:16:00 --> 01:16:04
But if you think of big industries like pharmaceutical

01:16:03 --> 01:16:08
industries, law, finance industries, I do think they, at some point, will

01:16:07 --> 01:16:12
hire people from other frontier labs to build their

01:16:11 --> 01:16:16
in-house models on their proprietary data, which will be then, again, another

01:16:15 --> 01:16:20
unlock with pre-training that is currently not there. Because even if you

01:16:19 --> 01:16:24
wanted to, you can't get that data. You can't get access to clinical trials

01:16:23 --> 01:16:28
most of the time and these types of things. So, I do think scaling, in that sense,

01:16:27 --> 01:16:31
might be still pretty much alive if you also look in

01:16:30 --> 01:16:35
domain-specific applications, because we are still right now, in this year, just looking

01:16:34 --> 01:16:39
at general purpose LLMs on, on ChatGPT, Anthropic and so forth. They are just

01:16:38 --> 01:16:43
general purpose, they're not even, I think, scratching the surface of what an LLM

01:16:42 --> 01:16:47
can do if it is really specifically trained and designed for a specific task.

01:16:47 --> 01:16:51
- I think on the data thing—this is one of the things that happened in 2025, and we totally

01:16:50 --> 01:16:55
forget it—is Anthropic lost in court and owed $1.5 billion to

01:16:54 --> 01:16:59
authors. Anthropic, I think, bought thousands of books and

01:16:58 --> 01:17:03
scanned them and was cleared legally for that because they bought

01:17:02 --> 01:17:07
he books, and that is kind of going through the system. And then the other side, they also

01:17:06 --> 01:17:11
torrented some books, and I think this torrenting was the path where the court

01:17:10 --> 01:17:15
said that they were then culpable to pay these billions of dollars to authors, which is just

01:17:14 --> 01:17:19
such a mind-boggling lawsuit that kind of just came and went. That is

01:17:18 --> 01:17:23
o much money- ... from the VC ecosystem.

01:17:22 --> 01:17:27
- These are court cases that will define the future of human civilization, because it's clear

01:17:26 --> 01:17:30
that data drives a lot of this, and there's this very complicated human

01:17:29 --> 01:17:35
tension of... I mean, you can empathize. You're both authors.

01:17:34 --> 01:17:39
And there's some degree to which, I mean, you put your heart and soul and

01:17:38 --> 01:17:44
your sweat and tears into the writing that you do. It

01:17:43 --> 01:17:48
feels a little bit like theft for somebody to train your data

01:17:47 --> 01:17:49
without giving you credit.

01:17:49 --> 01:17:54
- And there are, like Nathan said, also two layers to it. Someone might buy the book and

01:17:53 --> 01:17:57
then train on it, which could be argued fair or not fair, but then there

01:17:56 --> 01:18:01
are the straight-up companies who use pirated

01:18:00 --> 01:18:05
books where it's not even compensating the author. That is, I think, where people

01:18:04 --> 01:18:07
got a bit angry about it specifically, I would say.

01:18:06 --> 01:18:12
- Yeah, but there has to be some kind of compensation scheme. This is like moving towards-

01:18:11 --> 01:18:14
... towards something like Spotify streaming did-

01:18:13 --> 01:18:16
... originally for music. You know, what does that-

01:18:15 --> 01:18:20
... compensation look like? You have to define those kinds of models. You have to think through all of that.

01:18:19 --> 01:18:24
One other thing I think people are generally curious about, I'd love to get your

01:18:23 --> 01:18:29
thoughts, as LLMs are used more and more. If you look at even arXiv, but

01:18:28 --> 01:18:33
GitHub, more and more of the data is generated by

01:18:32 --> 01:18:39
LLMs. What do you do in that kind of world? How big of a problem is that?

01:18:38 --> 01:18:43
- Largest problem's the infrastructure and systems, but from an AI point

01:18:42 --> 01:18:46
of view, it's kind of inevitable.

01:18:45 --> 01:18:50
- So it's basically LLM-generated data that's curated by humans essentially, right?

01:18:49 --> 01:18:54
- Yes, and I think that a lot of open source contributors are legitimately burning

01:18:53 --> 01:18:57
out. If you have a popular open source repo, somebody's like, "Oh, I want to do

01:18:56 --> 01:19:01
pen source AI. It's good for my career," and they just vibe-

01:19:00 --> 01:19:05
code something and they throw it in. You might get more of this-

01:19:04 --> 01:19:06
I have a-

01:19:05 --> 01:19:06
- than I do.

01:19:05 --> 01:19:10
- Yeah, so I have actually a case study here.

01:19:09 --> 01:19:13
I have a repository called MLxtend that I developed as a

01:19:12 --> 01:19:17
student around 10 years ago, and it is a reasonably

01:19:16 --> 01:19:21
popular library still for certain algorithms, I think especially like frequent

01:19:20 --> 01:19:25
data mining stuff. And there were recently two or

01:19:24 --> 01:19:29
three people who submitted a lot of PRs in a very short amount of time.

01:19:28 --> 01:19:33
I do think LLMs have been involved in submitting these PRs. Me, as the

01:19:32 --> 01:19:37
maintainer, there are two things. First, I'm a bit overwhelmed. I don't have time to

01:19:36 --> 01:19:40
read through it because, especially as an older library, that is not a priority for me.

01:19:40 --> 01:19:45
At the same time, I kind of also appreciate it because I think something

01:19:44 --> 01:19:49
people forget is it's not just using the LLM. There's still a human layer that

01:19:48 --> 01:19:53
verifies something, and that is in a sense also how data

01:19:52 --> 01:19:56
is labeled, right? One of the most expensive things is

01:19:55 --> 01:20:00
getting labeled data for RL from human feedback

01:19:59 --> 01:20:04
phases. And this is kind of like that, where it goes through

01:20:03 --> 01:20:08
phases, and then you actually get higher quality data out of it. And so I don't

01:20:07 --> 01:20:12
mind it in a sense. It can feel overwhelming, but I do think there is also value

01:20:11 --> 01:20:12
in it.

01:20:11 --> 01:20:16
- It feels like there's a fundamental difference between raw LLM-generated

01:20:15 --> 01:20:20
ata and LLM-generated data with a human in the loop

01:20:19 --> 01:20:24
that does some kind of verification, even if that verification is a small percent

01:20:23 --> 01:20:26
of the lines of code.

01:20:25 --> 01:20:29
- I think this goes with anything where

01:20:29 --> 01:20:34
people think also sometimes, "Oh, yeah. I can just use an LLM to learn

01:20:33 --> 01:20:38
about XYZ," which is true. You can, but there might be a person who

01:20:37 --> 01:20:42
is an expert who might have used an LLM to write specific code.

01:20:41 --> 01:20:46
There is kind of like this human work that went into it to make it nice,

01:20:45 --> 01:20:50
throwing out the not-so-nice parts to kind of pre-digest

01:20:49 --> 01:20:54
it for you, and that saves you time. I think that's the

01:20:53 --> 01:20:58
value-add, where you have someone filtering things or

01:20:57 --> 01:21:01
even using the LLMs correctly. This is still labor that you

01:21:01 --> 01:21:05
get for free. For example, if you read a Substack article,

01:21:04 --> 01:21:09
I could maybe ask an LLM to give me opinions on that, but

01:21:08 --> 01:21:13
I wouldn't even know what to ask. And I think there is still value in

01:21:12 --> 01:21:17
reading that article compared to me going to the LLM because you are the

01:21:16 --> 01:21:21
xpert. You select what knowledge is actually spot on, should be

01:21:20 --> 01:21:24
included, and you give me this very... this

01:21:23 --> 01:21:28
executive summary. And this is a huge value-add because now I

01:21:27 --> 01:21:32
don't have to waste three to five hours to go through this

01:21:31 --> 01:21:36
myself, maybe get some incorrect information and so on. And so I think

01:21:35 --> 01:21:37
that's also where the future

01:21:37 --> 01:21:44
still is for writers even though there are LLMs that... Can kind of save you time.

01:21:43 --> 01:21:48
- It's kinda fascinating to watch. I'm sure you guys do this,

01:21:47 --> 01:21:52
but for me, I look at the difference between a summary

01:21:52 --> 01:21:57
and the original content. Even if it's a page-long summary of a

01:21:56 --> 01:22:01
page-long content, it's interesting to see

01:22:00 --> 01:22:05
how the LLM-based summary takes the edge off. What

01:22:04 --> 01:22:08
is the signal it removes from the thing?

01:22:07 --> 01:22:10
- The voice is what I talk about a lot.

01:22:09 --> 01:22:14
- Voice? Well, voice... I would love to hear what you mean by voice, but

01:22:13 --> 01:22:17
sometimes there's literally insights.

01:22:16 --> 01:22:20
By removing an insight, you're changing the meaning of

01:22:20 --> 01:22:25
the thing. So I'm continuously disappointed how bad LLMs are

01:22:25 --> 01:22:30
at really getting to the core insights, which is what a

01:22:29 --> 01:22:38
great summary does. Yet even if I have these extremely elaborate prompts where I'm

01:22:37 --> 01:22:42
really trying to dig for the insights, it's still not

01:22:41 --> 01:22:46
quite there, which... I mean, that's a whole deep philosophical

01:22:45 --> 01:22:50
question about what is human knowledge and wisdom and what does it mean to be

01:22:49 --> 01:22:53
insightful and so on. But when you talk about the voice, what do you mean?

01:22:52 --> 01:22:56
- When I write, I think a lot of what I'm trying to do is

01:22:55 --> 01:23:00
take what you think as a researcher, which is very raw. A

01:22:59 --> 01:23:04
researcher is trying to encapsulate an idea at the frontier of their

01:23:03 --> 01:23:08
understanding and they're trying to put what is a feeling into

01:23:07 --> 01:23:12
words. I try to do this in my writing, which

01:23:11 --> 01:23:16
makes it come across as raw but also high-information in a way that

01:23:15 --> 01:23:20
some people will get it and some won't. And that's the nature of research.

01:23:19 --> 01:23:24
And language models don't do this well. They're all trained

01:23:23 --> 01:23:27
with Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback, which takes feedback from many

01:23:26 --> 01:23:31
people and averages how the model behaves from this.

01:23:30 --> 01:23:35
And I think it's going to be hard for a model to

01:23:34 --> 01:23:39
be very incisive when there's that sort of filter. This is a

01:23:40 --> 01:23:45
wonderful fundamental problem for researchers in RLHF. This

01:23:44 --> 01:23:49
provides so much utility in making the models better, but also

01:23:48 --> 01:23:53
the problem formulation is kind of... there's this

01:23:52 --> 01:23:56
knot in it that you can't get past. These language

01:23:56 --> 01:24:00
models don't have this prior in their deep expression they're trying to get

01:23:59 --> 01:24:04
at. I don't think it's impossible. There are stories of models that

01:24:03 --> 01:24:08
really shock people. Like, I think of... I would love to have

01:24:07 --> 01:24:12
tried Bing Sydney. Did that have more voice? Because

01:24:11 --> 01:24:15
it would so often go off the rails, which is historically

01:24:14 --> 01:24:19
obviously a scary way—like telling a reporter to leave his wife—is a crazy model

01:24:18 --> 01:24:23
to potentially put in general adoption. But that's kind of like

01:24:23 --> 01:24:29
a trade-off; is this RLHF process in some ways adding limitations?

01:24:28 --> 01:24:33
- That's a terrifying place to be as one of these frontier labs and companies,

01:24:33 --> 01:24:36
because millions of people are using them.

01:24:35 --> 01:24:40
- There was a lot of backlash last year with GPT-4o getting

01:24:39 --> 01:24:44
removed, and I've personally never used the model, but I've talked to people at

01:24:43 --> 01:24:48
OpenAI where they get emails from

01:24:47 --> 01:24:52
users that might be detecting subtle differences in the deployments

01:24:51 --> 01:24:56
in the middle of the night. And they email them like, "My friend is different."

01:24:55 --> 01:25:00
And they find these employees' emails and send them things because they are so

01:24:59 --> 01:25:01
attached to this

01:25:01 --> 01:25:06
et of model weights and configuration that is deployed to the users.

01:25:05 --> 01:25:09
We see this with TikTok. You open it... I don't use TikTok,

01:25:08 --> 01:25:13
but supposedly in five minutes the algorithm gets you. It's locked in.

01:25:12 --> 01:25:17
And those are language models doing recommendations. I think

01:25:16 --> 01:25:21
there are ways you can do this. Within five minutes of chatting with it,

01:25:20 --> 01:25:23
the model just gets you. And that is something that

01:25:23 --> 01:25:29
people aren't really ready for. Like, don't give that to kids.

01:25:28 --> 01:25:31
At least until we know what's happening.

01:25:30 --> 01:25:35
- But there's also going to be this mechanism... What's going to happen

01:25:34 --> 01:25:36
with these LLMs as they're used more and more...

01:25:36 --> 01:25:40
Unfortunately, the nature of the human condition is such that people commit suicide.

01:25:39 --> 01:25:44
And so what journalists will do is they will report extensively

01:25:43 --> 01:25:48
on the people who commit suicide. And they would very likely

01:25:47 --> 01:25:51
link it to the LLMs because they have that data about the conversations.

01:25:50 --> 01:25:55
If you're really struggling in your life, if you're depressed,

01:25:54 --> 01:25:59
if you're thinking about suicide, you're going to probably talk to LLMs about it.

01:25:58 --> 01:26:03
And so what journalists will do is say, "Well, the suicide was committed

01:26:02 --> 01:26:07
because of the LLM." And that's going to lead to the companies,

01:26:06 --> 01:26:10
because of legal issues and so on,

01:26:10 --> 01:26:15
more and more taking the edge off of the LLM. So it's going to be

01:26:14 --> 01:26:19
as generic as possible. It's so difficult to operate in this space

01:26:18 --> 01:26:24
because you don't want an LLM to cause harm to humans at that level, but also

01:26:24 --> 01:26:28
this is the nature of the human experience,

01:26:27 --> 01:26:32
is to have a rich conversation, a fulfilling conversation,

01:26:31 --> 01:26:36
one that challenges you from which you grow. You need that edge.

01:26:35 --> 01:26:43
And that's something extremely difficult for AI researchers on the RLHF front to

01:26:42 --> 01:26:48
actually have to solve, because you're dealing with the human condition.

01:26:47 --> 01:26:51
- A lot of researchers at these companies are so well-motivated,

01:26:50 --> 01:26:57
and definitely Anthropic and OpenAI culturally want to do good for the world.

01:26:56 --> 01:27:01
And it's such a—I'm like, "Ooh, I don't wanna work on this,"

01:27:00 --> 01:27:05
because on the one hand, a lot of people see AI as a health ally,

01:27:04 --> 01:27:09
as somebody they can talk to about their health confidentially,

01:27:08 --> 01:27:11
but then it bleeds all the way into this,

01:27:11 --> 01:27:16
like talking about mental health, where it's

01:27:16 --> 01:27:20
heartbreaking that this will be the thing where somebody goes over the edge,

01:27:19 --> 01:27:24
but other people might be saved. And I'm like, "I don't..."

01:27:23 --> 01:27:28
As a researcher, it's like, I don't want to train image generation models and release

01:27:27 --> 01:27:32
them openly because I don't want to enable somebody to have a tool on their

01:27:31 --> 01:27:35
laptop that can harm other people.

01:27:34 --> 01:27:38
I don't have the infrastructure in my company to do that safely. But

01:27:37 --> 01:27:42
here's a lot of areas like this where it just needs people that will

01:27:42 --> 01:27:48
approach it with complexity and conviction. It's just such a hard problem.

01:27:47 --> 01:27:52
- But also, we as a society, as users of these technologies, need to make sure that we're

01:27:51 --> 01:27:56
having the complicated conversation about it versus just fearmongering—

01:27:55 --> 01:28:00
that Big Tech is causing harm to humans or stealing your data.

01:27:59 --> 01:28:04
It's more complicated than that. And you're right.

01:28:03 --> 01:28:07
There's a very large number of people inside these companies, many of whom I know,

01:28:07 --> 01:28:11
who deeply care about helping people. They are

01:28:10 --> 01:28:15
considering the full human experience of people from across the world, not just Silicon

01:28:14 --> 01:28:19
Valley. People across the United States and the world, what their needs are.

01:28:18 --> 01:28:22
It's really difficult to design this one system

01:28:22 --> 01:28:27
that is able to help all these different kinds of people across different age groups,

01:28:26 --> 01:28:32
cultures, and mental states.

01:28:31 --> 01:28:36
- I wish that the timing of AI was different relative to the relationship

01:28:35 --> 01:28:39
of Big Tech to the average person. Big Tech's reputation was so low,

01:28:38 --> 01:28:43
and with how AI is so expensive, it's inevitably going to be a Big Tech thing.

01:28:42 --> 01:28:47
It takes so many resources, and people say the US is,

01:28:46 --> 01:28:51
"betting the economy on AI" with this build-out. To have these be intertwined

01:28:50 --> 01:28:55
at the same time makes for such a hard communication environment.

01:28:54 --> 01:28:59
It would be good for me to go talk to more people in the world who hate Big Tech

01:28:58 --> 01:29:03
and see AI as a continuation of this.

01:29:02 --> 01:29:07
- And one of the things you recommend, one of the antidotes that

01:29:06 --> 01:29:14
you talk about, is to find agency in this system. As opposed to

01:29:13 --> 01:29:18
sitting back in a powerless way and consuming the AI slop

01:29:17 --> 01:29:25
as it rapidly takes over the internet. Find agency by using AI to build stuff,

01:29:24 --> 01:29:29
build apps, build... One, that actually helps you build intuition,

01:29:28 --> 01:29:33
but two, it's empowering because you can understand how it works,

01:29:32 --> 01:29:37
what the weaknesses are. It gives your voice

01:29:36 --> 01:29:41
power to say, "This is a bad use of the technology, and this is a good use."

01:29:40 --> 01:29:45
And you're more plugged into the system then,

01:29:44 --> 01:29:49
so you can understand it better and you can steer it better as a consumer.

01:29:48 --> 01:29:53
- I think that's a good point you brought up about agency. Instead of ignoring it and saying,

01:29:52 --> 01:29:57
"Okay, I'm not going to use it," I think it's probably long-term

01:29:56 --> 01:30:00
healthier to say, "Okay, it's out there. I can't put it back."

01:30:00 --> 01:30:05
when they came out. How do I make best use of it, and

01:30:04 --> 01:30:08
how does it help me to up-level myself? The one thing I worry about here, though,

01:30:08 --> 01:30:13
is, if you just fully use it for something you love to do, the thing you love to do

01:30:12 --> 01:30:17
is no longer there. And that could potentially, I feel, lead to burnout.

01:30:16 --> 01:30:20
For example, if I use an LLM to do all my coding for me,

01:30:19 --> 01:30:24
now there's no coding. I'm just managing something that is coding for me.

01:30:23 --> 01:30:28
Two years later, let's say, if I just do that eight hours a day,

01:30:27 --> 01:30:36
having something code for me, do I feel fulfilled still?

01:30:36 --> 01:30:41
Is this hurting me in terms of being excited about my job, excited about what I'm doing?

01:30:40 --> 01:30:44
Am I still proud to build something?

01:30:43 --> 01:30:48
- On that topic of enjoyment, it's quite interesting. We should just throw this in there,

01:30:47 --> 01:30:51
that there's this recent survey of about

01:30:50 --> 01:30:56
791 professional developers, meaning 10-plus years of experience.

01:30:55 --> 01:31:02
- That's a long time. As a junior developer?

01:31:01 --> 01:31:06
- Yeah, in this day and age. So, there's also many fronts

01:31:05 --> 01:31:10
that are surprising. They break it down

01:31:09 --> 01:31:13
by junior and senior developers. But, I mean, it just shows

01:31:12 --> 01:31:22
that both junior and senior developers use AI-generated code in code they ship.

01:31:22 --> 01:31:26
So this is not just for fun or intermediate learning things.

01:31:26 --> 01:31:31
This is code they ship. 25%—like, most of them

01:31:30 --> 01:31:34
use around 50% or more. And what's interesting is,

01:31:33 --> 01:31:38
for the category of over 50% of your code that you ship

01:31:37 --> 01:31:42
is AI-generated, senior developers are much more likely

01:31:41 --> 01:31:47
to do so. But you don't want AI to take away the thing you love.

01:31:46 --> 01:31:50
I think this speaks to my experience, these results I'm about to say.

01:31:49 --> 01:31:54
Together, about 80% of people find it either somewhat

01:31:53 --> 01:32:00
more enjoyable or significantly more enjoyable to use AI as part of the work.

01:31:59 --> 01:32:03
- I think it depends on the task. From my personal usage, for example,

01:32:03 --> 01:32:08
I have a website where I sometimes tweak things.

01:32:07 --> 01:32:12
I personally don't enjoy this. So in that sense,

01:32:11 --> 01:32:16
if the AI can help me to implement something on my website, I'm all for it.

01:32:15 --> 01:32:20
It's great. But then, at the same time, when I solve a complex problem—

01:32:19 --> 01:32:24
well, if there's a bug, and I hunt this bug,

01:32:23 --> 01:32:28
and I find it, it's the best feeling in the world. You feel great.

01:32:27 --> 01:32:32
But now, if you don't even think about the bug,

01:32:31 --> 01:32:35
you just go directly to the LLM, well, you never have this kind of feeling, right?

01:32:35 --> 01:32:39
But then there could be the middle ground where,

01:32:38 --> 01:32:43
well, you try yourself, you can't find it, you use the LLM,

01:32:42 --> 01:32:47
and then you don't get frustrated because it helps you and you move on to something that you enjoy.

01:32:46 --> 01:32:51
And so, looking at these statistics, I think what is not factored in

01:32:50 --> 01:32:55
is that it's averaging over all the different scenarios.

01:32:54 --> 01:32:59
We don't know if it's for the core task

01:32:58 --> 01:33:03
or if it's for something mundane that people would not have enjoyed otherwise.

01:33:02 --> 01:33:07
So, in a sense, AI is really great for doing mundane things that take a lot of work.

01:33:06 --> 01:33:11
For example, my wife the other day—she has a podcast

01:33:10 --> 01:33:18
for book discussions, a book club, and she was transferring the show notes from

01:33:17 --> 01:33:22
Spotify to YouTube, and then the links somehow broke.

01:33:21 --> 01:33:26
And she had in some episodes, because it is so many books, like 100 links,

01:33:25 --> 01:33:30
and it would have been really painful to go in there and fix each link manually.

01:33:29 --> 01:33:34
So I suggested, "Hey, let's try ChatGPT." We copied the text into ChatGPT,

01:33:33 --> 01:33:38
and it fixed them. Instead of two hours

01:33:37 --> 01:33:42
going from link to link fixing that, it made that type of

01:33:41 --> 01:33:46
work much more seamless. I think everyone has a

01:33:45 --> 01:33:50
use case where AI is useful for something that would be

01:33:49 --> 01:33:51
really boring, really mundane.

01:33:51 --> 01:33:59
- For me personally, since we're talking about coding, and you mentioned debugging...

01:33:58 --> 01:34:03
the source of enjoyment for me, more on the Cursor side than Claude Code,

01:34:02 --> 01:34:10
is that I have a friend, I have a pair programmer. It's less

01:34:09 --> 01:34:16
lonely. You made debugging sound like this great joy.

01:34:15 --> 01:34:19
No, I would say debugging is like a drink of

01:34:18 --> 01:34:22
water after you've been going through a desert

01:34:21 --> 01:34:26
for days. You skip the whole desert part

01:34:25 --> 01:34:30
where you're suffering. Sometimes it's nice to have

01:34:29 --> 01:34:34
a friend who can't really find the bug, but can give you some intuition about

01:34:33 --> 01:34:38
he code, and together you're

01:34:37 --> 01:34:42
going through the desert and finding that drink of water. At least

01:34:41 --> 01:34:46
for me, maybe it speaks to the loneliness of the programming experience.

01:34:45 --> 01:34:49
That is a source of joy.

01:34:48 --> 01:34:53
- It's maybe also related to delayed gratification. I'm a person who,

01:34:52 --> 01:34:57
even as a kid, I liked the idea of Christmas

01:34:56 --> 01:35:00
presents—having them, getting them—better than actually

01:35:00 --> 01:35:05
receiving the presents. I would look forward to the day I get the

01:35:04 --> 01:35:08
presents, but then it's over and I'm disappointed. And maybe it's the same

01:35:07 --> 01:35:12
with food. I think food tastes better when you're really

01:35:11 --> 01:35:19
hungry. You're right, with debugging, it is not always great. It's often

01:35:18 --> 01:35:23
frustrating, but then if you can solve it, then it's

01:35:22 --> 01:35:27
great. But there's a sweet Goldilocks zone; if it's too hard, then

01:35:26 --> 01:35:31
it's just wasting your time. But I think

01:35:30 --> 01:35:35
another challenge is how will people learn? We

01:35:34 --> 01:35:38
looked at the chart and saw that more senior

01:35:37 --> 01:35:42
developers are shipping more AI-generated code than the junior ones.

01:35:42 --> 01:35:46
It's very interesting, because intuitively you would think it's the junior developers

01:35:45 --> 01:35:50
because they don't know how to do the thing yet,

01:35:49 --> 01:35:54
and so they use AI to do that thing. It could mean the AI is

01:35:53 --> 01:35:58
not good enough yet to solve that task, but it could also mean

01:35:57 --> 01:36:00
experts are more effective at using it. They know

01:36:00 --> 01:36:06
how to use it better, review the code, and then they trust the code more.

01:36:06 --> 01:36:10
One issue for society in the future will be: how do you become an

01:36:09 --> 01:36:18
expert if you never try to do the thing yourself? One way I always learned

01:36:17 --> 01:36:22
is by trying things myself. If you look at math textbooks and the

01:36:21 --> 01:36:26
solutions, you learn something, but you learn actually

01:36:25 --> 01:36:30
better if you try first. Then you appreciate the solution differently

01:36:29 --> 01:36:33
because you know how to put it into your mental framework. If

01:36:33 --> 01:36:38
LLMs are here all the time, would you actually go to the length

01:36:37 --> 01:36:43
of struggling? Would you be willing to struggle? Struggle is not nice, right? But

01:36:42 --> 01:36:47
if you use the LLM to do everything, at some point you will never really take

01:36:46 --> 01:36:48
the next step, and then

01:36:48 --> 01:36:53
you will maybe not get that unlock that you would get as an expert using an

01:36:52 --> 01:36:57
LLM. So, I think there's like a Goldilocks sweet

01:36:56 --> 01:37:00
spot where maybe the trick here is you make dedicated

01:36:59 --> 01:37:04
offline time where you study two hours a day, and the rest of the day use

01:37:03 --> 01:37:08
LLMs. But I think it's important also for people to still invest in

01:37:07 --> 01:37:11
themselves, in my opinion, to not just LLM everything.

01:37:10 --> 01:37:17
- Yeah, as a civilization, we each individually have to find that Goldilocks zone.

01:37:16 --> 01:37:21
And in the programming context as developers. Now, we've had this fascinating

01:37:20 --> 01:37:25
conversation that started with pre-training and mid-training.

01:37:24 --> 01:37:28
Let's get to post-training. A lot of fun stuff in

01:37:27 --> 01:37:32
post-training. So, what are some of the interesting ideas in post-training?

01:37:31 --> 01:37:36
- The biggest one from 2025 is learning this

01:37:35 --> 01:37:40
reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. You can scale up the training

01:37:39 --> 01:37:43
there, which means doing a lot of this kind of iterative generate-grade

01:37:43 --> 01:37:48
loop, and that lets the models learn both interesting

01:37:47 --> 01:37:52
behaviors on the tool use and software side. This could be searching, running

01:37:51 --> 01:37:55
commands on their own and seeing the outputs, and then also that training

01:37:55 --> 01:37:59
enables this inference time scaling very nicely. And it just

01:37:58 --> 01:38:03
urned out that this paradigm was very nicely linked, where this

01:38:02 --> 01:38:07
kind of RL training enables inference time scaling. But inference time scaling could have

01:38:06 --> 01:38:11
been found in different ways. So, it was kind of this perfect storm where the models change a lot,

01:38:10 --> 01:38:17
and the way that they're trained is a major factor in doing so. And this has changed

01:38:17 --> 01:38:21
how people approach post-training dramatically.

01:38:20 --> 01:38:26
- Can you describe RLVR, popularized by DeepSeek R1? Can you describe how it works?

01:38:25 --> 01:38:30
- Yeah. Fun fact: I was on the team that came up with the term

01:38:29 --> 01:38:34
RLVR, which is from our Tulu 3 work before DeepSeek. We

01:38:33 --> 01:38:38
don't take a lot of credit for being the people to popularize the scaling

01:38:37 --> 01:38:42
RL, but as fun as what academics get, as an

01:38:41 --> 01:38:44
aside, is the ability to name and influence

01:38:43 --> 01:38:48
the discourse, because the closed labs can only say so much. That

01:38:47 --> 01:38:52
one of the things you can do as an academic is you might not have the compute to train the

01:38:51 --> 01:38:56
model, but you can frame things in a way that ends up being

01:38:55 --> 01:39:00
described as a community coming together around this RLVR term, which

01:38:59 --> 01:39:04
is very fun. And then DeepSeek are the people that did the training breakthrough,

01:39:03 --> 01:39:07
which is, they scaled the reinforcement learning. You

01:39:07 --> 01:39:12
have the model generate answers and then grade the completion if it

01:39:11 --> 01:39:15
was right, and then that accuracy is your

01:39:14 --> 01:39:19
eward for reinforcement learning. So reinforcement learning is

01:39:18 --> 01:39:23
classically an agent that acts in an environment, and the environment gives

01:39:22 --> 01:39:26
it a state and a reward back, and you try to maximize this

01:39:25 --> 01:39:30
reward. In the case of language models, the reward is normally

01:39:29 --> 01:39:33
accuracy on a set of verifiable tasks, whether it's math

01:39:32 --> 01:39:38
problems or coding tasks. And it starts to get blurry with things like

01:39:38 --> 01:39:42
factual domains. That is also, in some ways, verifiable,

01:39:41 --> 01:39:46
or constraints on your instruction, like respond only with

01:39:45 --> 01:39:50
words that start with A." All of these things are verifiable in

01:39:49 --> 01:39:54
some way, and the core idea of this is you find a

01:39:53 --> 01:39:58
lot more of these problems that are verifiable and you let the model try it

01:39:57 --> 01:40:01
many times while taking these RL steps, these

01:40:01 --> 01:40:06
RL gradient updates. The infrastructure evolved from

01:40:05 --> 01:40:10
reinforcement learning from human feedback, where in that era the score

01:40:09 --> 01:40:13
they were trying to optimize was a learned reward model of human

01:40:12 --> 01:40:17
preferences. So you kind of changed the problem domains and that

01:40:16 --> 01:40:21
let the optimization go on to much bigger scales, which kind of

01:40:20 --> 01:40:25
kickstarted a major change in what the models can do and how people use them.

01:40:24 --> 01:40:29
- What kind of domains is RLVR amenable to?

01:40:28 --> 01:40:32
- Math and code are the famous ones, and then there's a lot of

01:40:32 --> 01:40:37
work on what is called the rubrics, which is related to a word people might

01:40:36 --> 01:40:41
have heard: LLM-as-a-judge. For each problem,

01:40:40 --> 01:40:45
I'll have a set of problems in my dataset. I will then have an LLM

01:40:44 --> 01:40:49
and ask it, "What would a good answer to this problem look

01:40:48 --> 01:40:53
like?" And then you could try the problem over and over again and

01:40:52 --> 01:40:56
assign a score based on this rubric. That's not necessarily verifiable like

01:40:55 --> 01:41:00
math and code domains, but this rubrics idea and other

01:40:59 --> 01:41:04
scientific problems that might be a little bit more vague is where

01:41:03 --> 01:41:08
the attention is, where they're trying to push this set of methods into

01:41:07 --> 01:41:12
these kind of more open-ended domains so the models can learn a lot more.

01:41:11 --> 01:41:14
- I think that's called reinforcement learning with AI feedback, right?

01:41:14 --> 01:41:18
- That's the older term for it coined in Anthropic's Constitutional

01:41:17 --> 01:41:22
AI paper. It's like a lot of these things come in cycles.

01:41:21 --> 01:41:26
- Also, just one step back for RLVR. I think the

01:41:25 --> 01:41:30
interesting thing here is that you ask the LLM a, let's say,

01:41:29 --> 01:41:34
math question, and then you know the correct answer, and you let the LLM,

01:41:33 --> 01:41:38
as you said, figure it out. How it does it—you don't constrain it

01:41:37 --> 01:41:42
much. There are some constraints like "use the same language, don't switch

01:41:41 --> 01:41:45
between Spanish and English." But let's say you're pretty much hands-off.

01:41:45 --> 01:41:49
You only give the question and the answer, and then the LLM has

01:41:48 --> 01:41:53
the task to arrive at the right answer, but the

01:41:52 --> 01:41:57
beautiful thing here is what happens in practice: the LLM will

01:41:56 --> 01:42:00
do a step-by-step description, like as a student or as

01:41:59 --> 01:42:04
a mathematician would derive the solution. It will use those steps,

01:42:03 --> 01:42:08
and that helps the model to improve its own accuracy.

01:42:07 --> 01:42:12
And then, like you said, the inference scaling.

01:42:11 --> 01:42:16
Inference scaling loosely means spending more compute

01:42:15 --> 01:42:20
during inference, and here the inference scaling is

01:42:20 --> 01:42:25
that the model would use more tokens. In the DeepSeek R1 paper, they

01:42:24 --> 01:42:29
showed the longer they train the model, the longer the responses are.

01:42:28 --> 01:42:33
They grow over time. They use more tokens, so it becomes more expensive.

01:42:32 --> 01:42:37
It becomes expensive for simple tasks, but these explanations help accuracy.

01:42:36 --> 01:42:41
There are also papers showing what the model explains

01:42:40 --> 01:42:45
does not necessarily have to be correct or maybe it's unrelated to the

01:42:44 --> 01:42:48
answer, but for some reason, it still helps the model that it

01:42:47 --> 01:42:52
is explaining. And I think it's also—again, I don't want to

01:42:51 --> 01:42:56
anthropomorphize these LLMs, but it's kind of like how we humans operate.

01:42:55 --> 01:43:00
If there's a complex math problem in a math class,

01:42:59 --> 01:43:03
you usually have a note paper and you do it step by step. You cross out

01:43:02 --> 01:43:07
hings. And the model also self-corrects, and that was, I think, the

01:43:06 --> 01:43:11
aha moment in the DeepSeek R1 paper. They called it the aha moment because the model itself

01:43:10 --> 01:43:15
recognized it made a mistake and then said, "Ah, I did something wrong, let me try again."

01:43:14 --> 01:43:19
And I think that's just so cool that this falls

01:43:18 --> 01:43:23
out of just giving it the correct answer and having it figure out how to do

01:43:22 --> 01:43:27
it, that it kind of does in a sense what a human would do.

01:43:26 --> 01:43:31
Although LLMs don't think like humans, it's kind of like an interesting coincidence

01:43:30 --> 01:43:35
and it... And the other nice side effect is it's great for

01:43:34 --> 01:43:39
us humans often to see these steps. It builds trust, but also we

01:43:38 --> 01:43:41
us humans to see these steps. It builds trust, but also we learn and can double check things.

01:43:40 --> 01:43:42
- There's a lot in here. I think

01:43:41 --> 01:43:45
some of the debate... There's been a lot of debate this year on if the language models

01:43:44 --> 01:43:49
like these... I think the aha moments are kind of fake because in pre-training

01:43:49 --> 01:43:52
you essentially have seen the whole internet.

01:43:51 --> 01:43:56
so you have definitely seen people explaining their work, even verbally, like

01:43:55 --> 01:44:00
a transcript of a math lecture. "You try this, oh, I messed this up." And what RLVR

01:43:59 --> 01:44:03
is very good at doing is amplifying

01:44:02 --> 01:44:07
these behaviors because they're very useful in enabling the model to think longer and to

01:44:06 --> 01:44:11
check its work. And I agree that it is very beautiful that this

01:44:10 --> 01:44:15
training kind of... The model learns to amplify this in a way that is so useful for the

01:44:14 --> 01:44:17
final answers being better.

01:44:16 --> 01:44:21
- I can give you also a hands-on example. I was training the Qwen 3 base model with

01:44:20 --> 01:44:29
RLVR on MATH-500. The base model had an accuracy of about 15%. Just 50 steps,

01:44:28 --> 01:44:33
like in a few minutes with RLVR, the model went from

01:44:32 --> 01:44:36
15% to 50% accuracy. And the model... You can't tell me

01:44:35 --> 01:44:39
it's learning anything fundamentally about math in

01:44:38 --> 01:44:43
- The Qwen example is weird because there've been two papers this year, one of which I was on, about

01:44:42 --> 01:44:45
data contamination in Qwen

01:44:44 --> 01:44:49
and specifically that they train on a lot of this special mid-training phase

01:44:48 --> 01:44:51
that we should take a minute on, because it's weird

01:44:50 --> 01:44:54
because they train on problems that are almost identical to MATH.

01:44:53 --> 01:44:58
- Exactly. And so you can see that basically the RL,

01:44:57 --> 01:45:02
it's not teaching the model any new knowledge about math. You can't do that in 50 steps.

01:45:01 --> 01:45:04
So the knowledge is already there, in the pre-training, you're just unlocking it.

01:45:03 --> 01:45:08
- I still disagree with the premise because there's a lot of weird complexities that you

01:45:07 --> 01:45:10
can't prove because

01:45:10 --> 01:45:15
one of the things that points to weirdness is that if you take the Qwen 3 so-called base

01:45:14 --> 01:45:19
model and you... You could Google like "math

01:45:18 --> 01:45:23
dataset, Hugging Face", and you could take a problem and what you do if you put it

01:45:22 --> 01:45:27
into Qwen 3 base... All these math problems have words, so it'd be like "Alice

01:45:26 --> 01:45:31
has five apples and takes one... and gives three to whoever," and there are these

01:45:30 --> 01:45:34
word problems. With these Qwen-based models, why people are suspicious of them

01:45:34 --> 01:45:37
is if you change the numbers but keep the words-

01:45:36 --> 01:45:41
Qwen will produce, without tools, will produce a

01:45:40 --> 01:45:44
very high accuracy decimal representation

01:45:43 --> 01:45:48
of the answer, which means there's some... At some time, it was shown

01:45:47 --> 01:45:52
problems that were almost identical to the test set, and it was using

01:45:51 --> 01:45:56
tools to get a very high precision answer, but a language model without tools

01:45:55 --> 01:46:00
will never actually have this. So it's kind of been this

01:45:59 --> 01:46:04
big debate in the research community: how much of these reinforcement learning

01:46:03 --> 01:46:07
papers that are training on Qwen and measuring specifically on this

01:46:06 --> 01:46:11
math benchmark, where there's been multiple papers talking about contamination, is like,

01:46:10 --> 01:46:15
how much can you believe them? And I think this is what caused the reputation of RLVR

01:46:14 --> 01:46:19
being about formatting, because you can get these gains so quickly,

01:46:18 --> 01:46:23
therefore it must already be in the model. But there's a lot of complexity here that we...

01:46:22 --> 01:46:27
It's not really like controlled experimentation, so we don't really know.

01:46:26 --> 01:46:31
- But if it weren't true, I would say distillation wouldn't work, right?

01:46:30 --> 01:46:35
I mean, distillation can work to some extent, but the thing is that

01:46:34 --> 01:46:39
is, I think, the biggest problem, and I research this contamination because we don't know what's in the

01:46:38 --> 01:46:43
data. Unless you have a new dataset, it is really impossible. And the

01:46:42 --> 01:46:47
same, you mentioned the math dataset, where you have a question and

01:46:46 --> 01:46:51
then answer and an explanation is given, but then also even something simpler

01:46:50 --> 01:46:55
like MMLU, which is a multiple-choice benchmark. If you just

01:46:54 --> 01:46:58
change the format slightly, like, I don't know, if you

01:46:58 --> 01:47:03
se a dot instead of a parenthesis or something like that, the

01:47:02 --> 01:47:05
model accuracy will vastly differ.

01:47:04 --> 01:47:10
- I think that that could be like a model issue rather than a general issue.

01:47:09 --> 01:47:14
- It's not even malicious by the developers of the LLM, like, "Hey, we want to cheat at that benchmark."

01:47:13 --> 01:47:18
It has seen something at some point. I think the only fair way to evaluate an LLM

01:47:17 --> 01:47:23
is to have a new benchmark that is after the cutoff date when the LLM was deployed.

01:47:22 --> 01:47:27
- Can we lay out what would be the recipe of all

01:47:26 --> 01:47:31
the things that go into post-training? And you mentioned RLVR was

01:47:30 --> 01:47:33
a really exciting, effective thing.

01:47:32 --> 01:47:37
Maybe we should elaborate. RLHF still has a really important

01:47:36 --> 01:47:41
component to play. What kind of other ideas are there on post-training?

01:47:40 --> 01:47:43
- I think you can kind of take this in order. I think you could

01:47:43 --> 01:47:47
view it as what made o1, which is this first reasoning model,

01:47:46 --> 01:47:51
possible, or what will the latest model be? And they

01:47:50 --> 01:47:55
actually... You're going to have similar interventions at these,

01:47:54 --> 01:47:59
where you start with mid-training, and the thing that is

01:47:58 --> 01:48:03
rumored to enable o1 and similar models is really careful data

01:48:02 --> 01:48:07
curation, where you're providing a broad set of what is

01:48:06 --> 01:48:10
called reasoning traces, which is just the model generating

01:48:09 --> 01:48:14
words in a forward process that is reflecting, like

01:48:13 --> 01:48:18
breaking down a problem into intermediate steps and trying to solve them. So at mid-training,

01:48:17 --> 01:48:19
you need to have

01:48:19 --> 01:48:23
data that is similar to this to make it so that when you move into post-training,

01:48:23 --> 01:48:28
primarily with these verifiable rewards, it can learn.

01:48:27 --> 01:48:31
And then what is happening today is you're figuring

01:48:30 --> 01:48:35
out which problems to give the model and how

01:48:34 --> 01:48:39
long you can train it for and how much inference you can

01:48:38 --> 01:48:43
enable the model to use when solving these verifiable problems. So as

01:48:42 --> 01:48:45
models get better, certain problems

01:48:45 --> 01:48:50
are no longer... The model will solve them 100% of the time, and

01:48:49 --> 01:48:53
therefore there's very little signal in this. If we look at the GRPO

01:48:52 --> 01:48:57
equation, this one is famous for this because essentially the

01:48:56 --> 01:49:01
reward given to the agent is based on how good

01:49:00 --> 01:49:05
a given action—an action is a completion—is relative to the other

01:49:04 --> 01:49:09
answers to that same problem. So if all the problems get the same answer, there's no signal in these

01:49:08 --> 01:49:13
types of algorithms. So what they're doing is they're finding harder problems, which is

01:49:12 --> 01:49:16
why you hear about things like scientific domains, where it's so

01:49:16 --> 01:49:20
hard to get anything right. If you have a lab or something, it just

01:49:19 --> 01:49:24
generates so many tokens or much harder software problems. So the

01:49:23 --> 01:49:28
frontier models are all pushing into these harder domains when they can train on more

01:49:27 --> 01:49:32
problems and the model will learn more skills at once. The RLHF

01:49:31 --> 01:49:36
link to this is that RLHF has been and still is kind of like the

01:49:35 --> 01:49:39
finishing touch on the models, where it makes the models more useful

01:49:38 --> 01:49:43
by improving the organization or style or tone. There are different things that

01:49:42 --> 01:49:47
resonate with different audiences, like some people like a really quirky model and RLHF could

01:49:46 --> 01:49:51
be good at enabling that personality, and some people hate

01:49:50 --> 01:49:55
this markdown bulleted list thing that the models do,

01:49:54 --> 01:49:59
but it's actually really good for quickly parsing information. In RLHF, this

01:49:58 --> 01:50:03
human feedback stage is really great for putting

01:50:02 --> 01:50:07
this into the model at the end of the day. It's what made ChatGPT

01:50:06 --> 01:50:10
so magical for people. And that use has actually remained fairly

01:50:09 --> 01:50:14
stable. This formatting can also help

01:50:13 --> 01:50:18
the models get better at math problems, for example. So it's

01:50:17 --> 01:50:22
like the border between style and formatting, and like

01:50:21 --> 01:50:25
the method that you use to answer a problem is

01:50:24 --> 01:50:29
actually all very closely linked in terms of when

01:50:28 --> 01:50:33
you're training these models, which is why RLHF can still make a model better at

01:50:32 --> 01:50:36
math, but these verifiable domains are a much more direct process to

01:50:35 --> 01:50:40
doing this because it makes more sense with the problem

01:50:39 --> 01:50:44
formulation, which is why it ends up all forming together. But to summarize, it's like

01:50:43 --> 01:50:48
mid-training is give the model the skills it needs to then

01:50:47 --> 01:50:52
learn. RL with verifiable rewards is letting the model try

01:50:51 --> 01:50:55
a lot of times, so put a lot of compute into trial-and-error learning across hard

01:50:54 --> 01:50:59
problems. And then RLHF would be like finishing the model, making it easy to

01:50:58 --> 01:51:03
use and kind of just rounding the model out.

01:51:02 --> 01:51:06
- Can you comment on the amount of compute required for RLVR?

01:51:06 --> 01:51:10
- It's only gone up and up. I think Ilya Sutskever was

01:51:09 --> 01:51:13
famous for saying they use a similar amount of compute for pre-training and

01:51:12 --> 01:51:17
post-training. Back to the scaling discussion, they involve very

01:51:16 --> 01:51:21
different hardware for scaling. Pre-training is very compute-bound, which is like this

01:51:20 --> 01:51:25
FLOPs discussion, which is just how many matrix multiplications can you get through at once.

01:51:24 --> 01:51:28
And because with RL you're generating these answers, you're trying the

01:51:28 --> 01:51:32
model in real-world environments, it ends up being much more memory-bound because

01:51:31 --> 01:51:36
you're generating long sequences and the attention mechanisms have

01:51:35 --> 01:51:40
this behavior where you get a quadratic increase in memory

01:51:40 --> 01:51:44
as you're getting to longer sequences. So the compute becomes very different.

01:51:43 --> 01:51:48
In pre-training we would talk about a model—if we go back to like the Biden

01:51:47 --> 01:51:52
administration executive order, it's like 10 to the 25th FLOPs to train a

01:51:51 --> 01:51:56
model. If you're using FLOPs in post-training, it's a lot weirder because the

01:51:55 --> 01:51:59
reality is just like: how many hours are you allocating? How many GPUs

01:51:59 --> 01:52:03
for? And I think in terms of time, the RL compute is

01:52:02 --> 01:52:07
getting much closer because you just can't put it all into one system.

01:52:06 --> 01:52:11
Pre-training is so computationally dense where all the GPUs are talking to each other and

01:52:10 --> 01:52:15
it's extremely efficient, whereas RL has all these moving parts and can take a long time

01:52:15 --> 01:52:19
to generate a sequence of 100,000 tokens. If you think

01:52:18 --> 01:52:23
about GPT-5.2 Pro taking an hour, it's like, what if your training run

01:52:22 --> 01:52:26
has a sample for an hour and you have to make sure that's handled efficiently?

01:52:25 --> 01:52:30
So I think in GPU hours or just wall-clock hours,

01:52:29 --> 01:52:33
the RL runs are probably approaching the same number of days as pre-training,

01:52:32 --> 01:52:37
but they probably aren't using as many GPUs at the same time.

01:52:36 --> 01:52:41
There are rules of thumb where in labs you don't want your pre-training

01:52:40 --> 01:52:45
runs to last more than a month because they fail catastrophically. And if you

01:52:44 --> 01:52:49
are planning a huge cluster to be held for two months and then it

01:52:48 --> 01:52:53
fails on day 50, the opportunity costs are just so big.

01:52:52 --> 01:52:57
So people don't want to put all their eggs in one basket.

01:52:56 --> 01:53:01
GPT-4 was the ultimate YOLO run, and nobody ever wanted to

01:53:00 --> 01:53:05
do it before, where it took three months to train and everybody was shocked that it

01:53:04 --> 01:53:08
worked. I think people are a little bit more cautious and incremental now.

01:53:07 --> 01:53:12
- So RLVR is more, let's say, unlimited in

01:53:11 --> 01:53:16
how much you can train or still get benefit, where RLHF, because it's a preference

01:53:15 --> 01:53:20
tuning, you reach a certain point where it doesn't really make sense to spend more

01:53:19 --> 01:53:23
RL budget on that. So just a step back with preference tuning:

01:53:22 --> 01:53:27
there are multiple people that can give multiple, let's say,

01:53:26 --> 01:53:31
explanations for the same thing and they can both be correct, but at some point you learn a certain

01:53:30 --> 01:53:35
style and it doesn't make sense to iterate on it. My

01:53:34 --> 01:53:39
favorite example is: if relatives ask me what laptop they should buy,

01:53:38 --> 01:53:43
I give them an explanation or ask, "What is your use case?"

01:53:42 --> 01:53:47
They, for example, prioritize battery life and

01:53:46 --> 01:53:51
storage. Other people like us, for example, we would prioritize RAM and

01:53:50 --> 01:53:55
compute. Both answers are correct, but different people

01:53:54 --> 01:53:59
require different answers. With preference tuning, you're trying to average somehow.

01:53:58 --> 01:54:03
You are asking the data labelers to give you, not the right,

01:54:02 --> 01:54:06
but the preferred answer and then you train on that. But at some point you learn

01:54:05 --> 01:54:10
that average preferred answer. And there's no reason to keep

01:54:10 --> 01:54:15
training longer on it because it's just a style, whereas with RLVR,

01:54:14 --> 01:54:19
you let the model solve more and more

01:54:18 --> 01:54:23
complex, difficult problems. So I think it makes more sense to allocate

01:54:22 --> 01:54:31
more budget long-term to RLVR. Also, right now we are in an RLVR 1.0

01:54:30 --> 01:54:35
blend where it's still that simple thing where we have a question and

01:54:34 --> 01:54:39
answer, but we don't do anything with the stuff in between.

01:54:38 --> 01:54:43
There were multiple research papers, also by Google for example,

01:54:42 --> 01:54:47
on process reward models that also give scores for the

01:54:46 --> 01:54:50
xplanation—how correct is the explanation. And I think that will be the

01:54:50 --> 01:54:54
next thing, let's say RLVR 2.0 for this year,

01:54:53 --> 01:54:58
focusing in between question and answer, like how to leverage that

01:54:57 --> 01:55:02
information, the explanation, to help it get better

01:55:01 --> 01:55:06
accuracy. So that's one angle. And there was a DeepSeek

01:55:05 --> 01:55:09
Math-V2 paper where they also had

01:55:08 --> 01:55:13
interesting inference scaling there where, first, they had

01:55:12 --> 01:55:17
eveloped models that grade themselves, a separate model.

01:55:16 --> 01:55:21
And I think that will be one aspect. And the other, like Nathan mentioned, it will

01:55:20 --> 01:55:24
be for RLVR branching into other domains.

01:55:23 --> 01:55:27
- The place where people are excited are value functions,

01:55:26 --> 01:55:31
which is pretty similar. So process reward models are kind of like...

01:55:30 --> 01:55:33
Process reward models assign

01:55:33 --> 01:55:37
how good something is at each intermediate step in a reasoning

01:55:36 --> 01:55:41
process, where value functions apply value to every token the language

01:55:40 --> 01:55:47
model generates. Both of these have been largely unproven in the language

01:55:46 --> 01:55:51
modeling and reasoning model era. People are more optimistic about

01:55:50 --> 01:55:54
value functions for whatever reason now. I think process reward

01:55:53 --> 01:55:58
models were tried a lot more in this pre-o1, pre-reasoning

01:55:57 --> 01:56:02
model era, and a lot of people had a lot of headaches with them. So I think a lot of it is human

01:56:01 --> 01:56:03
ature...

01:56:03 --> 01:56:08
Value models have a very deep history in reinforcement learning. They're one of the

01:56:07 --> 01:56:12
first things core to deep reinforcement learning existing, is

01:56:11 --> 01:56:16
training value models. So right now people are excited

01:56:15 --> 01:56:20
about trying value models, but there's very little proof. And there are negative

01:56:19 --> 01:56:23
xamples in trying to scale up process reward models.

01:56:22 --> 01:56:26
These things don't always hold in the future. We came to this

01:56:25 --> 01:56:30
discussion by talking about scaling. The simple way to summarize what you're

01:56:29 --> 01:56:34
saying is you don't want to do too much RLHF, where the signal doesn't scale.

01:56:33 --> 01:56:38
People have worked on RLHF for language models for years, especially with intense

01:56:37 --> 01:56:42
interest after ChatGPT. And the first release

01:56:41 --> 01:56:46
