of a reasoning model trained with RLVR, OpenAI's o1, had a scaling

01:56:45 --> 01:56:50
plot where if you increase training compute logarithmically, you get a linear

01:56:49 --> 01:56:54
increase in evaluations. This has been reproduced multiple times. DeepSeek

01:56:53 --> 01:56:58
had a plot like this. But there's no scaling law for RLHF where if you

01:56:57 --> 01:57:02
log-increase the compute, you get performance. In fact, the seminal scaling

01:57:01 --> 01:57:05
paper for RLHF is scaling laws for reward model over-optimization.

01:57:05 --> 01:57:09
So that's a big line to draw with RLVR and the

01:57:08 --> 01:57:13
methods we have now. In the future, they will follow this scaling

01:57:13 --> 01:57:18
paradigm: where you can let the best runs run for an

01:57:17 --> 01:57:21
extra 10x and you get performance, but you can't do this with

01:57:20 --> 01:57:25
RLHF. And that is just going to be field-defining in how people approach

01:57:24 --> 01:57:29
them. While I'm a shill for people to academically do RLHF,

01:57:28 --> 01:57:33
to do the best RLHF you might not

01:57:32 --> 01:57:37
need the extra 10 or 100x of compute, but to do the best

01:57:37 --> 01:57:42
RLVR you do. I think there's a seminal paper

01:57:41 --> 01:57:46
from a Meta internship. It's called something like "The Art of

01:57:45 --> 01:57:49
Scaling Reinforcement Learning with Language Models." What they describe as a

01:57:48 --> 01:57:53
framework is Scale-RL. Their incremental experiment

01:57:52 --> 01:57:57
was like 10,000 V100 hours, which is like thousands or

01:57:56 --> 01:58:01
tens of thousands of dollars per experiment. They do a lot of them, and

01:58:00 --> 01:58:05
This cost is not accessible to the average

01:58:04 --> 01:58:08
academic, which is a hard equilibrium where

01:58:07 --> 01:58:12
it's trying to figure out how to learn from each community.

01:58:11 --> 01:58:15
- I was wondering if we could take a bit of a tangent and talk

01:58:15 --> 01:58:19
about education and learning. If you're someone listening

01:58:18 --> 01:58:23
to this who's a smart person interested in programming

01:58:22 --> 01:58:28
and AI, I presume building something from scratch is a good beginning.

01:58:28 --> 01:58:32
So can you take me through what you would recommend people do?

01:58:32 --> 01:58:36
- I would personally start, like you said, implementing a simple model from

01:58:35 --> 01:58:40
scratch that you can run on your computer. The goal is not,

01:58:39 --> 01:58:44
when you build a model from scratch, to have something for every day use.

01:58:43 --> 01:58:47
It's not going to be your personal assistant replacing

01:58:46 --> 01:58:51
an existing open-weight model or ChatGPT. It's to see what exactly

01:58:50 --> 01:58:55
goes into the LLM, what comes out, and how the pre-training works

01:58:54 --> 01:58:59
on your own computer, preferably.

01:58:59 --> 01:59:03
Then you learn about pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and the

01:59:02 --> 01:59:07
attention mechanism. You get a solid understanding of how things work, but

01:59:06 --> 01:59:11
at some point you reach a limit, because small models can only do so much.

01:59:11 --> 01:59:16
The problem with learning about LLMs at scale is that it's

01:59:15 --> 01:59:19
exponentially more complex to make a larger model,

01:59:18 --> 01:59:23
because the model isn't just larger—you have to shard your

01:59:22 --> 01:59:27
parameters across multiple GPUs. Even for the KV cache, there are multiple

01:59:26 --> 01:59:31
ways to implement it. One is just to understand how it works, just to grow the

01:59:30 --> 01:59:35
cache. You grow it step-by-step by, let's say, concatenating

01:59:34 --> 01:59:39
lists, but then that wouldn't be optimal on

01:59:38 --> 01:59:43
GPUs. You would pre-allocate a tensor and then fill it in.

01:59:42 --> 01:59:47
But that adds another 20 or 30 lines of code. And for each thing,

01:59:46 --> 01:59:51
you add so much code. The goal with the book is basically to understand

01:59:50 --> 01:59:55
how the LLM works. It's not going to be a production-level LLM, but once you have

01:59:54 --> 01:59:57
that, you can understand the production-level LLM.

01:59:56 --> 02:00:01
- So you're trying to always build an LLM that's going to fit on one GPU?

02:00:00 --> 02:00:04
- Yes. Most of them do. I have some bonus materials on some

02:00:04 --> 02:00:09
MoE models. One or two of them may require multiple GPUs,

02:00:08 --> 02:00:13
but the goal is to have it on one GPU. And the beautiful thing is,

02:00:12 --> 02:00:17
you can self-verify. It's almost like RLVR. When you code these from

02:00:16 --> 02:00:21
scratch, you can take an existing model from the Hugging Face

02:00:20 --> 02:00:25
Transformers library. The library is great, but if

02:00:24 --> 02:00:29
you want to learn about LLMs, it's not the best place to start because the

02:00:28 --> 02:00:33
code is so complex to fit so many use cases.

02:00:32 --> 02:00:36
Because people use it in production, it has to be really sophisticated,

02:00:35 --> 02:00:40
really intertwined, and hard to read. It's not linear.

02:00:39 --> 02:00:43
- It started as a fine-tuning library, and then it grew to be the

02:00:42 --> 02:00:45
standard representation of every model architecture.

02:00:45 --> 02:00:50
Hugging Face is the default place to get a model, and Transformers is the software.

02:00:49 --> 02:00:57
It enables it so people can easily load a model and do something basic with it.

02:00:56 --> 02:01:01
- And all frontier labs that have open-weight models have a Transformers

02:01:00 --> 02:01:04
version of it, like from DeepSeek to gpt-oss-120b. That's the

02:01:03 --> 02:01:08
canonical weight format you can load. But even

02:01:07 --> 02:01:12
Transformers, the library, is not used in production. People use

02:01:11 --> 02:01:16
SGLang or vLLM, and it adds another layer of complexity.

02:01:15 --> 02:01:20
- We should say that the Transformers library has like 400 models.

02:01:19 --> 02:01:23
- So it's the one library that tries to implement a lot of LLMs, and

02:01:23 --> 02:01:27
so you have a huge codebase, basically. It's huge. It's

02:01:26 --> 02:01:31
like, I don't know, maybe millions—

02:01:30 --> 02:01:31
- That's crazy.

02:01:30 --> 02:01:35
- hundreds of thousands of lines of code. Understanding the part

02:01:34 --> 02:01:38
you want to understand is finding the needle in the haystack. But what's beautiful is

02:01:38 --> 02:01:42
you have a working implementation, so you can work backwards. What I would

02:01:42 --> 02:01:47
recommend doing, or what I also do, is if I want to understand, for example, how OLMo

02:01:46 --> 02:01:51
is implemented, I would look at the weights in the model hub, the config file,

02:01:50 --> 02:01:55
and then you can see, "Oh, they used so many layers. They

02:01:54 --> 02:01:59
use, let's say, Group Query Attention or Multi-Head Attention in that case."

02:01:58 --> 02:02:02
Then you see all the components in a human-readable, 100-line config file.

02:02:01 --> 02:02:06
And then you start, let's say, with your GPT-2 model and add these things.

02:02:05 --> 02:02:10
The cool thing here is you can then load the pretrained weights

02:02:09 --> 02:02:14
and see if they work in your model. You want to match the

02:02:13 --> 02:02:18
same output that you get with a Transformer model, and then you can use that,

02:02:17 --> 02:02:22
basically as a verifiable reward to make your architecture correct.

02:02:21 --> 02:02:26
Sometimes it takes me a day. With OLMo 3, the challenge was RoPE

02:02:25 --> 02:02:30
for the position embeddings. They had a YaRN extension

02:02:29 --> 02:02:33
and there was some custom scaling there,

02:02:32 --> 02:02:37
and I couldn't quite match these things. In this struggle,

02:02:36 --> 02:02:41
you kind of understand things. At the end, you know you have it correct

02:02:40 --> 02:02:45
because you can unit test it. You can check against the reference

02:02:44 --> 02:02:49
implementation. I think that's one of the best ways to learn, really.

02:02:48 --> 02:02:52
To basically reverse-engineer something.

02:02:51 --> 02:02:55
- I think that is something everyone interested in getting into AI

02:02:55 --> 02:03:00
today should do. That's why I liked your book. I came to

02:02:59 --> 02:03:04
language models from the RL and robotics field. I had never taken the

02:03:03 --> 02:03:07
time to just learn all the fundamentals.

02:03:06 --> 02:03:11
This transformer architecture is so fundamental, just as deep

02:03:10 --> 02:03:15
learning was in the past, and people need to do this.

02:03:14 --> 02:03:19
I think where a lot of people get overwhelmed

02:03:18 --> 02:03:23
is, "How do I apply this to have impact or find a career path?"

02:03:22 --> 02:03:27
Because language models make this fundamental stuff so accessible,

02:03:26 --> 02:03:31
and people with motivation will learn it. Then it's like,

02:03:30 --> 02:03:35
"How do I get cycles on goal to contribute to research?"

02:03:34 --> 02:03:38
I'm actually fairly optimistic because the field moves so fast

02:03:37 --> 02:03:42
hat a lot of times the best people don't fully solve a problem

02:03:41 --> 02:03:46
because there's a bigger problem to solve that's very low-hanging fruit,

02:03:45 --> 02:03:50
so they move on. I think that a lot of what I was trying to do in this

02:03:49 --> 02:03:54
RLHF book is take post-training techniques and describe how

02:03:53 --> 02:03:58
people think about them influencing the model and what people are doing.

02:03:57 --> 02:04:02
Then it's remarkable how many things

02:04:01 --> 02:04:06
I just think people stop studying or don't pursue.

02:04:05 --> 02:04:08
I think people trying to go narrow after doing the fundamentals is good,

02:04:08 --> 02:04:13
and then reading the relevant papers

02:04:12 --> 02:04:17
and being engaged in the ecosystem. It's like you actually...

02:04:16 --> 02:04:20
The proximity that random people online have

02:04:19 --> 02:04:24
to the leading researchers—no one knows who all the...

02:04:23 --> 02:04:28
The anonymous accounts on X and ML are very popular, and no one knows who

02:04:27 --> 02:04:32
all these people are. It could just be random people that study this stuff deeply, especially with

02:04:31 --> 02:04:36
e AI tools. To just be like, "I don't understand this, keep digging into it," is a

02:04:35 --> 02:04:40
very useful thing. But there's a lot of research areas that

02:04:39 --> 02:04:44
maybe have three papers that you need to read, and then

02:04:43 --> 02:04:48
one of the authors will probably email you back. But you have to put in a lot

02:04:47 --> 02:04:52
of effort into these emails to understand the field. I think it would

02:04:51 --> 02:04:55
be for a newcomer easily weeks of work to feel like they can

02:04:54 --> 02:04:59
truly grasp what is a very narrow area, but I think going

02:04:58 --> 02:05:03
narrow after you have the fundamentals will be very useful to people because

02:05:02 --> 02:05:07
I've become very interested in character training, which is how you

02:05:07 --> 02:05:12
make the model funny or sarcastic or serious,

02:05:11 --> 02:05:15
and what do you do to the data to do this? A student

02:05:15 --> 02:05:19
at Oxford reached out to me and was like, "Hey, I'm interested in this," and I advised him.

02:05:18 --> 02:05:23
And that paper now exists. There's like two or

02:05:22 --> 02:05:27
three people in the world that were very interested in this. He's a PhD student, which

02:05:26 --> 02:05:28
gives him an advantage, but

02:05:28 --> 02:05:33
for me, that was a topic I was waiting for someone to be like, "Hey, I have time to spend

02:05:32 --> 02:05:37
cycles on this." I'm sure there's a lot more very narrow things where you're just like,

02:05:36 --> 02:05:40
"It doesn't make sense that there was no answer to this." I think it's just

02:05:40 --> 02:05:45
here's so much information coming that people are like, "I can't grab onto any of

02:05:44 --> 02:05:49
these," but if you just stick in an area, I think there's a lot of interesting things to learn.

02:05:48 --> 02:05:53
- Yeah, I think you can't try to do it all because it would be very overwhelming and

02:05:52 --> 02:05:57
you would burn out. For me, for example, I haven't kept up with

02:05:56 --> 02:06:01
computer vision in a long time; I just focused on LLMs. But coming back to your book,

02:06:00 --> 02:06:05
I think this is a really great book and a really good bang for the buck because

02:06:04 --> 02:06:09
if you want to learn about RLHF, I wouldn't go out there and read RLHF

02:06:08 --> 02:06:11
papers because you would be spending two years—

02:06:10 --> 02:06:12
- Some of them contradict.

02:06:11 --> 02:06:16
I've just edited the book, and there's no chapter where I had to be like,

02:06:15 --> 02:06:20
"X papers say one thing and Y papers say another, and we'll see what

02:06:19 --> 02:06:22
comes out to be true."

02:06:21 --> 02:06:26
- Just to go through the table of contents, what are some ideas we might have missed in the

02:06:25 --> 02:06:30
bigger picture of post-training? First of all, you did the problem setup, training

02:06:29 --> 02:06:33
overview, what are preferences, preference data, and the optimization

02:06:33 --> 02:06:37
tools, reward modeling, regularization, instruction tuning,

02:06:36 --> 02:06:44
rejection sampling, and reinforcement learning. Then,

02:06:43 --> 02:06:48
Constitutional AI and AI feedback, reasoning, and inference-time

02:06:47 --> 02:06:51
scaling to use in function calling, synthetic data and distillation,

02:06:51 --> 02:06:55
evaluation, and then an open questions section,

02:06:54 --> 02:06:59
over-optimization, style and information, and then product

02:06:58 --> 02:07:02
UX, character and post-training. What are some ideas worth

02:07:02 --> 02:07:06
mentioning that connect both the educational and the research components?

02:07:06 --> 02:07:09
You mentioned character training, which is pretty interesting.

02:07:08 --> 02:07:13
- Character training is interesting because there's so little on it. We talked about how people

02:07:12 --> 02:07:17
ngage with these models. We feel good using them because

02:07:16 --> 02:07:20
they're positive, but that can go too far; it can be too positive. And it's like,

02:07:19 --> 02:07:23
essentially, it's: How do you change your data

02:07:23 --> 02:07:28
nd decision-making to make it exactly what you want? And like,

02:07:27 --> 02:07:31
OpenAI has this thing called a model spec, which is essentially their

02:07:30 --> 02:07:35
internal guideline for what they want the model to do, and they publish this to

02:07:34 --> 02:07:39
developers. So, essentially, you can know what is a failure of

02:07:38 --> 02:07:43
OpenAI's training—where they have the intentions and they haven't met them yet—

02:07:42 --> 02:07:47
versus what is something that they actually wanted to do and that you don't like.

02:07:46 --> 02:07:50
And that transparency is very nice, but all the methods for curating these

02:07:49 --> 02:07:54
documents and how easy it is to follow them is not very well known. I think the

02:07:53 --> 02:07:58
way the book is designed is that the RL chapter is obviously what people want because

02:07:57 --> 02:08:02
verybody hears about it with RLVR, and it's the same algorithms and the same

02:08:01 --> 02:08:06
math, but you can use it in very different documents.

02:08:05 --> 02:08:09
So I think the core of RLHF is like how messy

02:08:09 --> 02:08:13
preferences are. It's essentially a rehash of a paper I

02:08:12 --> 02:08:17
wrote years ago, but this is essentially the chapter that'll tell you why

02:08:16 --> 02:08:21
RLHF is never ever fully solvable because,

02:08:21 --> 02:08:29
the way that even RL is set up, it assumes that preferences can be

02:08:28 --> 02:08:33
quantified and that multiple preferences can be reduced

02:08:32 --> 02:08:37
to single values. And I think it relates in the economics literature to the

02:08:36 --> 02:08:41
Von Neumann-Morgenstern utility theorem, and that is the chapter where all

02:08:40 --> 02:08:45
of that philosophical, economic, and psychological

02:08:44 --> 02:08:49
context tells you what gets compressed into doing RLHF. So it's like you

02:08:48 --> 02:08:53
have all of this and then later in the book it's like: You use this RL map to make the

02:08:52 --> 02:08:56
number go up. And I think that's why it'll be very rewarding for people to do

02:08:56 --> 02:09:00
research on, because quantifying preferences is something

02:08:59 --> 02:09:04
that humans have designed a problem in order to

02:09:03 --> 02:09:08
make preferences studyable. But there's kind of fundamental debates, like,

02:09:08 --> 02:09:13
an example is in a language model response you have different things you care about, like

02:09:12 --> 02:09:16
accuracy or style. And when you're collecting the data, they all get compressed

02:09:15 --> 02:09:20
into: "I like this more than another." And that is

02:09:19 --> 02:09:24
happening, and there's a lot of research in other areas of the

02:09:23 --> 02:09:27
world that go into how you should actually do this. I think

02:09:27 --> 02:09:29
social choice theory is the

02:09:29 --> 02:09:34
subfield of economics around how you should aggregate preferences.

02:09:33 --> 02:09:38
And I went to a workshop that published a white

02:09:37 --> 02:09:41
paper on: "How can you think about using social choice theory for

02:09:40 --> 02:09:45
RLHF?" So I mostly would want people that get excited about the math to

02:09:44 --> 02:09:49
come and find things where they could stumble into this broader

02:09:48 --> 02:09:53
context. I think there's a fun thing: I just keep a list of all the tech

02:09:52 --> 02:09:56
reports of reasoning models I like. So in Chapter 14,

02:09:56 --> 02:10:00
where there's a short summary of RLVR, there's just a gigantic table where I

02:09:59 --> 02:10:04
list every single reasoning model that I like.

02:10:03 --> 02:10:09
I think in education, a lot of it needs to be like, at this point, what I like,

02:10:08 --> 02:10:13
because the language models are so good at the math. For example, the

02:10:12 --> 02:10:17
famous paper, Direct Preference Optimization, which is a much simpler way of solving

02:10:16 --> 02:10:21
the problem than RL. The derivations in the appendix skip steps

02:10:20 --> 02:10:25
of math. And for this book, I redid the derivations and I'm like,

02:10:24 --> 02:10:28
"What the heck is this log trick that they use to change the math?"

02:10:27 --> 02:10:31
But doing it with language models, they're like, "This is the log trick." And I'm

02:10:31 --> 02:10:35
like, "I don't know if I like this, that the math is so commoditized."

02:10:34 --> 02:10:39
I think some of the struggle in reading this appendix-

02:10:38 --> 02:10:44
...and following the math is good for learning.

02:10:43 --> 02:10:48
- Yeah, we're returning to this often on the topic of education.

02:10:47 --> 02:10:52
You both have brought up the word "struggle" quite a bit.

02:10:51 --> 02:10:56
So there is value. If you're not struggling as part of this process,

02:10:55 --> 02:11:00
you're not fully following the proper process for learning.

02:10:59 --> 02:11:03
proper process for learning, I suppose.

02:11:02 --> 02:11:07
- Some providers are working on models for education designed to not give-

02:11:06 --> 02:11:11
actually, I haven't used them, but I'd guess they're designed to not give

02:11:10 --> 02:11:12
all the information at once.

02:11:12 --> 02:11:17
And make people work for it. Training models to do this would be a wonderful contribution.

02:11:16 --> 02:11:20
Where, like all of the stuff in the book, you had to reevaluate every decision.

02:11:19 --> 02:11:25
decision for it- It's a great example. There's a chance we work on it at Ai2,

02:11:24 --> 02:11:27
which I thought would be so fun.

02:11:26 --> 02:11:31
- It makes sense. I did something like that the other day for video games.

02:11:30 --> 02:11:34
Sometimes for pastime I play video games, like I like-

02:11:34 --> 02:11:38
Video games with puzzles, like Zelda and Metroid. And there's this

02:11:38 --> 02:11:42
new game where I really got stuck and was okay with it.

02:11:41 --> 02:11:46
I don't want to struggle for two days, so I used an LLM.

02:11:45 --> 02:11:50
But then you say, "Hey, please don't add spoilers. Just, you know, I'm here and there.

02:11:49 --> 02:11:54
What do I have to do next?" You can do the same thing for math where you say,

02:11:53 --> 02:11:58
"Okay, I'm stuck at this point. Don't give me the full solution,

02:11:57 --> 02:12:02
but what is something I could try?" Where you carefully probe it.

02:12:01 --> 02:12:06
But the problem here is I think it requires discipline.

02:12:05 --> 02:12:10
Many people enjoy math, but there are also a lot of people

02:12:09 --> 02:12:14
who need to do it for their homework, and then it's like a shortcut.

02:12:13 --> 02:12:18
We could develop an educational LLM, but other LLMs are still there,

02:12:17 --> 02:12:21
and there's still a temptation to use the other LLMs.

02:12:20 --> 02:12:24
- I think many people in college understand the stuff they're passionate about-

02:12:24 --> 02:12:28
...they're self-aware and they understand it shouldn't be easy.

02:12:27 --> 02:12:32
I think we just have to develop a good taste-

02:12:31 --> 02:12:35
...talk about research taste, school taste about

02:12:34 --> 02:12:38
stuff that you should be struggling on-

02:12:37 --> 02:12:41
...and stuff you shouldn't be. It's tricky, because you don't have good long-term vision

02:12:40 --> 02:12:45
sometimes you don't have good long-term vision

02:12:44 --> 02:12:48
about what would be actually useful to you in your career.

02:12:48 --> 02:12:52
But you have to develop that taste, yeah.

02:12:51 --> 02:12:56
- I was talking to my fiancee or friends about this,

02:12:55 --> 02:13:00
there's this brief 10-year window where all of the homework and all the

02:12:59 --> 02:13:04
xams could be digital. Before that, everybody had to do all the exams in blue books

02:13:03 --> 02:13:08
because there was no other way. And now after AI, everyone's going to need to be in blue books and

02:13:07 --> 02:13:12
oral exams because everyone could cheat so easily. It's like this brief generation that had a

02:13:11 --> 02:13:15
different education system where everything could be

02:13:15 --> 02:13:19
digital, but you still couldn't cheat. And now it's just going back.

02:13:18 --> 02:13:21
It's just very funny.

02:13:20 --> 02:13:25
- You mention character training. Just zooming out on a more general

02:13:24 --> 02:13:28
topic, for that project how much compute was

02:13:27 --> 02:13:31
required? And in general, to contribute as a

02:13:30 --> 02:13:35
researcher, are there places where not too

02:13:34 --> 02:13:39
much compute is required where you can actually contribute as an individual

02:13:38 --> 02:13:40
researcher?

02:13:39 --> 02:13:44
- For the character training thing, I think this research is built on fine-tuning

02:13:43 --> 02:13:48
about 7 billion parameter models with LoRA, which is

02:13:47 --> 02:13:51
essentially only fine-tuning a small subset of the weights of the model.

02:13:51 --> 02:13:56
I don't know exactly how many GPU hours that would take.

02:13:55 --> 02:13:56
- But it's doable.

02:13:55 --> 02:14:00
- Not doable for every academic. The situation for some academics is so dire

02:13:59 --> 02:14:04
that the only work you can do is doing inference where you have closed models or open

02:14:03 --> 02:14:08
models and you get completions from them and you can look at them and understand the

02:14:07 --> 02:14:12
models. And that's very well-suited to evaluation, where you want to

02:14:11 --> 02:14:15
be the best at creating representative

02:14:14 --> 02:14:19
problems that the models fail on or show certain abilities, which I think

02:14:18 --> 02:14:23
that you can break through with this. I think that

02:14:22 --> 02:14:27
he top-end goal for a researcher working on evaluation, if you

02:14:26 --> 02:14:30
want to have career momentum, is that Frontier Labs pick up your

02:14:29 --> 02:14:34
evaluation. You don't need to have every project do this. But if you

02:14:33 --> 02:14:38
go from a small university with no compute and find something that Claude struggles with,

02:14:37 --> 02:14:42
and then the next Claude model has it in the blog post, there's your

02:14:41 --> 02:14:46
career rocket ship. I think that's hard, but if you want to

02:14:45 --> 02:14:50
scope the maximum possible impact with minimum compute, it's something

02:14:49 --> 02:14:54
like that, which is just get very narrow and it takes learning of where the

02:14:53 --> 02:14:58
models are going. So you need to build a tool that tests

02:14:57 --> 02:15:02
where Claude 4.5 will fail. If I'm going to start

02:15:01 --> 02:15:06
a research project, I need to think where the models in eight months are going to be struggling.

02:15:05 --> 02:15:09
- But what about developing totally novel ideas?

02:15:08 --> 02:15:13
- This is a trade-off. I think that if you're doing a PhD, you could also be like,

02:15:13 --> 02:15:18
"It's too risky to work in language models. I'm going way longer term,"

02:15:17 --> 02:15:20
which is like what is—

02:15:19 --> 02:15:23
what is the thing that's going to define language model development in 10 years?

02:15:22 --> 02:15:27
I end up being a person that's pretty practical. I mean, I went to

02:15:26 --> 02:15:31
my PhD where it was like, "I got into Berkeley. Worst case, I get a master's, and then I go

02:15:30 --> 02:15:34
work in tech." I'm very practical about it, so I'm like

02:15:34 --> 02:15:39
the life afforded to people to work at these AI companies, the amount of...

02:15:38 --> 02:15:43
OpenAI's average compensation is over a million dollars in stock a year per

02:15:42 --> 02:15:47
employee. For any normal person in the US, to

02:15:46 --> 02:15:51
get into this AI lab is transformative for your life. So I'm pretty practical about it.

02:15:50 --> 02:15:54
there's still a lot of upward mobility working in language models if you're

02:15:53 --> 02:15:58
focused. And look at these jobs. But from a research perspective,

02:15:57 --> 02:16:00
the transformative impact in these

02:16:00 --> 02:16:05
academic awards... to be the next Yann LeCun is from not working on—

02:16:04 --> 02:16:08
not caring about language model development very much.

02:16:07 --> 02:16:10
- It's a big financial sacrifice in that case.

02:16:09 --> 02:16:14
- So I work with some awesome students, and they're like, "Should I go work at an

02:16:13 --> 02:16:18
AI lab?" And I'm like, "You're getting a PhD at a top school. Are you gonna

02:16:17 --> 02:16:22
leave to go to a lab?" I don't know. If you go work at a top lab,

02:16:21 --> 02:16:26
I don't blame you. Don't go work at some random startup that might go to zero. But if you're going to

02:16:25 --> 02:16:30
OpenAI, I'm like, "It could be worth leaving a PhD for."

02:16:30 --> 02:16:34
- Let's more rigorously think through this. So where would you

02:16:33 --> 02:16:38
give a recommendation for people to do a research contribution? So the options

02:16:37 --> 02:16:44
are academia: get a PhD. Spend five years publishing.

02:16:44 --> 02:16:47
Compute resources are constrained. There's—

02:16:46 --> 02:16:54
there's research labs that are more focused on open-weight models,

02:16:54 --> 02:17:02
and working there. Or closed frontier research labs.

02:17:01 --> 02:17:05
So OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI, and so on.

02:17:04 --> 02:17:09
- The two gradients are: the more closed, the more money you tend to get,

02:17:08 --> 02:17:13
but you also get less credit. In terms of building

02:17:13 --> 02:17:19
a portfolio of things that you've done, it's very clear

02:17:18 --> 02:17:23
what you have done as an academic. Versus if you are going to

02:17:22 --> 02:17:27
trade this fairly reasonable

02:17:26 --> 02:17:31
progression for being a cog in the machine, which could also be very fun.

02:17:30 --> 02:17:35
So I think it's very different career paths. But the

02:17:34 --> 02:17:38
opportunity cost for being a researcher is very high because PhD students are

02:17:37 --> 02:17:42
paid essentially nothing. So it ends up rewarding people that have a

02:17:41 --> 02:17:46
fairly stable safety net, and they realize that they can operate in the

02:17:45 --> 02:17:50
long term, wanting to do very interesting work and get a very interesting job.

02:17:49 --> 02:17:54
So it is a privileged position to be like,

02:17:53 --> 02:17:59
"I'm gonna see out my PhD and figure it out after because I want to do this."

02:17:59 --> 02:18:03
At the same time, the academic ecosystem is getting bombarded by funding

02:18:02 --> 02:18:07
etting cut and stuff. So there's just so many different trade-offs where I

02:18:06 --> 02:18:11
understand plenty of people that are like, "I don't enjoy it. I can't deal with this

02:18:10 --> 02:18:15
funding search. My grant got cut for no reason by the government,"

02:18:14 --> 02:18:19
or, "I don't know what's gonna happen." So I think there's a lot of

02:18:18 --> 02:18:21
uncertainty and trade-offs that, in my opinion, favor just

02:18:21 --> 02:18:26
aking the well-paying job with meaningful impact. It's not like

02:18:25 --> 02:18:30
you're getting paid to sit around at OpenAI. You're building the cutting edge of things

02:18:29 --> 02:18:35
that are— changing millions of people's relationship to tech.

02:18:34 --> 02:18:39
- But publication-wise, they're being more secretive, increasingly so.

02:18:38 --> 02:18:43
So you're publishing less and less. And so you are having

02:18:42 --> 02:18:48
a positive impact at scale, but you're a cog in the machine.

02:18:47 --> 02:18:52
- I think it honestly hasn't changed that much.

02:18:51 --> 02:18:56
I have been in academia. I'm not in academia anymore.

02:18:55 --> 02:19:00
wouldn't want to miss my time in academia. But what I wanted to say before I get to that

02:18:59 --> 02:19:04
is that I think it hasn't changed that much. I was working in

02:19:03 --> 02:19:08
computational biology, using AI or machine learning methods

02:19:07 --> 02:19:16
with collaborators, and a lot of people went from academia directly to

02:19:15 --> 02:19:20
Google. And I think it's the same. Back then, professors were

02:19:19 --> 02:19:23
sad that their students went into industry

02:19:22 --> 02:19:27
because they couldn't carry on their legacy. I think it's the same.

02:19:26 --> 02:19:31
It hasn't changed that much. The only thing that has

02:19:30 --> 02:19:35
changed is the scale. Cool stuff was always developed in

02:19:34 --> 02:19:39
dustry that was closed. You couldn't talk about it.

02:19:38 --> 02:19:43
And I think the difference now is your preference.

02:19:42 --> 02:19:47
Do you like to publish your work, or are you more in a

02:19:46 --> 02:19:51
closed lab? That's one difference. The compensation, of course,

02:19:50 --> 02:19:55
is another, but it's always been like that.

02:19:54 --> 02:19:59
It depends on where you feel comfortable. And nothing is

02:19:58 --> 02:20:03
forever. Right now, there's a third option, which is

02:20:02 --> 02:20:06
launching a startup. A lot of people are doing that.

02:20:05 --> 02:20:10
It's a very risky move, but it can be

02:20:09 --> 02:20:14
a high-risk, high-reward situation, whereas joining an industry lab

02:20:13 --> 02:20:18
is pretty safe. You also have upward mobility.

02:20:17 --> 02:20:22
I think once you've been at an industry lab, it's easier to find

02:20:21 --> 02:20:29
future jobs. But then again, how much do you enjoy the team

02:20:28 --> 02:20:33
and working on proprietary things versus how much you like

02:20:32 --> 02:20:36
publishing work? I mean, publishing is stressful.

02:20:35 --> 02:20:40
Acceptance rates at conferences can be arbitrary

02:20:39 --> 02:20:44
and very frustrating, but it's high reward if you have a paper

02:20:43 --> 02:20:49
published. You feel good because your name is on there. It's a high accomplishment.

02:20:48 --> 02:20:53
- I feel like my friends who are professors seem happier than those who work at

02:20:52 --> 02:20:58
a frontier lab, to be honest. There's a grounding there.

02:20:57 --> 02:21:01
The frontier labs definitely do this 9-9-6,

02:21:00 --> 02:21:04
which is shorthand for working all the time.

02:21:03 --> 02:21:08
- Can you describe 9-9-6? It's a culture invented, I believe,

02:21:07 --> 02:21:14
in China and adopted in Silicon Valley. What is 9-9-6? It's 9:00 AM to 9:00 PM,

02:21:14 --> 02:21:16
- Six days a week.

02:21:15 --> 02:21:21
- six days a week. What is that, 72 hours? Okay. So, is this basically the standard

02:21:21 --> 02:21:27
in AI companies in Silicon Valley? This kind of grind mindset.

02:21:26 --> 02:21:31
- Yeah, I mean, maybe not exactly like that, but I think there is a trend towards it.

02:21:30 --> 02:21:35
And it's interesting. I think it almost flipped because when I was in

02:21:34 --> 02:21:39
academia, I felt like that. As a professor, you write grants,

02:21:38 --> 02:21:42
you teach, and you do research. It's like three jobs in one,

02:21:41 --> 02:21:46
and it's more than a full-time job if you want to be successful.

02:21:45 --> 02:21:50
And I feel like now, like Nathan just said,

02:21:49 --> 02:21:54
the professors, in comparison to a lab, I think they have

02:21:53 --> 02:21:58
less pressure or workload than at a frontier lab because—

02:21:57 --> 02:22:01
- I think they work a lot. They're just so fulfilled. By working with students—

02:22:00 --> 02:22:05
and having a constant runway of mentorship and a mission that is

02:22:04 --> 02:22:09
very people-oriented, I think in a era when things are moving very fast

02:22:08 --> 02:22:12
and are very chaotic, it's very rewarding to people.

02:22:11 --> 02:22:15
- Yeah, and I think at a startup, it's this pressure. It's like

02:22:15 --> 02:22:20
you have to make it. And it's really important that people put in the time,

02:22:19 --> 02:22:24
but it is really hard because you have to deliver constantly, and

02:22:23 --> 02:22:28
I've been at a startup. I had a good time, but I don't know if I could do it

02:22:27 --> 02:22:32
forever. It's an interesting pace and it's

02:22:31 --> 02:22:36
exactly like we talked about in the beginning. These models are leapfrogging

02:22:35 --> 02:22:39
each other, and they are just constantly trying to take the

02:22:38 --> 02:22:43
next step compared to their competitors. It's just ruthless right now.

02:22:42 --> 02:22:46
- I think this leapfrogging nature and having multiple players is actually an

02:22:45 --> 02:22:50
underrated driver of language modeling progress where competition is

02:22:49 --> 02:22:54
o deeply ingrained in people, and these

02:22:53 --> 02:22:58
companies have intentionally created very strong cultures. Like, Anthropic is

02:22:57 --> 02:23:02
known to be so culturally, like, deeply

02:23:01 --> 02:23:06
committed and organized. I mean, we hear so little from them, and

02:23:05 --> 02:23:10
everybody at Anthropic seems very aligned. And it's like being in

02:23:09 --> 02:23:12
a culture that is super

02:23:12 --> 02:23:17
tight and having this competitive dynamic is a thing that's gonna

02:23:16 --> 02:23:21
make you work hard and create things that are better.

02:23:20 --> 02:23:24
But that comes at the cost of human capital, which is like

02:23:24 --> 02:23:29
you can only do this for so long, and people are definitely burning out.

02:23:28 --> 02:23:33
I wrote a post on burnout as I've tread in and out

02:23:32 --> 02:23:37
of this myself, especially trying to be a manager, full-mode training.

02:23:36 --> 02:23:40
It's a crazy job doing this. The book Apple in China by Patrick

02:23:39 --> 02:23:44
McGee, he talked about how hard the Apple engineers worked to set up the supply

02:23:43 --> 02:23:48
chains in China, and he was like, they had "saving marriage" programs, and

02:23:47 --> 02:23:52
he told in a podcast, he was like, "People died

02:23:51 --> 02:23:55
from this level of working hard." So I think it's just like

02:23:55 --> 02:24:00
it's a perfect environment for creating progress based on human

02:23:59 --> 02:24:04
expense, and there's gonna be a lot of... the

02:24:03 --> 02:24:07
human expense is the 996 that we started this with, which is like—

02:24:06 --> 02:24:09
... people do really grind.

02:24:08 --> 02:24:13
- I also read this book. I think they had a code word for if someone had to go home

02:24:12 --> 02:24:17
to spend time with their family to save the marriage, and it's crazy. Then the

02:24:16 --> 02:24:21
colleagues said, "Okay, this is like red alert for this situation. We have to let

02:24:20 --> 02:24:25
hat person go home this weekend." But at the same time, I don't

02:24:24 --> 02:24:29
hink they were forced to work. They were so passionate about the product, I guess, that

02:24:28 --> 02:24:33
you get into that mindset. And I had that sometimes as an

02:24:32 --> 02:24:37
academic, but also as an independent person, I have that sometimes. I overwork,

02:24:36 --> 02:24:41
and it's unhealthy. I had back issues, I had neck issues, because I

02:24:40 --> 02:24:45
did not take the breaks that I maybe should have taken. But no one forced

02:24:44 --> 02:24:47
me to; it's because I wanted to work, because it's exciting stuff.

02:24:46 --> 02:24:50
- That's what OpenAI and Anthropic are like. They want to do this work.

02:24:49 --> 02:24:54
- Yeah, but there's also a feeling of fervor that's

02:24:53 --> 02:24:58
building, especially in Silicon Valley, aligned with the scaling laws idea, where

02:24:57 --> 02:25:01
there's this hype where the world will be transformed in a scale of

02:25:00 --> 02:25:05
weeks and you want to be at the center of it. And then, you know,

02:25:04 --> 02:25:09
I have this great fortune of having

02:25:08 --> 02:25:13
conversations with a wide variety of human beings,

02:25:12 --> 02:25:17
and from there I get to see all these bubbles and echo chambers across

02:25:16 --> 02:25:21
the world. It's fascinating to see how we humans form them. And I think it's

02:25:20 --> 02:25:28
fair to say that Silicon Valley is a kind of echo chamber, a kind of silo and

02:25:27 --> 02:25:32
bubble. I think bubbles are actually really useful and

02:25:31 --> 02:25:35
effective. It's not necessarily a negative thing because you could be ultra-productive.

02:25:34 --> 02:25:37
It could be the Steve Jobs

02:25:37 --> 02:25:42
reality distortion field, because you just convince each other that

02:25:41 --> 02:25:46
breakthroughs are imminent, and by convincing each other of

02:25:45 --> 02:25:49
that, you make the breakthroughs imminent.

02:25:48 --> 02:25:53
- Byrne Hobart wrote a book classifying bubbles. One of them is financial bubbles,

02:25:52 --> 02:25:57
which is like speculation, which is bad, and the other one is for build-outs,

02:25:56 --> 02:26:01
because it pushes people to build these things. And I do think AI is in

02:26:00 --> 02:26:06
this, but I worry about it transitioning to a financial bubble, which is

02:26:05 --> 02:26:09
- Yeah, but also in the space of ideas, that

02:26:08 --> 02:26:12
bubble—you are doing a reality distortion

02:26:11 --> 02:26:16
field, and that means you are deviating from reality. And if

02:26:15 --> 02:26:23
you go too far from reality while also working, you know, 996,

02:26:22 --> 02:26:26
you might miss some fundamental aspects of the human experience,

02:26:26 --> 02:26:30
including beyond Silicon Valley. This is a common problem in Silicon Valley:

02:26:30 --> 02:26:35
it's a very specific geographic area. You might not understand the

02:26:34 --> 02:26:36
Midwest perspective, the full

02:26:36 --> 02:26:41
experience of all the other humans in the United States and across the world,

02:26:40 --> 02:26:45
and you speak a certain way to each other, you convince each other of a certain

02:26:44 --> 02:26:49
thing, and that can get you into real trouble. Whether AI

02:26:48 --> 02:26:52
is a big success and becomes a powerful technology

02:26:51 --> 02:26:56
or it's not, in either trajectory you can get yourself into

02:26:55 --> 02:27:00
trouble. So you have to consider all of that. Here you are, a young

02:26:59 --> 02:27:03
person trying to decide what you want to do with your life.

02:27:02 --> 02:27:07
- The thing that is... I don't even really understand this, but the

02:27:06 --> 02:27:11
SF AI memes have gotten to the point where "permanent underclass" was

02:27:10 --> 02:27:14
one of them, which was the idea that the last six months of

02:27:13 --> 02:27:18
2025 was the only time to build durable value in an AI

02:27:17 --> 02:27:22
startup or model. Otherwise, all the value will be captured by

02:27:21 --> 02:27:25
existing companies and you will therefore be poor, which...

02:27:24 --> 02:27:29
that's an example of the SF thing that goes so far. I still

02:27:28 --> 02:27:33
think for young people going to be able to tap into it, if you're really

02:27:32 --> 02:27:37
passionate about wanting to have an impact in AI, being physically in

02:27:36 --> 02:27:41
SF is the most likely place where you're going to do this. But it has

02:27:40 --> 02:27:42
trade-offs.

02:27:41 --> 02:27:46
- I think SF is an incredible place, but there is a bit of

02:27:45 --> 02:27:50
a bubble. And if you go into that bubble, which is extremely

02:27:49 --> 02:27:54
valuable, just get out also. Read history

02:27:53 --> 02:27:58
books, read literature, visit other places in the world.

02:27:57 --> 02:28:01
Twitter and Substack are not the entire world.

02:28:01 --> 02:28:05
- I would say, one of the people I worked with is moving to SF, and it's like, I

02:28:05 --> 02:28:09
need to get him a copy of Season of the Witch, which is a history of SF from

02:28:08 --> 02:28:17
1960 to 1985, which goes through the hippie revolution, like all the

02:28:16 --> 02:28:21
gays taking over the city and that culture emerging, and then the

02:28:20 --> 02:28:25
HIV/AIDS crisis and other things. And it's just like, that is so recent, and

02:28:24 --> 02:28:29
so much turmoil and hurt, but also love in SF.

02:28:28 --> 02:28:33
And it's like, no one knows about this. It's a great book, Season of the Witch. I recommend it.

02:28:32 --> 02:28:35
A bunch of my SF friends who

02:28:35 --> 02:28:40
get out recommended it to me. And I think that's just like living

02:28:39 --> 02:28:43
there... I lived there and I didn't appreciate this context, and it's

02:28:43 --> 02:28:47
just so recent.

02:28:46 --> 02:28:54
- Yeah. Okay, let's... We talked a lot about a lot of things. Certainly about

02:28:54 --> 02:28:59
he things that were exciting last year. But this year,

02:28:58 --> 02:29:02
One of the things you guys mentioned that's exciting is the scaling of text

02:29:02 --> 02:29:06
diffusion models, and just a different exploration of text diffusion. Can you talk

02:29:05 --> 02:29:10
about what that is and what the possibility it holds?

02:29:09 --> 02:29:14
So, different kinds of approaches than the current LMs?

02:29:13 --> 02:29:18
- Yeah, so we talked a lot about the transformer architecture and the autoregressive

02:29:17 --> 02:29:22
transformer architecture specifically, like GPT. And it doesn't mean no one

02:29:21 --> 02:29:26
lse is working on anything else. So, people are always on the,

02:29:25 --> 02:29:29
let's say, lookout for the next big thing. Because I think it would be almost

02:29:28 --> 02:29:33
upid not to. Because sure, right now, the transformer

02:29:32 --> 02:29:37
architecture is the thing, and it works best, and there's, right now, nothing

02:29:36 --> 02:29:41
else out there. But, you know, it's always a good idea to not put all your eggs into one

02:29:40 --> 02:29:45
basket. So, people are developing other alternatives to the

02:29:44 --> 02:29:49
autoregressive transformer. One of them would be, for example, text

02:29:48 --> 02:29:53
diffusion models. And listeners may know diffusion models from image

02:29:52 --> 02:29:57
neration, like Stable Diffusion popularized it. There was a paper on

02:29:56 --> 02:30:01
generating images. Back then, people used GANs, Generative Adversarial

02:30:00 --> 02:30:04
Networks. And then there was this diffusion process where you iteratively

02:30:03 --> 02:30:08
denoise an image, and that resulted in really good quality images over

02:30:07 --> 02:30:12
time. Stable Diffusion was a company. Other companies build their own diffusion models.

02:30:11 --> 02:30:16
And then people are now like, "Okay, can we try this also for text?"

02:30:15 --> 02:30:20
Doesn't, you know, make intuitive sense yet, because it feels like, okay, it's not

02:30:19 --> 02:30:24
something continuous like a pixel that we can differentiate. It's discrete text, so how do

02:30:23 --> 02:30:31
we implement that denoising process? It's kind of similar to the BERT models by

02:30:30 --> 02:30:35
Google. Like, when you go back to the original transformer, they were the encoder and

02:30:34 --> 02:30:39
the decoder. The decoder is what we are using right now in GPT and so

02:30:38 --> 02:30:42
forth. The encoder is more like a

02:30:42 --> 02:30:46
parallel technique where you have multiple tokens that you

02:30:45 --> 02:30:50
fill in in parallel. GPT models, they do autoregressive generation,

02:30:49 --> 02:30:54
completing the sentence one token at a time. And in

02:30:53 --> 02:30:58
BERT models, you have a sentence that has gaps.

02:30:57 --> 02:31:02
You mask them out, and then one iteration is filling in

02:31:01 --> 02:31:05
these gaps. Text diffusion is kind of like that, where you

02:31:04 --> 02:31:09
are starting with some random text, and then you are

02:31:08 --> 02:31:13
filling in the missing parts or refining them iteratively over multiple iterations.

02:31:12 --> 02:31:17
And the cool thing here is that this can do

02:31:16 --> 02:31:19
multiple tokens at the same time. It's like

02:31:19 --> 02:31:24
the promise of having it more efficient. Now, the trade-off is, of course,

02:31:23 --> 02:31:28
how good is the quality? It might be faster, and now

02:31:27 --> 02:31:32
you have this dimension of the denoising process. The more steps you do, the better the text

02:31:31 --> 02:31:35
becomes. And people...

02:31:34 --> 02:31:39
I mean, you can scale in different ways. They try to see if that is

02:31:38 --> 02:31:43
maybe a valid alternative to the autoregressive model in

02:31:42 --> 02:31:47
terms of giving you the same quality for less compute.

02:31:46 --> 02:31:51
Right now, there are papers that suggest if you want to get the same

02:31:50 --> 02:31:55
quality, you have to crank up the denoising steps, and then

02:31:54 --> 02:31:58
you end up spending the same compute you would spend on an autoregressive model.

02:31:58 --> 02:32:02
The other downside is, while being parallel sounds appealing,

02:32:01 --> 02:32:06
some tasks are not parallel. Like reasoning tasks or tool use,

02:32:06 --> 02:32:10
maybe where you have to ask a code interpreter to give you

02:32:09 --> 02:32:14
an intermediate result. That is tricky with diffusion models. So, there are some

02:32:13 --> 02:32:18
hybrids, but the main idea is: how can we parallelize it? It's an

02:32:17 --> 02:32:21
interesting avenue. I think right now, there are mostly

02:32:20 --> 02:32:25
research models out there, like LaMDA and some other ones.

02:32:24 --> 02:32:29
I saw some by startups, some deployed models. There is no

02:32:28 --> 02:32:33
big diffusion model at scale yet, like on the Gemini or ChatGPT level.

02:32:32 --> 02:32:37
But there was an announcement by Google,

02:32:36 --> 02:32:41
a site where they said they are launching Gemini Diffusion, and they

02:32:40 --> 02:32:45
put it into context of their Gemini Nano 2

02:32:44 --> 02:32:48
model, and they said basically: for the same quality on most

02:32:47 --> 02:32:52
benchmarks, we can generate things much faster. You

02:32:51 --> 02:32:55
mentioned what's next. I don't think the text diffusion model is going to replace

02:32:54 --> 02:32:59
autoregressive LLMs, but it will be something maybe for

02:32:58 --> 02:33:03
quick, cheap, at-scale tasks. Maybe the free

02:33:02 --> 02:33:08
tier in the future will be something like that.

02:33:08 --> 02:33:12
- I think there are examples where it's already being used. To paint an example of why this is

02:33:11 --> 02:33:16
better, for example, when GPT-5 is taking 30 minutes to

02:33:15 --> 02:33:20
respond, it's generating one token at a time. And this diffusion idea is

02:33:19 --> 02:33:24
essentially to generate all of those tokens and the completion in

02:33:23 --> 02:33:27
one batch, which is why it could be way faster. And I think it could be

02:33:26 --> 02:33:31
suited for... the startups I'm hearing about are code startups where you have a

02:33:30 --> 02:33:35
code base, and you have somebody that's effectively "vibe coding," and they say,

02:33:34 --> 02:33:38
"Make this change." And a code diff is essentially a

02:33:37 --> 02:33:42
huge reply from the model, but it doesn't have to have that

02:33:41 --> 02:33:46
much external context, and you can get it really fast by using these diffusion models.

02:33:45 --> 02:33:50
One example I've heard is that they use text diffusion to generate

02:33:49 --> 02:33:54
really long diffs, because doing it with an autoregressive model would take

02:33:53 --> 02:33:58
minutes, and that time for a user-facing product causes a lot of churn.

02:33:57 --> 02:34:02
Every second, you lose a lot of users. So, I think it's going to be this

02:34:01 --> 02:34:03
thing where it's going to—

02:34:02 --> 02:34:07
...grow and have some applications, but I actually thought that different types of models

02:34:06 --> 02:34:11
were going to be used for different things much sooner than they have been,

02:34:10 --> 02:34:15
so I kind of trade off. I think the tool-use point is the one that's stopping

02:34:14 --> 02:34:22
them from being most general purpose because, for Claude Code and ChatGPT search,

02:34:22 --> 02:34:26
the autoregressive chain is interrupted with some external tool, and

02:34:25 --> 02:34:29
I don't know how to do that with the diffusion setup.

02:34:28 --> 02:34:33
- So what's the future of tool use this year and then in the coming years? Do you

02:34:32 --> 02:34:37
think there's going to be a lot of developments there, and how that's integrated into the entire

02:34:36 --> 02:34:38
stack?

02:34:37 --> 02:34:42
- I do think right now, it's mostly on the proprietary LLM side,

02:34:41 --> 02:34:46
but I think we will see more of that in the open-source tooling. And I

02:34:45 --> 02:34:49
think it is a huge unlock because then you can really

02:34:48 --> 02:34:53
outsource certain tasks from just memorization to actual—

02:34:53 --> 02:34:58
you know, instead of having the LLM memorize what is 23 plus 5,

02:34:57 --> 02:34:59
just use a calculator.

02:34:58 --> 02:35:02
- So do you think that can help solve hallucination?

02:35:01 --> 02:35:06
- Not solve it, but reduce it. So the LLM still needs to know

02:35:06 --> 02:35:10
hen to ask for a tool call. And the

02:35:09 --> 02:35:14
second one is, well, it doesn't mean the internet is always correct. You can do

02:35:13 --> 02:35:18
a web search, but let's say I asked who won the World Cup

02:35:17 --> 02:35:22
in, let's say, 1998; it still needs to find the right website and get the right

02:35:21 --> 02:35:25
information. You can still go to the incorrect website and give me incorrect

02:35:24 --> 02:35:29
information. So I don't think it will fully solve that, but it is

02:35:28 --> 02:35:31
improving it in that sense.

02:35:31 --> 02:35:36
And so another cool paper earlier this year—I think it was

02:35:35 --> 02:35:39
December 31st, so it's not technically 2026,

02:35:38 --> 02:35:44
but close—the recursive language model.

02:35:43 --> 02:35:48
That's a cool idea to kind of take this even a bit further.

02:35:47 --> 02:35:51
Just to explain, Nathan, you also mentioned earlier,

02:35:51 --> 02:35:56
it's harder to do cool research in academia because of the compute budget. If I

02:35:55 --> 02:36:00
recall correctly, they did everything with GPT-5, so they didn't even use local models,

02:35:59 --> 02:36:03
but the idea is, let's say you have a long-context task; instead of having the

02:36:03 --> 02:36:07
LLM solve all of it in one shot or even in a chain,

02:36:06 --> 02:36:11
you break it down into sub-tasks. You have the LLM decide

02:36:10 --> 02:36:14
what is a good sub-task, and then

02:36:14 --> 02:36:19
recursively call an LLM to solve that. And I think something like

02:36:18 --> 02:36:23
that, adding tools—you know, each one maybe you have

02:36:22 --> 02:36:26
a huge Q&A task, so each one goes to the web and gathers

02:36:25 --> 02:36:30
information, and then you pull it together at the end and stitch it back together.

02:36:29 --> 02:36:35
I think there's going to be a lot of unlock using things like that where

02:36:35 --> 02:36:40
you don't necessarily improve the LLM itself; you improve how the LLM is

02:36:39 --> 02:36:44
used and what the LLM can use. One downside right now with tool use

02:36:43 --> 02:36:47
is you have to give the LLM permission to use tools. And

02:36:47 --> 02:36:51
that will take some trust, especially if you want to unlock things

02:36:51 --> 02:36:55
like having an LLM answer emails for you—or not even answer, but just

02:36:54 --> 02:36:59
sort them for you or select them for you or something like that. I don't know if I would

02:36:58 --> 02:37:04
today give an LLM access to my emails, right? I mean, this is a huge risk.

02:37:03 --> 02:37:08
- I think there's a cool... one last point on the tool use thing. I think that

02:37:07 --> 02:37:12
you hinted at this, and we've both come at this in our own ways, is that the open versus

02:37:12 --> 02:37:16
closed models use tools in very different ways, where open models, people go to

02:37:16 --> 02:37:20
Hugging Face and download the model, and then the person's going to be like, "What tool do I want?"

02:37:20 --> 02:37:25
I don't know, Exa is my preferred search provider, but somebody else might care

02:37:24 --> 02:37:29
for a different search startup. Where you release a model, it needs to be useful for multiple

02:37:28 --> 02:37:32
tools, for multiple use cases, which is really hard because you're making a

02:37:31 --> 02:37:36
general reasoning engine model, which is actually what gpt-oss-120b is

02:37:35 --> 02:37:40
good for. But on the closed models, you're deeply integrating

02:37:40 --> 02:37:44
the specific tool into your experience, and I think that open models will

02:37:44 --> 02:37:48
struggle to replicate some of the things that I like to do with closed

02:37:47 --> 02:37:52
models, which will be like, you can reference a mix of public and private

02:37:51 --> 02:37:56
information. And something that I keep trying every three to six months,

02:37:55 --> 02:38:00
I try Claude Code on the web, which is just prompting a model to

02:37:59 --> 02:38:04
make an update to some GitHub repository that I have. And it's just like

02:38:03 --> 02:38:08
that set of secure cloud environments is just so nice for

02:38:07 --> 02:38:11
just sending it off to do this thing and then come back to

02:38:10 --> 02:38:17
me, and these will probably help define some of the local open and closed

02:38:18 --> 02:38:22
niches. But I think initially, because there was such a rush to get tool use

02:38:22 --> 02:38:26
working, the open models were on the back foot, which is kind of inevitable. I think there's so

02:38:26 --> 02:38:30
much research, so many resources in these frontier labs, but it will be

02:38:30 --> 02:38:34
fun when the open models solve this because it's going to necessitate a bit

02:38:33 --> 02:38:38
more flexible and potentially interesting model that might work with this recursive

02:38:37 --> 02:38:42
idea to be an orchestrator and a tool use model,

02:38:41 --> 02:38:45
so hopefully the necessity drives some interesting innovation there.

02:38:45 --> 02:38:49
- So, continual learning—this is a longstanding

02:38:50 --> 02:38:54
topic, important problem. I think that increases in importance as

02:38:53 --> 02:38:58
the cost of training the models goes up. So can you explain what

02:38:57 --> 02:39:02
continual learning is and how important it might be this year and in the coming

02:39:01 --> 02:39:03
years to make progress?

02:39:03 --> 02:39:07
- This relates a lot to this kind of SF zeitgeist of, what is

02:39:06 --> 02:39:11
AGI, which is Artificial General Intelligence, and what is

02:39:10 --> 02:39:14
ASI, Artificial Superintelligence, and what are the language models that we

02:39:13 --> 02:39:18
have today capable of doing? I think the language models can solve a lot

02:39:17 --> 02:39:22
of tasks, but a key milestone among the AI community is

02:39:21 --> 02:39:26
essentially when AI could replace any remote worker,

02:39:25 --> 02:39:30
taking in information and solving digital tasks and doing them. And

02:39:29 --> 02:39:33
the limitation that's highlighted by people is

02:39:32 --> 02:39:37
that a language model will not learn from feedback the same way that an

02:39:36 --> 02:39:41
employee does. So if you hire an editor, the editor will mess up, but

02:39:40 --> 02:39:45
you will tell them. And if you hired a good editor, they don't do it again. But language models don't

02:39:44 --> 02:39:45
have this

02:39:45 --> 02:39:50
ability to modify themselves and learn very quickly. So the idea is, if we are going to

02:39:49 --> 02:39:54
actually get to something that is a true, general adaptable

02:39:53 --> 02:39:57
intelligence that can go into any remote work scenario, it needs to be able to learn

02:39:56 --> 02:40:00
quickly from feedback and on-the-job learning.

02:40:00 --> 02:40:05
I'm personally more bullish on language models being able to just provide

02:40:04 --> 02:40:09
them with very good context. You said, maybe offline, that

02:40:08 --> 02:40:12
you can write extensive documents to models where you say,

02:40:11 --> 02:40:16
"I have all this information. Here are all the blog posts I've ever written.

02:40:15 --> 02:40:20
I like this type of writing. My voice is based on this." But many people don't provide

02:40:19 --> 02:40:24
this to models, and the models weren't designed to take this amount

02:40:23 --> 02:40:28
of context previously. Agentic models are just starting. So it's

02:40:27 --> 02:40:32
this kind of trade-off: do we need to update the weights of this model

02:40:31 --> 02:40:35
with this continual learning thing to make them learn fast? Or the

02:40:34 --> 02:40:39
counterargument is we just need to provide them with more context and information,

02:40:38 --> 02:40:43
and they will have the appearance of learning fast by having a lot of context and being

02:40:42 --> 02:40:44
smart.

02:40:43 --> 02:40:47
- So we should mention the terminology here. Continual learning refers to

02:40:46 --> 02:40:53
changing the weights continuously so that the model adapts and adjusts

02:40:53 --> 02:40:57
based on the new incoming

02:40:56 --> 02:41:01
information, doing so continually, rapidly, and frequently.

02:41:00 --> 02:41:04
And then the thing you mentioned on the other side of it

02:41:04 --> 02:41:09
generally will be referred to as in-context learning. As

02:41:08 --> 02:41:13
you learn stuff, there's a huge context window. You can

02:41:12 --> 02:41:16
just keep loading it with extra information every time you prompt the system,

02:41:15 --> 02:41:22
which I think both legitimately can be seen as learning.

02:41:21 --> 02:41:25
It's just a different place where you're doing the learning.

02:41:24 --> 02:41:29
- I think, to be honest with you, continual learning — updating

02:41:28 --> 02:41:32
weights — we already have that in different flavors. If you think about

02:41:31 --> 02:41:36
how... I think the distinction here is: do you

02:41:35 --> 02:41:40
do that on a personalized custom model for each person, or

02:41:39 --> 02:41:44
do you do it on a global model scale? I think we have that already,

02:41:43 --> 02:41:48
going from GPT-5 to 5.1 and 5.2. It's

02:41:47 --> 02:41:52
maybe not immediate, but it is a curated update, a quick curated update

02:41:51 --> 02:41:56
where there was feedback about things they couldn't do, feedback by the

02:41:55 --> 02:41:59
community. They updated the weights, next model, and so forth. So it

02:41:58 --> 02:42:07
is a flavor of that. Another even finer-grained example is like

02:42:06 --> 02:42:11
RLVR; you run it, it updates. The problem is

02:42:10 --> 02:42:14
you can't just do that for each person because it would be too expensive to update the

02:42:14 --> 02:42:18
weights for each person, and I think that's the problem. Unless you get...

02:42:17 --> 02:42:22
Even at OpenAI scale, building the data centers, it would be too

02:42:21 --> 02:42:26
expensive. I think that is only feasible once you have something

02:42:25 --> 02:42:30
on the device where the cost is on the consumer. Like what Apple tried to do with the

02:42:29 --> 02:42:34
Apple Foundation models, putting them on the phone, where they learn from

02:42:33 --> 02:42:34
experience.

02:42:33 --> 02:42:42
- A bit of a related topic, but this kind of, maybe anthropomorphized term: memory.

02:42:42 --> 02:42:47
What are different ideas for the mechanism of how to add memory to these systems as we're

02:42:46 --> 02:42:50
increasingly seeing? Personalized memory especially?

02:42:49 --> 02:42:53
- Right now, it's mostly basically

02:42:52 --> 02:42:57
stuffing things into the context and then just recalling that.

02:42:56 --> 02:43:05
But again, I think it's expensive because you have to—you can cache it, but still

02:43:04 --> 02:43:09
you spend tokens on that. And the second one is you can only do

02:43:08 --> 02:43:13
so much. I think it's more like a preference or style. I mean, a lot of people do

02:43:12 --> 02:43:17
that when they solve math problems. You say it's way so you

02:43:16 --> 02:43:21
can add previous knowledge and stuff, but you also give it certain preference

02:43:20 --> 02:43:24
prompts: "do what I preferred last time," or something like that. But

02:43:23 --> 02:43:32
it doesn't unlock new capabilities. So for that, one thing people still use is

02:43:31 --> 02:43:35
LoRA adapters. These are basically, instead of updating the whole weight

02:43:34 --> 02:43:39
matrix, there are two smaller weight matrices that you kind of have

02:43:38 --> 02:43:43
in parallel or overlays like the delta. But yeah, you can

02:43:43 --> 02:43:46
do that to some extent, but then again,

02:43:45 --> 02:43:50
it is economics. There were also papers, for

02:43:49 --> 02:43:54
example, LoRA learns less but forgets less.

02:43:53 --> 02:43:58
It's like, there's no free lunch. If you want to learn more, you need to use more

02:43:57 --> 02:44:02
weights, but it gets more expensive. And then again, if you learn more, you forget

02:44:01 --> 02:44:05
more, and you have to find that Goldilocks zone basically.

02:44:04 --> 02:44:09
- We haven't really mentioned it much, but implied in this discussion is

02:44:08 --> 02:44:14
context length also. Is there a lot of innovation that's possible there?

02:44:13 --> 02:44:18
- I think the colloquially accepted thing is that it's a compute and data

02:44:17 --> 02:44:22
problem where you can... and sometimes small

02:44:21 --> 02:44:25
architecture things like attention variants. We talked about

02:44:24 --> 02:44:29
hybrid attention models, which is essentially if you have what

02:44:28 --> 02:44:33
looks like a state space model within your transformer. And those are

02:44:32 --> 02:44:37
better suited because you have to spend less compute to model the

02:44:37 --> 02:44:41
furthest along token. I think that,

02:44:41 --> 02:44:46
those aren't free because they have to be accompanied by a lot of compute or

02:44:47 --> 02:44:52
the right data. How many sequences of 100,000 tokens do you have in

02:44:51 --> 02:44:55
the world, and where do you get these? It just ends up being pretty

02:44:54 --> 02:44:59
expensive to scale them. We've gotten pretty quickly to a million tokens

02:44:58 --> 02:45:03
of input context length. I would expect it to keep increasing and

02:45:02 --> 02:45:07
get to 2 million or 5 million this year, but I don't expect it to go to 100

02:45:06 --> 02:45:11
million. That would be like a true breakthrough, and I think those

02:45:10 --> 02:45:15
breakthroughs are possible. I think of the continual learning thing as a research problem where

02:45:14 --> 02:45:19
there could be a breakthrough that just makes transformers work way

02:45:18 --> 02:45:23
better at this and it's cheap. These things could happen with so much scientific

02:45:22 --> 02:45:28
attention. But turning the crank, it'll be consistent increases over time.

02:45:27 --> 02:45:32
- Looking at the extremes, I think there's, again, no free lunch. So, the one

02:45:31 --> 02:45:36
xtreme to make it cheap: you have, let's say, an RNN that has a single state

02:45:35 --> 02:45:39
where you save everything from the previous stuff. It's like a

02:45:39 --> 02:45:44
specific fixed-size thing, so you never really grow the

02:45:43 --> 02:45:47
memory because you are stuffing everything into one state, but

02:45:46 --> 02:45:51
hen the longer the context gets, the more information you forget because

02:45:50 --> 02:45:55
you can't compress everything into one state. Then on the

02:45:54 --> 02:45:59
other hand, you have the transformers, which try to remember every token, which is great

02:45:58 --> 02:46:03
sometimes if you want to look up specific information, but very expensive because you have the

02:46:02 --> 02:46:07
KV cache that grows, the dot product that grows. But

02:46:06 --> 02:46:11
hen, like you said, the Mamba layers—I mean, they kind of have the same problem.

02:46:10 --> 02:46:15
Like an RNN, you try to compress everything into one state; you're a bit more selective

02:46:14 --> 02:46:18
there. But then I think it's like this Goldilocks zone again. With

02:46:18 --> 02:46:22
Nemotron 3, they found a good ratio of how many attention

02:46:21 --> 02:46:26
layers do you need for the global information where everything is accessible

02:46:25 --> 02:46:29
compared to having these compressed states. And I think that's

02:46:28 --> 02:46:33
how we will scale more—by finding better,

02:46:32 --> 02:46:37
let's say, ratios in the Goldilocks zone, like between

02:46:37 --> 02:46:42
making computing cheap enough to run, but then also making

02:46:41 --> 02:46:46
it powerful enough to be useful. And one more plug here,

02:46:45 --> 02:46:49
the Recursive Language Model paper, that is one of the

02:46:48 --> 02:46:53
papers that tries to kind of address the long context thing. So what they found

02:46:52 --> 02:46:57
is essentially instead of stuffing everything into this long context

02:46:57 --> 02:47:01
if you break it up into multiple smaller tasks, so you

02:47:00 --> 02:47:05
save memory by having multiple smaller cores, you can actually get

02:47:04 --> 02:47:09
better accuracy than having the LLM try everything all at once.

02:47:08 --> 02:47:13
I mean, it's a new paradigm. We will see, you know, there might be other flavors of

02:47:12 --> 02:47:17
that. So I think with that, we will still make improvement on

02:47:16 --> 02:47:21
long context, but then also, like Nathan said, I think the problem is for

02:47:20 --> 02:47:25
pre-training itself, we don't have as many long context documents as other

02:47:24 --> 02:47:29
documents. So it's harder to study basically

02:47:28 --> 02:47:32
how LLMs behave and stuff like that on that level.

02:47:31 --> 02:47:36
- There are some rules of thumb where essentially you pre-train a language model, like OLMo.

02:47:35 --> 02:47:39
we pre-trained at like 8K context length and then extended to

02:47:38 --> 02:47:43
32K with training. And there are some rules of thumb where you're essentially doubling

02:47:42 --> 02:47:47
the training context length, it takes like 2X compute, and then you can

02:47:46 --> 02:47:51
ormally like 2 to 4X the context length again.

02:47:50 --> 02:47:55
So I think a lot of it ends up being kind of compute bound at pre-training,

02:47:54 --> 02:47:59
which is in this... Like we talked about, everyone talks about this big increase in

02:47:58 --> 02:48:02
compute for the top labs this year, and that should reflect in some longer context

02:48:01 --> 02:48:06
windows. But I think on the post-training side, there are some more interesting things. As we have

02:48:05 --> 02:48:10
agents, the agents are gonna manage this context on their own, where now

02:48:09 --> 02:48:14
agents, people that use Claude Code a lot dread the compaction, which is when Claude takes its

02:48:13 --> 02:48:18
entire full 100,000 tokens of work and compacts it into a bulleted

02:48:17 --> 02:48:22
list. But what the next models will do—and I'm sure

02:48:21 --> 02:48:26
people are already working on this—is essentially the model can control when it

02:48:25 --> 02:48:30
compacts and how. So you can essentially train your RL algorithm where

02:48:29 --> 02:48:31
compaction is an action-

02:48:30 --> 02:48:35
...where it shortens the history and then the problem formulation will be, "I

02:48:34 --> 02:48:39
want to keep the maximum evaluation scores that I have gotten

02:48:39 --> 02:48:44
while the model compacts its history to the minimum length." Because then you

02:48:43 --> 02:48:48
have the minimum amount of tokens that you need to do this kind of compounding

02:48:47 --> 02:48:52
autoregressive prediction. So there are actually pretty nice problem setups in this, where

02:48:51 --> 02:48:55
the... Like these agentic models learn to use their context in a

02:48:54 --> 02:48:57
different way than just plow forward.

02:48:56 --> 02:49:01
- One interesting recent example would be DeepSeek-V3.2,

02:49:00 --> 02:49:05
where they had a sparse attention mechanism where they have

02:49:04 --> 02:49:09
ssentially a very efficient, small, lightweight indexer. And

02:49:08 --> 02:49:13
instead of attending to all tokens, it selects: "What tokens do I actually need?"

02:49:12 --> 02:49:17
I mean, it almost comes back to the original idea of

02:49:16 --> 02:49:21
attention where you are selective, but attention is always on, you have maybe zero

02:49:20 --> 02:49:25
weight on some of them, but you use them all. But they are even more like, "Let's just mask that out

02:49:24 --> 02:49:28
or not even do that."

02:49:28 --> 02:49:32
And even with sliding window attention in OLMo, that is also kind of that idea.

02:49:32 --> 02:49:36
You have a rolling window where you keep it fixed, because you don't need everything.

02:49:35 --> 02:49:40
Occasionally, some layers you might, but it's wasteful. But right now, I think,

02:49:39 --> 02:49:44
if you use everything, you're on the safe side—it gives you the best bang for the buck

02:49:43 --> 02:49:48
because you never miss information. And I think this year will be more

02:49:47 --> 02:49:52
about figuring out, like you said, how to be smarter about

02:49:51 --> 02:49:56
hat. Right now, people want to have the next state-of-the-art, and the

02:49:55 --> 02:49:59
state-of-the-art happens to be the brute-force, expensive

02:49:58 --> 02:50:03
thing. And then once you have that, as you said, keep that

02:50:02 --> 02:50:08
accuracy, but let's see how we can do that cheaper now, with tricks.

02:50:07 --> 02:50:09
- Yeah. All this scaling thing.

02:50:08 --> 02:50:13
The reason we get the Claude 4.5 Sonnet model first is

02:50:12 --> 02:50:16
because you can train it faster and you're not hitting these compute walls as soon.

02:50:16 --> 02:50:20
They can just try a lot more things and get the model faster, even though the bigger

02:50:19 --> 02:50:23
model is actually better.

02:50:22 --> 02:50:26
- I think we should say that there's a lot of exciting stuff going on in the AI space.

02:50:25 --> 02:50:30
My mind has recently been really focused on

02:50:29 --> 02:50:33
robotics. Today, we almost entirely didn't talk

02:50:32 --> 02:50:37
about robotics. There's a lot of stuff on image generation,

02:50:36 --> 02:50:41
video generation. I think it's fair

02:50:40 --> 02:50:45
to say that the most exciting research work in terms of the

02:50:44 --> 02:50:49
amount, intensity, and fervor is in the

02:50:48 --> 02:50:52
LLM space, which is why I think it's justified for us to focus on the

02:50:51 --> 02:50:56
LLMs that we're discussing. But it'd be nice to bring in

02:50:55 --> 02:51:00
certain things that might be useful. For example, world models—

02:50:59 --> 02:51:04
there's growing excitement on that. Do you think there will be any

02:51:03 --> 02:51:07
use in this coming year for world models in the LLM space?

02:51:06 --> 02:51:11
Yes, I do think so. Also with LLMs,

02:51:10 --> 02:51:15
what's interesting here is that if we unlock more LLM

02:51:14 --> 02:51:19
capabilities, it also automatically unlocks all the other fields because

02:51:18 --> 02:51:25
it makes progress faster. A lot of researchers and engineers use LLMs for

02:51:24 --> 02:51:29
coding. So even if they work on robotics, if you optimize these LLMs

02:51:28 --> 02:51:33
that help with coding, it pays off. But then,

02:51:32 --> 02:51:36
yes, world models are interesting. It's basically where you

02:51:35 --> 02:51:40
have the model run a simulation of the world in a sense, like a

02:51:39 --> 02:51:44
little toy thing of the real thing, which can, again,

02:51:43 --> 02:51:48
unlock capabilities regarding data the LLM is

02:51:47 --> 02:51:55
not aware of. It can simulate things. And I think LLMs happen to work

02:51:55 --> 02:51:59
well by pre-training and doing next-token prediction.

02:51:59 --> 02:52:04
But we could do this even more sophisticatedly in a

02:52:03 --> 02:52:08
sense. I think there was a paper by

02:52:07 --> 02:52:11
Meta, a paper called World Models. So where they

02:52:10 --> 02:52:15
basically apply the concept of world models to LLMs again,

02:52:14 --> 02:52:18
where instead of just having next-token prediction and

02:52:18 --> 02:52:22
verifiable rewards, checking the answer correctness, they also make sure the

02:52:21 --> 02:52:26
intermediate variables are correct. You know, it's kind of like

02:52:25 --> 02:52:29
the model is learning basically a code environment in a sense. And I

02:52:28 --> 02:52:33
think this makes a lot of sense. It's just expensive to do,

02:52:32 --> 02:52:39
but it is making things more sophisticated, like modeling

02:52:40 --> 02:52:44
the whole thing, not just the result. And so it can

02:52:44 --> 02:52:52
add more value. I remember when I was a grad student, there is a...

02:52:51 --> 02:52:56
competition called CASP, I think, where they do protein

02:52:55 --> 02:53:00
structure prediction. They predict the structure of a protein

02:52:59 --> 02:53:04
that is not solved yet at that point. So in a

02:53:03 --> 02:53:08
sense, this is actually great, and I think we need something like that for LLMs also,

02:53:07 --> 02:53:12
where you do the benchmark, but no one does. You hand in the results, but no one

02:53:11 --> 02:53:15
knows the solution. And then after the fact, someone reveals that. But,

02:53:15 --> 02:53:20
AlphaFold, when it came out, it crushed this

02:53:19 --> 02:53:24
benchmark. I mean, there were also multiple

02:53:23 --> 02:53:28
iterations, but I remember the first one. I'm not an expert in

02:53:27 --> 02:53:31
that subject, but the first one explicitly modeled the physical

02:53:31 --> 02:53:35
interactions of the... You know, the physics of the molecule.

02:53:34 --> 02:53:39
Also the angles, impossible angles. And then in the next version, I think they got rid of

02:53:38 --> 02:53:43
this, and just with brute force, scaling it up. And I think with LLMs, we are

02:53:42 --> 02:53:47
currently in this brute force scaling because it just happens to work. But I do think at some

02:53:46 --> 02:53:50
point it might make sense to bring back this

02:53:49 --> 02:53:54
thing. And I think with world models, I think that is

02:53:53 --> 02:53:58
where I think that might be actually quite cool. I mean, yeah.

02:53:57 --> 02:54:04
And of course, also for robotics, which is completely unrelated to LLMs.

02:54:03 --> 02:54:08
- Yeah. And robotics is very explicit. So there's the problem of locomotion

02:54:07 --> 02:54:09
or manipulation.

02:54:08 --> 02:54:12
Locomotion is much more solved, especially in the learning domain. But there's a

02:54:12 --> 02:54:15
lot of value, just like with the initial protein folding systems,

02:54:14 --> 02:54:21
bringing in the traditional model-based methods. So you don't... it's unlikely

02:54:21 --> 02:54:26
that you can just learn the manipulation or the whole body, local

02:54:25 --> 02:54:28
manipulation problem end to end.

02:54:28 --> 02:54:34
That's the dream. But then you realize when you look at the magic of the human hand

02:54:33 --> 02:54:38
the complexity of the real world, it's really hard to learn this all the

02:54:37 --> 02:54:41
way through, the way I guess AlphaFold 2 didn't.

02:54:40 --> 02:54:45
- I'm excited about the robotic learning space. I think it's collectively

02:54:44 --> 02:54:49
getting supercharged by all the excitement and investment in

02:54:48 --> 02:54:52
language models generally, where the infrastructure for training

02:54:51 --> 02:54:56
transformers, which is a general modeling thing, is becoming

02:54:55 --> 02:55:00
world-class industrial tooling,

02:54:59 --> 02:55:04
where wherever there was a limitation for robotics, it's just way better.

02:55:03 --> 02:55:08
There's way more compute. And then on top of that, they take these language models

02:55:07 --> 02:55:12
as kind of central units where you can do interesting explorative work around

02:55:11 --> 02:55:15
something that already works. And then I see it

02:55:14 --> 02:55:19
emerging as, kind of like we talked about, Hugging Face transformers and

02:55:18 --> 02:55:23
Hugging Face. I think when I was at Hugging Face, I was trying to get this to happen, but it was too early.

02:55:22 --> 02:55:26
It's like these open robotic models on Hugging

02:55:25 --> 02:55:30
Face, and having people be able to contribute data and fine-tune them.

02:55:29 --> 02:55:34
I think we're much closer now that the investment in robotics and self-driving

02:55:33 --> 02:55:38
cars is related and it enables this, where once you get to the point

02:55:37 --> 02:55:42
where you can have this sort of ecosystem where somebody can download a robotics model and

02:55:41 --> 02:55:46
maybe fine-tune it to their robot or share datasets across the world.

02:55:45 --> 02:55:50
There's some work in this area like RTX, I think it was

02:55:49 --> 02:55:54
