a few years ago, where people are starting to do that. But once they have this ecosystem,

02:55:53 --> 02:55:56
it'll look very different. And then this whole

02:55:56 --> 02:56:01
post-ChatGPT boom is putting more resources into that, which I think

02:56:00 --> 02:56:03
is a very good area for doing research.

02:56:02 --> 02:56:07
- This is also resulting in much better, more accurate, and more

02:56:06 --> 02:56:11
alistic simulators being built, closing the sim-to-real gap in the robotic

02:56:10 --> 02:56:15
space. But, you know, you mentioned a lot of excitement in the

02:56:14 --> 02:56:18
robotics space and a lot of investment. The downside of

02:56:17 --> 02:56:22
that, which happens in hype cycles, I personally

02:56:21 --> 02:56:25
believe, and most robotics people believe, that it's not...

02:56:24 --> 02:56:29
Robotics is not going to be solved at the

02:56:28 --> 02:56:33
time scale as being implicitly or explicitly

02:56:32 --> 02:56:37
promised. And so what happens when there's all these robotics companies

02:56:36 --> 02:56:43
that spring up and then they don't have a product that works? Then there's

02:56:42 --> 02:56:46
going to be this kind of crash of excitement, which is

02:56:45 --> 02:56:50
nerve-wracking. Hopefully something else will come in and keep swooping

02:56:49 --> 02:56:54
in so that the continued development of some of these ideas keeps going.

02:56:53 --> 02:56:58
- I think it's also related to the continual learning issue, essentially, where

02:56:57 --> 02:57:02
the real world is so complex. With LLMs,

02:57:01 --> 02:57:06
you don't really need to have something learn for the user,

02:57:05 --> 02:57:10
because there are a lot of things everyone has to do. Everyone maybe wants

02:57:09 --> 02:57:14
to, I don't know, fix their grammar in their email or

02:57:13 --> 02:57:18
code or something like that. It's more constrained, so you can kind of prepare the model for

02:57:17 --> 02:57:21
that. But preparing the robot for the real world is harder. I mean, you

02:57:20 --> 02:57:25
have the robotic foundation models, and you can learn

02:57:24 --> 02:57:29
certain things like grasping things. But then again,

02:57:28 --> 02:57:33
everyone's house is different. It's so different, and that is,

02:57:32 --> 02:57:37
I think, where the robot would have to learn on the job, essentially.

02:57:36 --> 02:57:40
And that, I guess, is the bottleneck right now: how to,

02:57:39 --> 02:57:43
customize it on the fly, essentially.

02:57:42 --> 02:57:47
- I don't think I can possibly understate the

02:57:46 --> 02:57:51
importance of the thing that doesn't get talked about almost at all

02:57:50 --> 02:57:54
by robotics folks or anyone, which is safety.

02:57:53 --> 02:57:58
All the interesting complexities we talk about learning, all the failure modes and

02:57:57 --> 02:58:01
failure cases, everything we've been talking about with LLMs—sometimes they fail in

02:58:00 --> 02:58:06
teresting ways. All of that is fun and games in the LLM space.

02:58:06 --> 02:58:14
In the robotic space, in people's homes, across millions of minutes and billions

02:58:13 --> 02:58:18
of interactions, you really are almost allowed to fail

02:58:17 --> 02:58:22
never. When you have embodied systems that are

02:58:21 --> 02:58:26
put out there in the real world, you just have to solve so many

02:58:27 --> 02:58:32
problems you never thought you'd have to solve when just thinking about the general

02:58:31 --> 02:58:33
robot learning problem.

02:58:32 --> 02:58:37
- I'm so bearish on in-home learned

02:58:36 --> 02:58:41
robots for consumer purchase. I'm very bullish on self-driving cars, and I'm

02:58:40 --> 02:58:45
very bullish for robotic automation, e.g., like Amazon distribution

02:58:44 --> 02:58:49
where Amazon has built whole new distribution centers designed for robots

02:58:48 --> 02:58:53
first rather than humans. There's a lot of excitement in AI circles about

02:58:52 --> 02:58:55
AI enabling automation

02:58:54 --> 02:58:58
and mass-scale manufacturing, and I do think that the path to robots doing that is more reasonable,

02:58:57 --> 02:59:02
where it's a thing that is

02:59:01 --> 02:59:06
designed and optimized to do a repetitive task that a human could

02:59:05 --> 02:59:10
conceivably do but doesn't want to. And then I'm much,

02:59:09 --> 02:59:14
but it's also going to take a lot longer than people

02:59:13 --> 02:59:17
probably predict. I think the leap from

02:59:17 --> 02:59:21
AI singularity to we can now scale up mass manufacturing in the

02:59:20 --> 02:59:25
US because we have a massive AI advantage is one that is

02:59:25 --> 02:59:31
troubled by a lot of political and other challenging problems.

02:59:31 --> 02:59:38
- Let's talk about timelines, specifically timelines to AGI or

02:59:37 --> 02:59:44
ASI. Is it fair, as a starting point, to say that nobody really agrees on the

02:59:43 --> 02:59:47
definitions of AGI and ASI?

02:59:46 --> 02:59:49
- I kind of think there's a lot of disagreement, but

02:59:49 --> 02:59:54
I've been getting pushback where a lot of people kind of say the same thing, which is like a

02:59:53 --> 02:59:58
thing that could reproduce most digital economic

02:59:57 --> 03:00:01
work. So, the remote worker is a fairly reasonable

03:00:00 --> 03:00:05
xample. And I think OpenAI's definition is somewhat

03:00:04 --> 03:00:09
related to that, which is like an AI that can do a lot of

03:00:08 --> 03:00:13
economically valuable tasks—which I don't really love as a definition, but I think

03:00:12 --> 03:00:16
it could be a grounding point,

03:00:15 --> 03:00:20
because language models today, while immensely powerful,

03:00:19 --> 03:00:24
are not this remote worker drop-in. And there are things that

03:00:23 --> 03:00:27
could be done by an AI that are way harder than remote

03:00:26 --> 03:00:31
work, which are like finding an unexpected

03:00:30 --> 03:00:35
scientific discovery that you couldn't even posit, which would be an example of something that

03:00:34 --> 03:00:39
somebody says is an artificial superintelligence problem. Or,

03:00:38 --> 03:00:46
taking in all medical records and finding linkages across certain

03:00:45 --> 03:00:50
illnesses that people didn't know, or figuring out that some common drug can

03:00:49 --> 03:00:53
treat some niche cancer. They would say that that is a superintelligence

03:00:52 --> 03:00:57
thing. So these are kind of natural tiers. My problem with it is that it

03:00:57 --> 03:01:02
becomes deeply entwined with the quest for meaning of AI and these religious

03:01:01 --> 03:01:07
aspects to it. So there's different paths you can take it.

03:01:06 --> 03:01:11
- And I don't even know if the remote worker is a good definition

03:01:10 --> 03:01:15
because what exactly is that? I actually, I mean, I

03:01:14 --> 03:01:18
like... I don't know if you like the originally titled

03:01:17 --> 03:01:26
AI27 report. They focus more on code and research taste, so the target there is the

03:01:25 --> 03:01:29
superhuman coder. So they have several milestone systems:

03:01:28 --> 03:01:32
Superhuman coders, superhuman AI researcher,

03:01:31 --> 03:01:36
then superintelligent AI researcher, and then the full ASI,

03:01:35 --> 03:01:39
artificial superintelligence. But

03:01:39 --> 03:01:45
after you develop the superhuman coder, everything else follows quickly.

03:01:45 --> 03:01:51
There, the task is to have fully autonomous, automated coding.

03:01:52 --> 03:01:56
So any kind of coding you need to do in order to perform

03:01:55 --> 03:02:00
research is fully automated. And from there,

03:01:59 --> 03:02:04
humans would be doing AI research together with that system, and they will

03:02:03 --> 03:02:08
quickly be able to develop a system that can actually do the research for you.

03:02:07 --> 03:02:15
That's the idea. And initially their prediction was 2027, 2028, and now they've

03:02:15 --> 03:02:19
pushed it back by three to four years to

03:02:18 --> 03:02:22
2031 (mean prediction). Probably my prediction is even

03:02:21 --> 03:02:26
beyond 2031, but at least you can, in a

03:02:25 --> 03:02:32
concrete way, think about how difficult it is to fully automate programming.

03:02:31 --> 03:02:36
- Yeah, I disagree with some of their presumptions and dynamics on how

03:02:35 --> 03:02:40
it would play out, but I think they did good work in the

03:02:39 --> 03:02:44
scenario-defining milestones that are concrete and tell a useful story, which is

03:02:43 --> 03:02:47
why the reach for this AI 2027 document

03:02:46 --> 03:02:51
ranscended Silicon Valley. It's because they told a good story and they

03:02:50 --> 03:02:53
did a lot of rigorous work to do this.

03:02:53 --> 03:02:57
I think the camp that I fall into is that AI is so-called

03:02:56 --> 03:03:01
"jagged," which will be excellent at some things and really bad at some things.

03:03:00 --> 03:03:05
I think that when they're close to this

03:03:04 --> 03:03:09
automated software engineer, what it will be good at is traditional ML systems

03:03:08 --> 03:03:13
and frontend, the model is excellent at; but distributed ML,

03:03:12 --> 03:03:17
the models are actually quite bad at because there's so little training data

03:03:16 --> 03:03:21
on doing large-scale distributed learning. This is something we already see,

03:03:20 --> 03:03:24
and I think this will just get amplified. And then it's kind of messier in these

03:03:23 --> 03:03:28
trade-offs, like how you think AI research works and so on.

03:03:28 --> 03:03:32
- So you think basically a superhuman coder is almost unachievable,

03:03:31 --> 03:03:35
because of the jagged nature of the thing, you're just always going to

03:03:34 --> 03:03:39
have gaps in capabilities?

03:03:38 --> 03:03:43
- I think it's assigning completeness to something where the models are already

03:03:42 --> 03:03:47
superhuman at some types of code. I think that will continue.

03:03:46 --> 03:03:50
And people are creative, so they'll utilize these incredible abilities

03:03:50 --> 03:03:54
to fill in the weaknesses of the models and move really fast. There'll always

03:03:54 --> 03:03:58
be this dance for a long time between the humans

03:03:57 --> 03:04:02
enabling the thing that the model can't do. And the best AI researchers

03:04:01 --> 03:04:05
are the ones that can enable this superpower.

03:04:04 --> 03:04:09
And I think those lines lead to what we already see. I think like Claude Code for building a

03:04:08 --> 03:04:12
website, you can stand up a beautiful website in a few hours or do data

03:04:11 --> 03:04:16
going to keep getting better, and we'll pick up some new coding skills

03:04:15 --> 03:04:24
along the way. Linking to what's happening in big tech,

03:04:23 --> 03:04:28
this AI 2027 report leans into the

03:04:27 --> 03:04:31
singularity idea, whereas I think research is messy,

03:04:30 --> 03:04:35
social and largely in the data in ways that AI models can't

03:04:34 --> 03:04:39
process. But what we do have today is really powerful and these

03:04:39 --> 03:04:43
tech companies are all collectively buying into this with tens of billions of dollars of

03:04:42 --> 03:04:47
investment. So we are going to get some much better version

03:04:46 --> 03:04:51
of ChatGPT, a much better version of Claude Code than we already have.

03:04:50 --> 03:04:55
I think it's just hard to predict where that is going, but the

03:04:55 --> 03:05:00
bright clarity of that future is why some of the most powerful people in the world are

03:04:59 --> 03:05:03
putting so much money into this. And I think it's just kind of

03:05:03 --> 03:05:07
small differences between like—we don't actually know what a better version of

03:05:06 --> 03:05:14
ChatGPT is, but also, can it automate AI research? I would say probably not, at

03:05:13 --> 03:05:18
least in this timeframe. Big tech is going to spend $100 billion much

03:05:17 --> 03:05:23
faster than we get an automated AI researcher that enables an AI research singularity.

03:05:22 --> 03:05:27
- So you think your prediction would be— if this is

03:05:26 --> 03:05:31
even a useful milestone or more than 10 years out?

03:05:30 --> 03:05:35
- I would say less than that on the software side, but I think longer than that on

03:05:34 --> 03:05:37
things like research.

03:05:36 --> 03:05:43
- Well, let's just for fun try to imagine a world where all software writing

03:05:42 --> 03:05:46
is fully automated. Can you imagine that world?

03:05:46 --> 03:05:51
- By the end of this year, the amount of software that'll be automated will be so

03:05:50 --> 03:05:55
high. But it'll be things like trying to train a model with

03:05:54 --> 03:05:58
RL and you need to have multiple bunches of GPUs

03:05:57 --> 03:06:02
communicating with each other. That'll still be hard, but it'll be much easier.

03:06:02 --> 03:06:05
- One way to think about this— the full automation of

03:06:04 --> 03:06:13
programming—is just thinking of lines of useful code written, the fraction of

03:06:12 --> 03:06:17
that to the number of humans in the loop. So presumably there'll be

03:06:16 --> 03:06:21
for a long time humans in the loop of software writing. It'll just be fewer and

03:06:20 --> 03:06:25
fewer relative to the amount of code written. Right?

03:06:24 --> 03:06:29
And the superhuman coder—I think the presumption there is it

03:06:28 --> 03:06:33
goes to zero, the number of humans in the loop. What does that world

03:06:32 --> 03:06:37
look like when the number of humans in the loop is in the

03:06:36 --> 03:06:40
hundreds, not in the hundreds of thousands?

03:06:39 --> 03:06:43
- I think software engineering will be driven more to system design

03:06:42 --> 03:06:47
and goals of outcomes, where I do think software is largely going to

03:06:46 --> 03:06:51
be. I think this has been happening over the last few weeks, where people

03:06:50 --> 03:06:55
have gone from a month ago saying, "Oh yeah, agents are kind of slop," which

03:06:54 --> 03:07:01
is a famous Karpathy quote, to what is a little bit of a meme—the

03:07:00 --> 03:07:05
industrialization of software when anyone can just create software with their

03:07:04 --> 03:07:09
fingerprints. I do think we are closer to that side of things, and it takes

03:07:08 --> 03:07:12
direction and understanding how systems work

03:07:12 --> 03:07:16
to extract the best from the language models. I think it's hard to accept the

03:07:15 --> 03:07:20
gravity of how much is going to change with software development and how many

03:07:19 --> 03:07:23
more people can do things without ever looking at the code.

03:07:22 --> 03:07:27
- I think what's interesting is to think about whether these systems will be

03:07:26 --> 03:07:30
independent—completely independent in the sense that, while I have no doubt

03:07:29 --> 03:07:34
hat LLMs will kind of at some point solve coding in a sense,

03:07:33 --> 03:07:38
like calculators solve calculating, right? So at some point, humans

03:07:37 --> 03:07:42
developed a tool where you never need a human to calculate that number.

03:07:41 --> 03:07:45
You just type it in, and it's an algorithm. You can do it in

03:07:45 --> 03:07:50
that sense. And I think that's the same probably for coding. But

03:07:49 --> 03:07:54
he question isn't... I think what will happen is, you will just say, "Build that website."

03:07:53 --> 03:07:58
It will make a really good website, and then you maybe refine it. But will it

03:07:57 --> 03:08:04
do things independently where... Will you still be having humans asking

03:08:03 --> 03:08:08
the AI to do something? Like will there be a person to say, "Build that

03:08:07 --> 03:08:13
website?" Or will there be AI that just builds websites or something?

03:08:12 --> 03:08:16
- I think talking about building websites is—

03:08:15 --> 03:08:17
- Too simple.

03:08:16 --> 03:08:21
- The problem with websites and the problem with the web,

03:08:20 --> 03:08:26
you know, HTML and all that kind of stuff, it's very resilient to just ... slop.

03:08:25 --> 03:08:30
It will show you slop; it's good at showing slop. I would rather

03:08:30 --> 03:08:37
think of safety-critical systems, like asking AI to end-to-end generate

03:08:37 --> 03:08:42
something that manages logistics, or manages cars,

03:08:41 --> 03:08:46
a fleet of cars—all that kind of stuff. So it end-to-end generates that for you.

03:08:45 --> 03:08:50
- I think a more intermediate example is take something like Slack or Microsoft

03:08:49 --> 03:08:55
Word. I think if organizations allow it, AI could very easily

03:08:54 --> 03:08:59
implement features end-to-end and do a fairly good job for like

03:08:58 --> 03:09:03
things that you want to try. You want to add a new tab in

03:09:02 --> 03:09:07
Slack that you want to use, and I think AI will be able to do that pretty well.

03:09:06 --> 03:09:10
- Actually, that's a really great example. How far away are we from that?

03:09:09 --> 03:09:12
- Like this year.

03:09:11 --> 03:09:16
- See, I don't know. I don't know.

03:09:15 --> 03:09:18
- I guess I don't know how bad production codebases are, but I think that

03:09:18 --> 03:09:23
within... on the order of a few years, a lot of people are going to be pushed to

03:09:22 --> 03:09:27
be more of a designer and product manager, where you have multiple of these

03:09:26 --> 03:09:31
agents that can try things for you and they might take one to two days to implement

03:09:30 --> 03:09:35
a feature or attempt to fix a bug. And you have these dashboards,

03:09:34 --> 03:09:39
which I think Slack is actually a good dashboard where your agents will talk to you and

03:09:38 --> 03:09:42
you'll then give feedback. But things like,

03:09:42 --> 03:09:47
if I make a website, like, "Do you want a passable logo?" I think these

03:09:46 --> 03:09:50
cohesive design things and the style is going to be very hard

03:09:50 --> 03:09:55
for models and deciding on what to add the next time.

03:09:54 --> 03:09:58
- I just... Okay. I hang out with a lot of programmers and some of them

03:09:57 --> 03:10:06
are a little bit on the skeptical side in general. That's just their vibe. I

03:10:05 --> 03:10:10
just think there's a lot of complexity involved in adding features to complex

03:10:09 --> 03:10:14
systems. Like, if you look at the browser, Chrome.

03:10:13 --> 03:10:18
If I wanted to add a feature, if I wanted to have tabs

03:10:17 --> 03:10:22
as opposed to up top, I want them on the left side.

03:10:21 --> 03:10:27
Interface-wise, right? I think we're not... This is not a next-year thing.

03:10:26 --> 03:10:30
- One of the Claude releases this year, one of their tests was: we give it a piece of

03:10:29 --> 03:10:34
software and leave Claude to run to recreate it entirely, and it could already

03:10:33 --> 03:10:38
almost rebuild Slack from scratch, just given the

03:10:37 --> 03:10:42
parameters of the software and left in a sandbox environment to do that.

03:10:41 --> 03:10:45
- So the "from scratch" part, I like almost better.

03:10:44 --> 03:10:48
- So it might be that smaller and newer companies are advantaged, and they're like,

03:10:48 --> 03:10:54
"We don't have the bloat and complexity, and therefore this feature exists."

03:10:53 --> 03:10:58
- And I think this gets to the point you mentioned, that some people you talk

03:10:57 --> 03:11:02
to are skeptical. I think that's not because the LLM can't do

03:11:01 --> 03:11:06
X, Y, Z. It's because people don't want it to do it this way.

03:11:05 --> 03:11:10
- Some of that could be a skill issue on the human side. We have to be

03:11:09 --> 03:11:14
honest with ourselves. And some of that could be an underspecification issue.

03:11:13 --> 03:11:21
So, programming, it's like you're just assuming... This is like an

03:11:20 --> 03:11:25
issue with communication in relationships and friendships. You're assuming the

03:11:24 --> 03:11:28
LLM is supposed to read your mind. This is where spec-driven design is

03:11:27 --> 03:11:33
really important. Using natural language to specify what you want.

03:11:32 --> 03:11:37
- If you talk to people at the labs, they use these in their training

03:11:36 --> 03:11:41
and production code. Claude Code is built with Claude Code, and they all

03:11:40 --> 03:11:45
use these things extensively. Dario talks about how much of Claude's

03:11:44 --> 03:11:50
code... It's like these people are slightly ahead in terms of the capabilities

03:11:49 --> 03:11:54
they have and what they probably spend on inference.

03:11:53 --> 03:11:58
They could spend 10 to 100x as much as we're spending on a lowly

03:11:57 --> 03:12:03
$100 or $200 a month plan. They truly let it rip. And I think that,

03:12:03 --> 03:12:08
with the pace of progress that we have, it seems like-

03:12:07 --> 03:12:12
a year ago we didn't have Claude Code and we didn't really have reasoning

03:12:11 --> 03:12:16
models. The difference between sitting here today and what we can do with these

03:12:15 --> 03:12:19
models is significant, and there's a lot of

03:12:18 --> 03:12:24
low-hanging fruit to improve them. The failure modes are pretty dumb. Like-

03:12:23 --> 03:12:28
"Claude, you tried to use a CLI command I don't have installed 14 times, and then

03:12:27 --> 03:12:32
I sent you the command to run." That, from a modeling perspective,

03:12:31 --> 03:12:35
is pretty fixable. So I don't know.

03:12:34 --> 03:12:39
- I agree with you. I've been becoming more and more bullish in

03:12:38 --> 03:12:43
general. Speaking to what you're articulating, I think it is a

03:12:42 --> 03:12:47
human skill issue. Anthropic is leading the

03:12:46 --> 03:12:51
way, along with other companies, in understanding

03:12:50 --> 03:12:55
how to best use the models for programming; therefore, they're effectively

03:12:54 --> 03:12:58
using them. There are a lot of programmers on the outskirts who

03:12:58 --> 03:13:02
don't... I mean, there's not a really good

03:13:01 --> 03:13:05
guide on how to use them. People are trying to figure it out, but-

03:13:04 --> 03:13:09
It might be very expensive. The entry point might be $2,000 a

03:13:08 --> 03:13:14
month, which is only for tech companies and rich people. That could be it.

03:13:13 --> 03:13:18
- But it might be worth it. If the final result is a working

03:13:17 --> 03:13:22
software system, it might be worth it. By the way, it's funny how we converged from

03:13:21 --> 03:13:26
the discussion of timeline to AGI to something more pragmatic and

03:13:25 --> 03:13:29
useful. Is there anything concrete, interesting,

03:13:28 --> 03:13:33
useful, and profound to be said about the timeline to AGI and

03:13:32 --> 03:13:40
ASI? Or are these discussions a bit too detached from the day to day?

03:13:39 --> 03:13:43
- There are interesting bets. A lot of people are trying to do RLVR—

03:13:42 --> 03:13:47
Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards—in real scientific domains,

03:13:46 --> 03:13:51
where startups with hundreds of millions of funding have wet labs where they're

03:13:50 --> 03:13:55
having language models propose hypotheses that are tested in the real

03:13:54 --> 03:14:01
world. I would say that they're early, but with the pace of progress, it's like-

03:14:00 --> 03:14:05
...maybe they're early by six months and they make it because they

03:14:04 --> 03:14:09
were there first, or maybe they're early by eight years; you don't know.

03:14:08 --> 03:14:15
That type of moonshot to branch this momentum into other sciences

03:14:15 --> 03:14:19
would be very transformative. If,

03:14:19 --> 03:14:24
AlphaFold moments happen in all sorts of other scientific domains

03:14:23 --> 03:14:28
by a startup solving this. I think there are startups—maybe Harmonic is

03:14:27 --> 03:14:31
one—where they're going all in on language models plus Lean for

03:14:31 --> 03:14:35
math. You had another guest where you talked about this recently, and

03:14:34 --> 03:14:39
we don't know exactly what's going to fall out of

03:14:38 --> 03:14:43
spending $100 million on that model. Most of them will

03:14:42 --> 03:14:47
fail, but a couple might be big breakthroughs that are very

03:14:46 --> 03:14:50
different than ChatGPT or Claude Code type software

03:14:50 --> 03:14:55
xperiences. A tool that's only good for a

03:14:54 --> 03:14:58
PhD mathematician but makes them 100X effective...

03:14:58 --> 03:15:03
- I agree. I think this will happen in a lot of domains, especially

03:15:02 --> 03:15:06
domains that have a lot of resources,

03:15:05 --> 03:15:10
like finance, legal, and pharmaceutical companies.

03:15:09 --> 03:15:14
But then again, is it really AGI? Because we are now

03:15:13 --> 03:15:18
specializing it again. Is it really that much different from back

03:15:17 --> 03:15:22
in the day when we had specialized algorithms? It's just the same thing,

03:15:21 --> 03:15:26
way more sophisticated, but I don't know,

03:15:25 --> 03:15:30
is there a threshold for AGI? I think the real cool thing here is that

03:15:29 --> 03:15:34
we have foundation models we can specialize. That's like the

03:15:33 --> 03:15:38
breakthrough. Right now, I think we are not there yet because, first,

03:15:37 --> 03:15:40
it's too expensive, but also,

03:15:39 --> 03:15:44
ChatGPT doesn't just give away their model to customize it. I think

03:15:44 --> 03:15:48
once that's true... And I can imagine this as a business model,

03:15:47 --> 03:15:52
where OpenAI says at some point,

03:15:51 --> 03:15:56
"Hey, Bank of America, for $100 million we will do your custom model,"

03:15:55 --> 03:16:00
something like that. I think that will be the huge economic

03:15:59 --> 03:16:02
value-add. The other thing, though, is

03:16:02 --> 03:16:06
also... Companies, I mean, what is the differentiating

03:16:06 --> 03:16:10
factor? If everyone uses the same LLM, if everyone uses

03:16:09 --> 03:16:14
ChatGPT, they will all do the same thing. Well, if

03:16:13 --> 03:16:18
everyone is moving in lockstep, but companies want to have a

03:16:17 --> 03:16:22
competitive advantage, there is no way around using some of their

03:16:21 --> 03:16:27
private data and specializing. It's gonna be interesting.

03:16:26 --> 03:16:31
- Seeing the pace of progress, it does feel like things are coming. I don't

03:16:30 --> 03:16:36
hink the AGI and ASI thresholds are particularly useful.

03:16:35 --> 03:16:40
- I think the real question, and this relates to the remote worker

03:16:39 --> 03:16:48
thing, is: when are we going to see a big, obvious leap in economic impact?

03:16:47 --> 03:16:52
Because currently there's not been an obvious leap in

03:16:51 --> 03:16:56
the economic impact of LLM models, for example.

03:16:55 --> 03:17:00
And that's, you know, aside from AGI or ASI, all that

03:16:59 --> 03:17:07
stuff, there's a real question of, "When are we going to see a GDP..." "...jump?"

03:17:06 --> 03:17:11
- Yeah, what is the GDP made up of? A lot of it is financial

03:17:10 --> 03:17:14
services, so I don't know what this is.

03:17:13 --> 03:17:14
- Right, GDP is a-

03:17:13 --> 03:17:18
It's just hard for me to think about the GDP bump, but

03:17:18 --> 03:17:23
I would say that software development becomes valuable in a different

03:17:22 --> 03:17:26
way, when you no longer have to look at the code anymore.

03:17:25 --> 03:17:30
When Claude Code will make you a small business.

03:17:29 --> 03:17:34
Which is essentially, Claude can set up your website, your bank account, your email, and your

03:17:33 --> 03:17:38
whatever else. And you just have to express what you're trying

03:17:37 --> 03:17:41
to put into the world. That's not just an

03:17:41 --> 03:17:46
enterprise market, but it is hard. I don't know how you get people to try doing

03:17:45 --> 03:17:50
that. I guess if ChatGPT can do it—people are trying ChatGPT.

03:17:49 --> 03:17:54
- I think it boils down to the scientific question of, "How hard is tool use

03:17:53 --> 03:17:58
to solve?" Because a lot of the stuff you're implying,

03:17:57 --> 03:18:02
the remote work stuff, is tool use. It's like... computer

03:18:01 --> 03:18:05
use, like how you have an LLM that goes out there,

03:18:05 --> 03:18:09
this agentic system, and does something in the world, and

03:18:08 --> 03:18:12
only screws up 1% of the time.

03:18:11 --> 03:18:13
- Computer use-

03:18:12 --> 03:18:13
Or less.

03:18:12 --> 03:18:17
- ...is a good example of what labs care about and we haven't seen a lot of progress on.

03:18:16 --> 03:18:21
We saw multiple demos in 2025 of, like,

03:18:20 --> 03:18:25
Claude can use your computer, or OpenAI had operator, and they all suck.

03:18:24 --> 03:18:29
They're investing money in this, and I think that'll be a good

03:18:28 --> 03:18:32
example. Whereas actually, something where it just seems

03:18:32 --> 03:18:36
like taking over the whole screen seems a lot harder

03:18:35 --> 03:18:40
than having an API that they can call in the back end.

03:18:39 --> 03:18:44
For some of that, you have to set up a different environment for them all to work in. They're

03:18:43 --> 03:18:48
not working on your MacBook; they are individually interfacing with

03:18:47 --> 03:18:52
Google and Amazon and Slack, and they handle all these things in a very

03:18:51 --> 03:18:56
different way than humans do. So some of this might be structural blockers.

03:18:55 --> 03:19:00
- Also, specification-wise, I think the problem is

03:18:59 --> 03:19:04
for arbitrary tasks, well, you still have to specify what you

03:19:03 --> 03:19:08
want your LLM to do. And how do you do that? What is the

03:19:07 --> 03:19:12
nvironment? How do you specify? You can say what the end goal is, but

03:19:11 --> 03:19:16
if it can't solve the end goal... with LLMs, if you ask it for text, it

03:19:15 --> 03:19:20
can always clarify or do sub-steps. How do you put that

03:19:19 --> 03:19:24
information into a system that, let's say, books a travel trip for you? You can say,

03:19:23 --> 03:19:28
"You screwed up my credit card information," but even to get it to that point,

03:19:27 --> 03:19:32
how do you, as a user, guide the

03:19:31 --> 03:19:37
model before it can even attempt that? I think the interface is really hard.

03:19:36 --> 03:19:40
- Yeah, it has to learn a lot about you specifically.

03:19:39 --> 03:19:44
And this goes to continual learning, about the general mistakes

03:19:43 --> 03:19:49
that are made throughout, and then mistakes that are made through you.

03:19:48 --> 03:19:52
- All the AI interfaces are getting set up to ask humans for input.

03:19:51 --> 03:19:55
I think Claude Code we talked about a lot.

03:19:54 --> 03:19:59
It asks feedback and questions. If it doesn't have enough specification

03:19:58 --> 03:20:03
your plan or your desired goal, it starts to ask questions, "Would you rather?"

03:20:02 --> 03:20:05
We talked about Memory, which saves across chats.

03:20:06 --> 03:20:11
Its first implementation is kind of odd, where it'll mention my

03:20:10 --> 03:20:12
dog's name or something in a chat.

03:20:11 --> 03:20:16
I'm like, "You don't need to be subtle about this. I don't care."

03:20:15 --> 03:20:19
But things that are emerging, ChatGPT has the Pulse feature.

03:20:19 --> 03:20:23
Which is like a curated couple of paragraphs with

03:20:23 --> 03:20:27
links to something to look at, and people talk about how models

03:20:27 --> 03:20:31
are going to ask you questions. Which I think is a

03:20:30 --> 03:20:35
very... It's probably going to work. The language model knows

03:20:34 --> 03:20:38
you had a doctor appointment and asks, "Hey, how are you feeling after that?"

03:20:37 --> 03:20:42
Which again goes into the territory where humans are very susceptible

03:20:41 --> 03:20:46
to this, and there's a lot of social change to come. But also,

03:20:45 --> 03:20:50
they're experimenting with having the models engage. Some people like this

03:20:49 --> 03:20:54
Pulse feature, which processes your chats and automatically searches

03:20:53 --> 03:20:59
for information and puts it in the app. So there are a lot of things coming.

03:20:58 --> 03:21:03
- I used that feature before, and I always feel bad because it does

03:21:02 --> 03:21:07
that every day, and I rarely check it out. It's like, how much compute

03:21:06 --> 03:21:12
is burned on something I don't even look at, you know? It's kind of like, "Oh..."

03:21:11 --> 03:21:16
- There's also a lot of idle compute in the world, so don't feel too bad.

03:21:16 --> 03:21:21
- Okay. Do you think new ideas might be needed?

03:21:20 --> 03:21:25
Is it possible that the path to AGI, however we define that,

03:21:24 --> 03:21:29
to solve computer use more generally, to solve biology and chemistry

03:21:30 --> 03:21:38
and physics—sort of the Dario Amodei definition of AGI? Do you think it's

03:21:37 --> 03:21:46
possible that totally new ideas are needed? Non-LLM, non-RL ideas?

03:21:45 --> 03:21:50
What might they look like? We're going into philosophy land a bit.

03:21:50 --> 03:21:55
- For something like a singularity to happen, I would say yes.

03:21:54 --> 03:21:59
The new ideas could be architectures or training algorithms,

03:21:58 --> 03:22:03
fundamental deep learning things. But in that nature,

03:22:02 --> 03:22:07
they're pretty hard to predict. I think we won't get very

03:22:06 --> 03:22:11
far even without those advances. We might get the software

03:22:10 --> 03:22:15
solution, but it might stop at software and not do computer use

03:22:14 --> 03:22:18
without more innovation. So I think that a lot of progress will be

03:22:17 --> 03:22:22
coming, but if you're gonna zoom out, there's still ideas in the

03:22:21 --> 03:22:25
next 30 years that are gonna look like that was a

03:22:24 --> 03:22:29
major scientific innovation that enabled the

03:22:28 --> 03:22:33
next chapter of this. And I don't know if it comes in one year or in 15 years.

03:22:32 --> 03:22:37
- Yeah. I wonder if the bitter lesson holds true for the next 100 years, what that looks

03:22:37 --> 03:22:38
like.

03:22:37 --> 03:22:42
- If scaling laws are fundamental in deep learning, I think the bitter lesson

03:22:41 --> 03:22:46
will always apply, which is compute will become more

03:22:45 --> 03:22:50
abundant, but even within abundant compute, the ones

03:22:49 --> 03:22:53
that have a steeper scaling law slope or a better offset—

03:22:53 --> 03:22:57
like, this is a 2D plot of performance and compute—and like

03:22:56 --> 03:23:02
ven if there's more compute available, the ones that get 100x out of it will win.

03:23:01 --> 03:23:07
- It might be something like literally computer clusters orbiting Earth

03:23:07 --> 03:23:10
with solar panels.

03:23:09 --> 03:23:14
- The problem with that is heat dissipation. You get all the radiation from the sun

03:23:13 --> 03:23:18
and don't have any air to dissipate heat. But there is a lot of space to put clusters.

03:23:17 --> 03:23:22
There's a lot of solar energy there and you could figure out the heat dissipation,

03:23:21 --> 03:23:26
as there is a lot of energy and there probably could be engineering will to solve

03:23:25 --> 03:23:28
the heat problem— so there could be.

03:23:27 --> 03:23:31
- Is it possible—and we should say that it definitely is possible—

03:23:30 --> 03:23:40
that we're basically going to be plateauing this year? Not in terms of—

03:23:39 --> 03:23:44
the system capabilities, but what they actually mean for

03:23:43 --> 03:23:51
human civilization. So on the coding front, really nice websites will be built.

03:23:50 --> 03:23:53
Very nice auto-complete.

03:23:53 --> 03:24:02
Very nice way to understand code bases and maybe help debug, but really just a

03:24:01 --> 03:24:05
very nice helper on the coding front. It can help research

03:24:04 --> 03:24:09
mathematicians do some math. It can help you with

03:24:08 --> 03:24:13
shopping. It's a nice helper. It's Clippy on

03:24:12 --> 03:24:20
steroids. What else? It may be a good education tool and all that kind of stuff,

03:24:19 --> 03:24:24
but computer use turns out extremely difficult to

03:24:23 --> 03:24:28
solve. So I'm trying to frame the cynical

03:24:27 --> 03:24:32
case in all these domains where there's not a really

03:24:31 --> 03:24:36
huge economic impact, but realize how costly it is to train these

03:24:35 --> 03:24:40
systems at every level, both the pre-training and the inference,

03:24:39 --> 03:24:44
how costly the inference is, the reasoning, all of that.

03:24:43 --> 03:24:48
Like, is that possible? And how likely is that, do you think?

03:24:47 --> 03:24:52
- When you look at the models, there are so many obvious things to improve and

03:24:51 --> 03:24:56
it takes a long time to train these models and to do this art,

03:24:55 --> 03:25:00
and it'll take us with the ideas that we have multiple years

03:24:59 --> 03:25:04
to actually saturate in terms of whatever benchmark or

03:25:03 --> 03:25:08
performance we are searching for. It might serve very narrow

03:25:07 --> 03:25:12
niches; like the average ChatGPT 800 million user might

03:25:11 --> 03:25:16
not get a lot of benefit out of this, but it is going to serve different

03:25:15 --> 03:25:18
populations by getting better at different things.

03:25:18 --> 03:25:22
- But I think what everybody's chasing now is a

03:25:22 --> 03:25:26
general system that's useful to everybody. So, okay, so if

03:25:26 --> 03:25:28
that's not... That can plateau, right?

03:25:28 --> 03:25:32
- I think that dream is actually kind of dying. As you talked about with the

03:25:31 --> 03:25:34
specialized models where it's like...

03:25:34 --> 03:25:39
And multimodal is often... Video generation is a totally different thing. Thing.

03:25:39 --> 03:25:42
- "That dream is kind of dying" is a big statement,

03:25:42 --> 03:25:47
because I don't know if it's dying. If you ask the actual frontier

03:25:46 --> 03:25:49
lab people, they... I mean, they're still chasing it, right?

03:25:48 --> 03:25:53
- I do think they are still rushing to get the next model out,

03:25:52 --> 03:25:57
which will be much better than the... "Much" is a relative term, but it will

03:25:56 --> 03:26:01
be better than the previous one. And I can't see them slowing down.

03:26:00 --> 03:26:04
I just think the gains will be made or felt more

03:26:03 --> 03:26:08
through not only scaling the model, but

03:26:07 --> 03:26:12
now... I feel like there's a lot of tech debt. It's like, "Well, let's just put the

03:26:11 --> 03:26:13
better model in there."

03:26:13 --> 03:26:18
Better model, better model. And now people are like, "Okay, let's also

03:26:17 --> 03:26:21
at the same time improve everything around it too."

03:26:20 --> 03:26:24
Like the engineering of the context and inference scaling.

03:26:23 --> 03:26:28
The big labs will still keep doing that. And now also the

03:26:27 --> 03:26:32
smaller labs will catch up, because now they are hiring more.

03:26:31 --> 03:26:36
There will be more people and LLMs. It's kind of like a circle.

03:26:35 --> 03:26:40
They also make them more productive and it's just... It's like amplification.

03:26:39 --> 03:26:44
I think what we can expect is amplification, but not like a change

03:26:43 --> 03:26:47
of any... not like a paradigm change. I don't think that is true, but

03:26:46 --> 03:26:51
everything will be just amplified and amplified, and I can see that

03:26:50 --> 03:26:53
continuing for a long time, you know?

03:26:52 --> 03:26:57
- Yeah. I guess my statement that the dream is dying depends on exactly

03:26:56 --> 03:27:01
what you think it's gonna be doing. Like, Claude Code is a general model

03:27:00 --> 03:27:05
that can do a lot of things, but it's not necessarily...

03:27:05 --> 03:27:10
It depends a lot on integrations. I bet Claude Code could do a fairly good job

03:27:09 --> 03:27:14
of doing your email, and the hardest part is figuring out how to give information

03:27:13 --> 03:27:18
to it and how to get it to be able to send your emails.

03:27:17 --> 03:27:22
But that's just kind of like... I think it goes back to what is the

03:27:21 --> 03:27:26
"one model to rule everything" ethos, which is just like a thing in the

03:27:25 --> 03:27:30
cloud that handles your entire digital life and is way smarter than everybody.

03:27:29 --> 03:27:33
It's like it's operating in a...

03:27:34 --> 03:27:39
So it's an interesting leap of faith to go from "Claude Code becomes that,"

03:27:39 --> 03:27:46
which in some ways is... There are some avenues for that, but I do think that the

03:27:46 --> 03:27:50
rhetoric of the industry is a little bit different.

03:27:49 --> 03:27:53
- I think the immediate thing we will feel next as a normal

03:27:52 --> 03:27:57
person using LLMs will probably be related to something

03:27:56 --> 03:28:01
trivial, like making figures. Right now, LLMs are terrible at

03:28:00 --> 03:28:05
making figures. Is it because we are getting served the cheap models

03:28:04 --> 03:28:09
with much less inference compute than behind the scenes? Maybe some.

03:28:09 --> 03:28:14
Like, there are some ways to get better figures, but if you ask today,

03:28:13 --> 03:28:18
..."Draw a flowchart of X, Y, Z," it's most of the time terrible.

03:28:18 --> 03:28:22
And it is a very simple task for a human. I think it's almost

03:28:22 --> 03:28:26
easier sometimes to draw something than to write something.

03:28:25 --> 03:28:29
- Yeah, the multimodal understanding does feel like something that is odd...

03:28:28 --> 03:28:32
that it's not better solved.

03:28:31 --> 03:28:36
- I think we're not saying one obvious thing that we're not realizing,

03:28:35 --> 03:28:40
that's a gigantic thing that's hard to measure, which is making all of

03:28:39 --> 03:28:47
human knowledge accessible— —to the entire world.

03:28:46 --> 03:28:51
One thing that is hard to articulate is the huge difference between

03:28:50 --> 03:28:55
Google Search and an LLM. I feel like I can basically

03:28:54 --> 03:29:05
ask an LLM anything and get an answer, and it's doing less and less hallucination.

03:29:04 --> 03:29:08
And that means understanding my own life,

03:29:08 --> 03:29:12
figuring out a career trajectory, solving the problems all around me,

03:29:11 --> 03:29:16
learning about anything through human history.

03:29:16 --> 03:29:22
I feel like nobody's really talking about that,

03:29:22 --> 03:29:26
because they just immediately take it for granted that this is awesome.

03:29:25 --> 03:29:30
That's why everybody's using it: because you get answers for stuff.

03:29:29 --> 03:29:34
Think about the impact across time.

03:29:33 --> 03:29:38
This is not just in the United States; it's all across the world.

03:29:37 --> 03:29:41
Kids throughout the world being able to learn these ideas—

03:29:41 --> 03:29:49
the impact that has across time is probably... ...That's the real impact.

03:29:48 --> 03:29:53
Talk about GDP; it won't be like a leap. It'll be...

03:29:52 --> 03:29:57
that's how we get to Mars, that's how we build these things,

03:29:56 --> 03:30:01
that's how we have a million new OpenAIs and all the innovation from there.

03:30:00 --> 03:30:07
It's this quiet force that permeates everything: human knowledge.

03:30:06 --> 03:30:11
- I agree with you. In a sense, it makes knowledge more accessible,

03:30:10 --> 03:30:14
but it also depends on what the topic is.

03:30:13 --> 03:30:22
For something like math, you can ask it questions and it answers,

03:30:21 --> 03:30:29
but if you want to learn a topic from scratch, the sweet spot is still elsewhere.

03:30:28 --> 03:30:33
There are really good math textbooks laid out linearly,

03:30:32 --> 03:30:37
and that is a proven strategy to learn a topic.

03:30:36 --> 03:30:39
It makes sense, if you start from zero,

03:30:39 --> 03:30:44
to use information-dense text to soak it up,

03:30:43 --> 03:30:48
but then you use the LLM to make infinite exercises. Like,

03:30:47 --> 03:30:52
you have problems in a certain area or have questions that something's-

03:30:51 --> 03:30:56
uncertain about certain things, you ask it to

03:30:55 --> 03:30:59
generate example problems, you solve them,

03:30:59 --> 03:31:03
and you need more background knowledge, you ask it to generate

03:31:02 --> 03:31:09
that. But then... it won't give you anything, let's

03:31:08 --> 03:31:13
ay, that is not in the textbook. It's just packaging it differently,

03:31:12 --> 03:31:14
if that makes sense.

03:31:13 --> 03:31:18
But then there are things I feel like where it also adds value in a

03:31:17 --> 03:31:22
more timely sense, where there is no good

03:31:21 --> 03:31:25
alternative besides a human doing it on the fly. For example, if

03:31:25 --> 03:31:29
you're planning to go to Disneyland and you try to

03:31:28 --> 03:31:33
figure out which tickets to buy for which park when, well,

03:31:32 --> 03:31:37
there is no textbook on that. There is no information-dense resource.

03:31:36 --> 03:31:41
There's only the sparse internet, and then there is a lot of value in the

03:31:40 --> 03:31:45
LLM. You just ask it. You have constraints on traveling these days.

03:31:44 --> 03:31:49
I want to go there and there. Please figure out what I need, when and

03:31:48 --> 03:31:52
from where, what it costs and stuff like that, and it is a very

03:31:51 --> 03:31:56
customized, on-the-fly package.

03:31:56 --> 03:32:00
And this is like one of a thousand examples of personalized-

03:31:59 --> 03:32:06
Personalization is essentially pulling information from the sparse internet, the

03:32:05 --> 03:32:07
non-information-dense thing where

03:32:07 --> 03:32:12
there's no better version that exists. It just doesn't exist. You make it

03:32:11 --> 03:32:13
almost from scratch.

03:32:12 --> 03:32:17
- And if it does exist, it's full of- speaking of Disney World, full of-

03:32:16 --> 03:32:25
what would you call it? Ad slop. It's impossible. Take any city in the world,

03:32:25 --> 03:32:30
what are the top 10 things to do? An LLM is just way better to ask

03:32:29 --> 03:32:30
than anything on the internet.

03:32:29 --> 03:32:34
- Well, for now, that's because they're subsidized and they're gonna be paid for

03:32:33 --> 03:32:36
by ads.

03:32:35 --> 03:32:37
- Oh my goodness.

03:32:37 --> 03:32:39
- It's coming.

03:32:38 --> 03:32:45
- No. No. I mean, I'm hoping there's a very clear indication what's an ad

03:32:44 --> 03:32:47
and what's not an ad in that context.

03:32:46 --> 03:32:51
- That's something I mentioned a few years ago. If, I don't know,

03:32:50 --> 03:32:55
if you are looking for a new running shoe, well, is it a

03:32:54 --> 03:32:59
coincidence that Nike maybe comes up first? Maybe, maybe not.

03:32:58 --> 03:33:03
But I think there are clear laws. You have to be clear about that.

03:33:02 --> 03:33:07
I think that's what everyone fears. It's the subtle message in there,

03:33:07 --> 03:33:11
but that also brings us to the topic of ads,

03:33:11 --> 03:33:16
where I think this was a thing. Hopefully, I think for-

03:33:15 --> 03:33:19
in 2025, just because I think it's

03:33:18 --> 03:33:23
they're still not making money in other ways right now.

03:33:22 --> 03:33:27
Having ad spots in there... but the thing is, they couldn't,

03:33:26 --> 03:33:32
because there are alternatives without ads and people would just flock-

03:33:31 --> 03:33:35
to the other products. It's also just crazy how-

03:33:35 --> 03:33:39
yeah, how they're one-upping each other, spending

03:33:39 --> 03:33:42
so much money to just get the users.

03:33:41 --> 03:33:45
- I think so. Like, some Instagram ads— I don't use Instagram,

03:33:44 --> 03:33:52
but I understand the appeal of paying a platform to find users who will

03:33:51 --> 03:33:57
genuinely like your product, and that is the best case of things like Instagram ads.

03:33:56 --> 03:34:01
But there are also plenty of cases where advertising is very awful for

03:34:00 --> 03:34:03
incentives, and I think that a world where

03:34:03 --> 03:34:08
the power of AI can integrate with that positive view of, "I am a person and

03:34:07 --> 03:34:12
I have a small business and I want to make the best, I don't

03:34:11 --> 03:34:16
know, damn steak knives in the world, and I want to sell them to somebody

03:34:15 --> 03:34:20
who needs them." And if AI can make that sort of advertising thing

03:34:19 --> 03:34:24
work even better, that's very good for the world, especially with

03:34:23 --> 03:34:27
digital infrastructure, because that's how the modern web has been

03:34:27 --> 03:34:31
built. But that's not to say that

03:34:30 --> 03:34:35
addicting feeds so that you can show people more content

03:34:34 --> 03:34:39
is a good thing. So, I think that's even what OpenAI would say, is they want to

03:34:38 --> 03:34:43
find a way that can make the monetization upside of

03:34:42 --> 03:34:46
ads while still giving their users agency.

03:34:45 --> 03:34:50
And I personally would think that Google is probably going to be better at figuring out

03:34:49 --> 03:34:53
how to do this, because they already have ad supply

03:34:53 --> 03:34:57
and if they figure out how to turn this demand in their Gemini app into

03:34:56 --> 03:35:01
useful ads, then they can turn it on. And

03:35:01 --> 03:35:05
somebody will figure it out—I don't know if it's this year, but there will be

03:35:04 --> 03:35:07
xperiments with it.

03:35:06 --> 03:35:10
- I do think what holds companies back right now is really just that the

03:35:09 --> 03:35:14
competition is not doing it. It's more like a reputation thing.

03:35:13 --> 03:35:18
It's just, I think people are just afraid right now of ruining or

03:35:17 --> 03:35:20
losing their reputation, losing users,

03:35:19 --> 03:35:24
because it would make headlines if someone launched these ads. But—

03:35:23 --> 03:35:28
- Unless they were great, but the first ads won't be great because it's a hard problem

03:35:27 --> 03:35:29
that we don't know how to solve.

03:35:28 --> 03:35:33
- Yeah, I think also the first version of that will likely be something like on X,

03:35:32 --> 03:35:36
like the timeline where you have a promoted post sometimes in between.

03:35:35 --> 03:35:40
It'll be something where it will say "promoted" or something small, and then there will be an

03:35:39 --> 03:35:44
image. I think right now the problem is: who makes the first move?

03:35:43 --> 03:35:47
- If we go 10 years out, the proposition for ads is that

03:35:47 --> 03:35:51
you will make so much money on ads by having so many users

03:35:50 --> 03:35:55
that you can use this to fund better R&D and make better models, which is why

03:35:54 --> 03:35:59
YouTube is dominating the market for any—

03:35:58 --> 03:36:03
Netflix is scared of YouTube. They have the ads,

03:36:02 --> 03:36:07
they make—I pay $28 a month for Premium. They make at least $28 a month

03:36:06 --> 03:36:11
off of me and many other people. And they're just creating

03:36:10 --> 03:36:15
such a dominant position in video. So I think that's the proposition: that

03:36:15 --> 03:36:18
ads can make you have a sustained advantage.

03:36:17 --> 03:36:22
in what you're spending per user. But there's so much

03:36:21 --> 03:36:27
money in it right now that somebody starting that flywheel

03:36:26 --> 03:36:29
is scary because it's a long-term bet.

03:36:29 --> 03:36:34
- Do you think there'll be some crazy big moves this year

03:36:33 --> 03:36:40
business-wise? Like Google or Apple acquiring Anthropic or something like this?

03:36:40 --> 03:36:44
- Dario will never sell, but we are starting to see some types of

03:36:43 --> 03:36:47
consolidation with Groq for $20 billion

03:36:46 --> 03:36:51
and Scale AI for almost $30 billion and countless

03:36:50 --> 03:36:55
other deals like this that are structured in a way that is

03:36:54 --> 03:36:59
detrimental to the Silicon Valley ecosystem, which is this

03:36:58 --> 03:37:03
licensing deal where not everybody gets brought along,

03:37:02 --> 03:37:07
rather than a full acquisition that benefits the rank-and-file employee

03:37:06 --> 03:37:10
by getting their stock vested. That's a big issue for culture to

03:37:09 --> 03:37:14
address because the startup ecosystem is the lifeblood where,

03:37:14 --> 03:37:21
if you join a startup, even if it's not successful, it might get acquired on a

03:37:20 --> 03:37:25
cheap premium and you'll get paid out for this equity.

03:37:24 --> 03:37:28
These licensing deals are taking the top talent a lot of the time.

03:37:27 --> 03:37:32
The deal for Groq to NVIDIA is rumored to be

03:37:31 --> 03:37:36
tter to the employees, but it is still this antitrust-avoiding thing.

03:37:35 --> 03:37:40
But I think that this trend of consolidation will continue.

03:37:39 --> 03:37:44
Me and many smart people I respect have been expecting

03:37:43 --> 03:37:47
consolidation to have happened sooner, but it seems

03:37:46 --> 03:37:50
like some of these things are starting to turn,

03:37:49 --> 03:37:56
but at the same time, companies are raising ridiculous amounts of money for

03:37:55 --> 03:38:00
easons where I'm like, "I don't know why you're taking that money."

03:37:59 --> 03:38:05
So it's mixed this year, but some consolidation pressure is starting.

03:38:04 --> 03:38:09
- What kind of surprising consolidation will we see? You say Anthropic

03:38:08 --> 03:38:13
is a "never." I mean, Groq is a big one. Groq with a Q, by the way.

03:38:12 --> 03:38:17
- Yeah. There's just a lot of startups and a very high premium on AI startups.

03:38:16 --> 03:38:20
So there could be a lot of

03:38:19 --> 03:38:20
- that kind of stuff, yeah.

03:38:19 --> 03:38:24
- $10 billion range acquisitions, which is really big for a startup that was

03:38:23 --> 03:38:27
maybe founded a year ago. I think

03:38:26 --> 03:38:31
Manus.ai... this company based in Singapore that Meta-founded was

03:38:30 --> 03:38:35
founded eight months ago and then had a $2 billion exit. I think there will

03:38:34 --> 03:38:40
be some other multi-billion dollar acquisitions, like Perplexity.

03:38:39 --> 03:38:40
- Like Perplexity, right?

03:38:40 --> 03:38:44
- Yeah, people rumor them to Apple. I think there's a lot of

03:38:43 --> 03:38:48
pressure and liquidity in AI. There's pressure on

03:38:47 --> 03:38:50
big companies to have outcomes and-

03:38:50 --> 03:38:55
I would guess that a big acquisition gives people leeway

03:38:54 --> 03:38:57
to then tell the next chapter of that story.

03:38:56 --> 03:39:01
- I guess Cursor—we've been talking about code—somebody acquires Cursor.

03:39:00 --> 03:39:02
if somebody acquires Cursor...

03:39:02 --> 03:39:06
- They're in such a good position by having so much user data.

03:39:05 --> 03:39:10
And we talked about continual learning. They had one of the most interesting sentences

03:39:09 --> 03:39:14
in a blog post, which is that they had their new Composer model, which was

03:39:13 --> 03:39:18
a fine-tune of one of these large Mixture of Expert models from China. You can

03:39:17 --> 03:39:23
know that by asking it or because the model sometimes responds in Chinese—

03:39:22 --> 03:39:27
...which none of the American models do. And they had a blog post where they're like,

03:39:26 --> 03:39:31
"We're updating the model weights every 90 minutes based on real-world feedback

03:39:30 --> 03:39:35
from people using it." Which is like the closest thing to real-world RL

03:39:34 --> 03:39:37
happening on a model, and it's just mentioned in one of their blog posts—

03:39:36 --> 03:39:37
- That's incredible.

03:39:36 --> 03:39:39
- which is super cool.

03:39:38 --> 03:39:42
- And by the way, I should say I use Composer a lot because one of the benefits it

03:39:42 --> 03:39:44
has is that it's fast.

03:39:43 --> 03:39:46
- I need to try it 'cause everybody says this.

03:39:45 --> 03:39:52
- And there'll be some IPOs potentially. You think Anthropic, OpenAI, xAI.

03:39:51 --> 03:39:53
- They can all raise so much money

03:39:53 --> 03:39:58
so easily that they don't feel a need to. So long as fundraising is easy, they're not

03:39:57 --> 03:40:01
going to IPO because public markets apply pressure.

03:40:00 --> 03:40:04
I think we're seeing in China that the ecosystem's a little different with both

03:40:03 --> 03:40:08
MiniMax and Z.ai applying for,

03:40:07 --> 03:40:12
filing IPO paperwork, which will be interesting to see how the Chinese market reacts.

03:40:11 --> 03:40:16
I actually would guess that it's going to be similarly hypey to

03:40:15 --> 03:40:20
the US, so long as all this is going and not based on the reality that they're both

03:40:19 --> 03:40:24
losing a ton of money. I wish more of the gigantic American

03:40:23 --> 03:40:28
AI startups were public because it would be very interesting to see how they're spending money

03:40:27 --> 03:40:32
and have more insight. And also just to give people

03:40:31 --> 03:40:35
access to investing in these, because I think they're some of the most

03:40:35 --> 03:40:37
formidable companies—they're the

03:40:37 --> 03:40:42
companies of the era. And the tradition is now for so many of the

03:40:41 --> 03:40:46
big startups in the US to not go public. It's like we're still waiting for Stripe and

03:40:45 --> 03:40:49
the IPO, but Databricks definitely didn't. They raised like a Series G

03:40:49 --> 03:40:54
or something. And I just feel like it's kind of a weird

03:40:53 --> 03:40:56
equilibrium for the market where it's like,

03:40:56 --> 03:40:59
I would like to see these companies go public and

03:40:59 --> 03:41:02
evolve in that way that a company can.

03:41:01 --> 03:41:06
- Do you think 10 years from now some of the frontier model companies are

03:41:05 --> 03:41:09
still around? Anthropic, OpenAI?

03:41:08 --> 03:41:13
- I definitely don't see it as a winner-takes-all unless there truly

03:41:12 --> 03:41:17
is some algorithmic secret that one of them finds that lets this

03:41:16 --> 03:41:21
flywheel. Because the development path is so similar for all of them. Google

03:41:20 --> 03:41:25
and OpenAI have all the same products, and then Anthropic's more

03:41:24 --> 03:41:29
focused, but when you talk to people it sounds like they're solving a lot of the same

03:41:28 --> 03:41:32
problems. So I think... and there's offerings that'll spread out. There's a lot

03:41:31 --> 03:41:37
of... it's a very big cake being made that people are going to take money out of.

03:41:36 --> 03:41:44
- I don't want to trivialize it, but OpenAI and Anthropic are primarily LLM service

03:41:43 --> 03:41:47
providers. And some of the other companies like Google and

03:41:47 --> 03:41:52
xAI, linked to X, do other stuff

03:41:51 --> 03:41:56
too. And so it's very possible, if AI becomes more commodified,

03:41:56 --> 03:42:01
that the companies just providing LLMs will die.

03:42:00 --> 03:42:05
- I think the advantage they have is a lot of users, and I think they will

03:42:04 --> 03:42:12
just pivot. Like Anthropic, I think, pivoted. I don't think they

03:42:11 --> 03:42:16
originally planned to work on code, but they found, "Okay, this

03:42:15 --> 03:42:20
a nice niche, and now we are comfortable and we push on this niche." I can see

03:42:19 --> 03:42:24
the same thing... Let's say hypothetically, I'm not sure

03:42:23 --> 03:42:27
if it will be true, but let's say Google takes all the market share of the general

03:42:27 --> 03:42:32
chatbot. Maybe OpenAI will then focus on some other sub-topic.

03:42:31 --> 03:42:38
They have too many users to go away in the foreseeable future.

03:42:37 --> 03:42:41
- I think Google is always ready to say, "Hold my beer," with AI models.

03:42:40 --> 03:42:44
- I think the question is if the companies can support the

03:42:43 --> 03:42:48
valuations. I see the AI companies being

03:42:47 --> 03:42:52
looked at in some ways like AWS, Azure, and GCP are,

03:42:51 --> 03:42:56
all competing in the same space and all very successful businesses. There's a

03:42:55 --> 03:43:00
chance that the API market is so unprofitable that they go

03:42:59 --> 03:43:04
up and down the stack to products and hardware. They have so much cash that they can build

03:43:03 --> 03:43:08
power plants and data centers, which is a durable advantage now. But there's

03:43:07 --> 03:43:12
also a reasonable outcome that these APIs are so valuable and

03:43:11 --> 03:43:16
so flexible for developers that they become something like

03:43:15 --> 03:43:21
AWS. But AWS and Azure are also going to have these APIs, so

03:43:21 --> 03:43:26
having five or six people competing in the API market is hard. So maybe that's why

03:43:25 --> 03:43:28
they get squeezed out.

03:43:27 --> 03:43:32
- You mentioned "RIP Llama." Is there a path to winning for Meta?

03:43:32 --> 03:43:39
- I think nobody knows. They're moving a lot, so they're signing licensing deals

03:43:38 --> 03:43:43
with Black Forest Labs, which is an image generation company,

03:43:42 --> 03:43:47
or Midjourney. So I think in some ways

03:43:46 --> 03:43:51
on the product and consumer-facing AI front, it's too

03:43:50 --> 03:43:55
early to tell. I think they have some people who are excellent

03:43:54 --> 03:43:59
and very motivated being close to Zuckerberg. So I think there's still a

03:43:58 --> 03:44:04
story to unfold there. Llama is a bit different, where Llama was the

03:44:04 --> 03:44:09
most focused expression of the organization. And I don't see Llama

03:44:08 --> 03:44:13
being supported to that extent. I think it was a very successful

03:44:12 --> 03:44:16
brand for them. So they still might participate in the

03:44:15 --> 03:44:20
open ecosystem or continue the Llama brand into a different

03:44:19 --> 03:44:22
service, because people know what Llama is.

03:44:21 --> 03:44:23
- You think there's a Llama 5?

03:44:24 --> 03:44:27
- Not an open-weight one.

03:44:26 --> 03:44:35
- It's interesting. I think Llama was the pioneering open-weight model. With Llama 1,

03:44:34 --> 03:44:39
2, and 3, there was a lot of love. But I think then,

03:44:38 --> 03:44:43
hypothesizing or speculating, I think the leaders

03:44:42 --> 03:44:47
at Meta, like the upper executives, they... I think they got

03:44:46 --> 03:44:51
very excited about Llama because they saw how popular it was in the community. And then

03:44:50 --> 03:44:55
I think the problem was trying to, let's say, monetize the open—or not

03:44:54 --> 03:44:59
monetize the open source, but use it to make a bigger

03:44:58 --> 03:45:06
splash. It felt almost forced, like developing these very big Llama 4 models

03:45:05 --> 03:45:09
to be on top of the

03:45:08 --> 03:45:13
benchmarks. But I don't think the goal of Llama models is to be on top

03:45:12 --> 03:45:17
of the benchmarks beating, let's say, ChatGPT or other models. I think the goal was to

03:45:16 --> 03:45:21
have a model that people can use, trust, modify, and understand.

03:45:20 --> 03:45:24
So that includes having smaller models. They don't have to be the best models.

03:45:23 --> 03:45:28
And what happened was, these models were, of course... the benchmarks

03:45:28 --> 03:45:33
uggested that they were better than they were because they had specific

03:45:32 --> 03:45:37
models trained on preferences so that they performed well on benchmarks. That's kind of,

03:45:36 --> 03:45:40
like, this overfitting thing to force it to be the best. But then at the same

03:45:39 --> 03:45:44
time, they didn't do the small models that people could use. And I think

03:45:43 --> 03:45:47
that no one could run these big models then. And then there was kind of a

03:45:47 --> 03:45:51
weird thing. I think it's just because people got too excited

03:45:50 --> 03:45:55
about headlines pushing the frontier. I think that's it.

03:45:54 --> 03:45:57
- And too much on the benchmarking side.

03:45:56 --> 03:45:58
- It's too much work.

03:45:57 --> 03:46:01
- I think it imploded under internal

03:46:00 --> 03:46:05
political fighting and misaligned incentives. The researchers want to

03:46:04 --> 03:46:08
build the best models, but there's a layer of organization—

03:46:07 --> 03:46:12
...and management that is trying to demonstrate that they do these things. And then

03:46:11 --> 03:46:20
there are rumors about how, for example, some horrible technical decision was made.

03:46:19 --> 03:46:25
It just seems like it got so bad that it all just crashed out.

03:46:24 --> 03:46:29
- Yeah, but we should also give huge props to Mark

03:46:28 --> 03:46:32
Zuckerberg. I think it comes from Mark, actually, from

03:46:32 --> 03:46:36
Mark Zuckerberg, from the top of the leadership, saying open source is

03:46:35 --> 03:46:40
important. The fact that that leadership exists

03:46:39 --> 03:46:43
means there could be a Llama 5, where they learn the lessons from

03:46:42 --> 03:46:48
benchmarking and say, "We're going to be GPT-OSS—"

03:46:47 --> 03:46:52
...and provide a really awesome library of open source."

03:46:51 --> 03:46:56
- What people say is that there's a debate between Mark and

03:46:55 --> 03:47:00
Alexandr Wang, who is very bright, but much more against open

03:46:59 --> 03:47:03
source. And to the extent that he has a lot of influence over the AI org, it

03:47:02 --> 03:47:07
seems much less likely, because it seems like Mark brought him in for a

03:47:06 --> 03:47:11
fresh leadership eye in directing AI. And

03:47:10 --> 03:47:15
if being open or closed is no longer the defining nature of the model, I

03:47:14 --> 03:47:19
don't expect that to be a defining argument between Mark and

03:47:18 --> 03:47:23
Alex. They're both very bright, but I just have a hard time

03:47:23 --> 03:47:27
understanding all of it because Mark wrote this piece in July of

03:47:27 --> 03:47:32
2024, which was probably the best

03:47:31 --> 03:47:36
blog post at the time, saying "The Case for Open Source AI."

03:47:36 --> 03:47:41
And then July 2025 came around and it was, "We're reevaluating our relationship with

03:47:40 --> 03:47:43
open source." So it's just kind of...

03:47:42 --> 03:47:47
- But I think also the problem... Not the problem, but I think,

03:47:46 --> 03:47:51
well, we may have been a bit too harsh, and that caused some of that.

03:47:50 --> 03:47:55
Because I mean, we as open source developers or the community...

03:47:54 --> 03:47:59
Even though the model was maybe not what everyone hoped

03:47:58 --> 03:48:02
for, it got a lot of backlash. And I think that was unfortunate

03:48:01 --> 03:48:06
because I can see that as a company, they were hoping for

03:48:05 --> 03:48:10
positive headlines. And instead of just getting

03:48:09 --> 03:48:14
no headlines or positive headlines, in turn they got negative

03:48:13 --> 03:48:18
headlines. And then it kind of reflected bad on the company.

03:48:17 --> 03:48:22
I think that is also something where it's maybe a spite reaction,

03:48:21 --> 03:48:26
almost like, "Okay, we tried to do something nice, we tried to

03:48:25 --> 03:48:29
give you something cool, like an open source model, and now you are

03:48:28 --> 03:48:33
kind of being negative about us, even for the company."

03:48:32 --> 03:48:37
So in that sense, it looks like, "Well, maybe then we'll change our mind."

03:48:36 --> 03:48:39
I guess. I don't know.

03:48:38 --> 03:48:44
- Yeah, that's where the dynamics of discourse on

03:48:44 --> 03:48:49
X can lead us, as a community, astray.

03:48:48 --> 03:48:52
Because sometimes it feels random. People pick the thing they like and don't

03:48:51 --> 03:49:00
like. I mean, you can see the same thing with Grok 4.1 and Grok Code Fast 1.0.

03:48:59 --> 03:49:09
I don't think, vibe-wise, people love it publicly. But a lot of people use it.

03:49:09 --> 03:49:13
So if you look to Reddit and X, they don't really give it

03:49:13 --> 03:49:18
praise from the programming community, but they use it.

03:49:17 --> 03:49:22
And the same thing with probably Llama. I don't understand the dynamics of

03:49:21 --> 03:49:25
either positive hype or negative hype. I don't understand it.

03:49:25 --> 03:49:29
- I mean, one of the stories of 2025 is the US filling the gap of Llama,

03:49:29 --> 03:49:34
which is the rise of these Chinese open-weight models, models-

03:49:34 --> 03:49:38
to the point where that was the single issue I've spent a lot of energy on lately,

03:49:37 --> 03:49:42
trying to do policy work to get the US to invest in this.

03:49:41 --> 03:49:44
- So just tell me the story of ADAM.

03:49:43 --> 03:49:48
- The ADAM Project started as me calling it the American DeepSeek

03:49:47 --> 03:49:52
Project, which doesn't really work for DC audiences, but it's the story of

03:49:51 --> 03:49:55
the most impactful thing I can do with my career, which is

03:49:54 --> 03:49:59
that these Chinese open-weight models are cultivating a lot of power,

03:49:58 --> 03:50:03
and there is a lot of demand for building on these open models,

03:50:02 --> 03:50:07
especially in enterprises in the US that are very cagey about Chinese models.

03:50:06 --> 03:50:11
- The ADAM Project, American Truly

03:50:10 --> 03:50:15
Open Models, is a US-based initiative to build and host high-quality,

03:50:14 --> 03:50:18
genuinely open-weight AI models and supporting infrastructure

03:50:17 --> 03:50:22
xplicitly aimed at competing with and catching up to

03:50:21 --> 03:50:26
China's rapidly advancing open-source AI ecosystem.

03:50:25 --> 03:50:30
- I think the one-sentence summary would be that... or two sentences.

03:50:29 --> 03:50:34
One is a proposition that open models are going to be an engine for AI

03:50:33 --> 03:50:38
research because that is what people start with; therefore, it's important to

03:50:37 --> 03:50:41
wn them. And the second one is, therefore, the US

03:50:41 --> 03:50:46
should be building the best models so that the best research happens in the

03:50:45 --> 03:50:50
US, and those US companies take the value from being the

03:50:49 --> 03:50:53
home of where AI research is happening. And without more

03:50:52 --> 03:50:57
investment in open models—we have plots on the website where it's like, "Qwen,

03:50:56 --> 03:51:01
Qwen, Qwen"—it's all these models that are

03:51:00 --> 03:51:05
xcellent from these Chinese companies that are cultivating influence

03:51:04 --> 03:51:09
internationally. I think the US is

03:51:08 --> 03:51:13
pending way more on AI, and the ability to create open models that

03:51:12 --> 03:51:17
are a generation beyond what the cutting edge of closed labs

03:51:16 --> 03:51:21
costs roughly $100 million, which is a lot of money, but

03:51:20 --> 03:51:25
not a lot of money to these companies. Therefore, we need a

03:51:24 --> 03:51:28
centralizing force of people who want to do this. And I think

03:51:29 --> 03:51:33
we got engagement from people pretty much across the full stack, whether

03:51:32 --> 03:51:34
it's policy.

03:51:33 --> 03:51:37
- So there has been support from the administration?

03:51:36 --> 03:51:40
- I don't think anyone technically in government has

03:51:41 --> 03:51:45
igned it publicly, but I know people that have worked in AI

03:51:44 --> 03:51:49
policy, in both the Biden and Trump administrations, are very supportive

03:51:48 --> 03:51:53
of promoting open-source models in the US. I think, for example,

03:51:52 --> 03:51:57
AI2 got a grant from the NSF for $100 million over four

03:51:56 --> 03:52:01
years, which is the biggest CS grant the

03:52:00 --> 03:52:06
NSF has ever awarded, and it's for AI2 to attempt this. It's a starting point.

03:52:05 --> 03:52:10
But the best thing happens when there are multiple organizations building models,

03:52:09 --> 03:52:14
because they can cross-pollinate ideas and build this ecosystem.

03:52:13 --> 03:52:18
I don't think it works if it's just Llama releasing models,

03:52:17 --> 03:52:22
because Llama could go away. The same thing applies for AI2;

03:52:21 --> 03:52:29
I can't be the only one building models. It becomes a lot of time spent on

03:52:29 --> 03:52:34
talking to people, whether in policy... I know NVIDIA is

03:52:33 --> 03:52:38
very excited about this. I think Jensen Huang has been talking about the urgency

03:52:37 --> 03:52:42
for this, and they've done a lot more in 2025, where the

03:52:41 --> 03:52:45
Nemotron models are more of a focus. They've started releasing some

03:52:45 --> 03:52:49
data along with NVIDIA's open models, and very few

03:52:48 --> 03:52:53
companies do this, especially of NVIDIA's size, so there

03:52:52 --> 03:52:57
are signs of progress. We hear about Reflection AI, where they say their

03:52:57 --> 03:53:02
two billion dollar fundraise is dedicated to building US open models, and I feel

03:53:01 --> 03:53:06
and their announcement tweet reads like a blog post, right?

03:53:06 --> 03:53:12
I think that cultural tide is starting to turn. In July,

03:53:12 --> 03:53:17
four or five DeepSeek-caliber Chinese open-weight models and

03:53:16 --> 03:53:21
zero from the US. That's the moment where I realized,

03:53:20 --> 03:53:25
like, "Oh, I guess I have to spend energy on this because nobody else is gonna do it."

03:53:24 --> 03:53:29
So it takes a lot of people contributing together, and I don't say that,

03:53:28 --> 03:53:32
the Adam Project isn't the thing that's helping to move the ecosystem, but it's

03:53:31 --> 03:53:36
people like me doing this sort of thing to get the word out.

03:53:35 --> 03:53:40
- Do you like the 2025 America's AI Action Plan? That

03:53:39 --> 03:53:44
includes open source stuff. The White House AI Action Plan includes

03:53:43 --> 03:53:48
a dedicated section titled "Encourage Open-Source and Open-Weight

03:53:47 --> 03:53:52
AI," defining such models and arguing they have unique value for

03:53:51 --> 03:53:53
innovation and startups.

03:53:52 --> 03:53:56
- Yeah. I mean, the AI Action Plan is a plan,

03:53:55 --> 03:54:00
but largely, I think it's maybe the most

03:53:59 --> 03:54:04
coherent policy document that has come out of the administration, and I hope

03:54:04 --> 03:54:09
that it largely succeeds. I know people that have worked on the AI Action Plan and

03:54:08 --> 03:54:12
the challenges of taking policy and making it real. I have no idea how

03:54:11 --> 03:54:16
to do this as an AI researcher, but largely a lot of things in

03:54:15 --> 03:54:20
that were very real, and there's a huge build-out of AI in the

03:54:19 --> 03:54:24
country. There are a lot of issues that people are hearing about, from water use to

03:54:23 --> 03:54:28
whatever, and we should be able to build things in this country, but also,

03:54:27 --> 03:54:32
we need to not ruin places in our country in the process of

03:54:31 --> 03:54:36
building it, and it's worthwhile to spend energy on. I

03:54:35 --> 03:54:39
think that's a role the federal government plays. They set the agenda.

03:54:38 --> 03:54:43
And with AI, setting the agenda that open-weight should be a

03:54:42 --> 03:54:47
first consideration is a large part of

03:54:46 --> 03:54:49
what they can do and then people think about it.

03:54:49 --> 03:54:53
- Also, for education and talent for these companies, it's very

03:54:52 --> 03:54:56
important because otherwise, if there are only closed

03:54:55 --> 03:55:00
models, how do you get the next generation of

03:54:59 --> 03:55:03
people contributing at some point? Because otherwise, you will

03:55:02 --> 03:55:07
point only be able to learn after you

03:55:06 --> 03:55:11
joined a company. But at that point, how do you hire

03:55:10 --> 03:55:15
talented people? How do you identify talented people? I think open source

03:55:14 --> 03:55:19
is essential for a lot of things, but also even just for

03:55:18 --> 03:55:22
educating the population and training the next generation of

03:55:21 --> 03:55:24
researchers. It's the way, or the only way.

03:55:24 --> 03:55:28
- The way that I could've gotten this to go more viral was to tell a

03:55:27 --> 03:55:32
story of Chinese AI integrating with an authoritarian state,

03:55:31 --> 03:55:36
being ASI and taking over the world, and therefore we need our own American models. But it's very

03:55:35 --> 03:55:40
intentional why I talk about innovation and science in the US

03:55:39 --> 03:55:44
because I think it's both more realistic as an outcome, but also

03:55:43 --> 03:55:48
it's a world that I would like to manifest.

03:55:47 --> 03:55:52
- I would say, though, also even any open-weight

03:55:51 --> 03:55:56
model, I do think, is a valuable model.

03:55:55 --> 03:56:00
- Yeah. And my argument is that we should be in a leading position. But I think it's

03:56:00 --> 03:56:05
worth saying it simply because there are still voices in the AI ecosystem

03:56:04 --> 03:56:10
that say we should consider banning the release of open models due to safety risks.

03:56:09 --> 03:56:14
And I think it's worth adding that, effectively, that's

03:56:13 --> 03:56:18
impossible without making the US have its own great firewall, which is

03:56:17 --> 03:56:21
also known to not work that well because

03:56:21 --> 03:56:25
the cost for training these models, whether it's one to a hundred million dollars, is

03:56:24 --> 03:56:29
attainable to a huge amount of people

03:56:28 --> 03:56:33
in the world that want to have influence, so these models will be trained all over the

03:56:32 --> 03:56:38
world. And we want the models, especially when, like,

03:56:38 --> 03:56:43
I mean, there are safety concerns, but we want this information and tools to flow

03:56:42 --> 03:56:46
freely across the world and into the US so that people can use them and

03:56:45 --> 03:56:51
learn from them. Stopping that would be such a restructuring of our internet

03:56:50 --> 03:56:52
hat it seems impossible.

03:56:51 --> 03:56:56
- Do you think maybe in that case the big open-weight models from China are

03:56:55 --> 03:57:00
actually a good thing in a sense, like, for the US companies? Because maybe

03:56:59 --> 03:57:04
the US companies you mentioned earlier are usually one generation behind

03:57:03 --> 03:57:08
in terms of what they release open source versus what they are using? For example,

03:57:07 --> 03:57:11
gpt-oss might not be the cutting-edge model. Gemini 3 might not

03:57:10 --> 03:57:15
be, but they do that because they know this is safe to release. But then when they

03:57:14 --> 03:57:19
see, these companies see, for example, there is DeepSeek-V3.2,

03:57:18 --> 03:57:23
which is really awesome, and it gets used and there is no

03:57:22 --> 03:57:27
backlash, there is no security risk, that could then, again, encourage them to

03:57:26 --> 03:57:31
release better models. Maybe that, in a sense, is a very positive thing.

03:57:30 --> 03:57:34
- A hundred percent. These Chinese companies have set things into motion that I

03:57:33 --> 03:57:39
think would potentially not have happened if they were not all releasing models.

03:57:38 --> 03:57:45
So I think it was like I'm almost sure that those discussions have been had

03:57:44 --> 03:57:46
by leadership.

03:57:45 --> 03:57:50
- Is there a possible future where the dominant AI models in the

03:57:49 --> 03:57:51
world are all open source?

03:57:50 --> 03:57:55
