- Depends on the trajectory of progress that you predict. If you think saturation in

03:57:54 --> 03:57:59
progress is coming within a few years, so essentially,

03:57:58 --> 03:58:03
within the time where financial support is still very good, then open models

03:58:02 --> 03:58:07
will be so optimized and so much cheaper to run that they'll win out. This

03:58:06 --> 03:58:11
goes back to open source ideas where so many more people will be

03:58:10 --> 03:58:15
putting money into optimizing the serving of these

03:58:14 --> 03:58:18
open-weight common architectures that they will become standards, and

03:58:17 --> 03:58:22
then you could have chips dedicated to them, and it'll be way cheaper than

03:58:21 --> 03:58:26
the offerings from these closed companies that are custom.

03:58:25 --> 03:58:30
- We should say that the AI27 report kinda predicts one

03:58:29 --> 03:58:33
of the things it does from a narrative perspective is that there will be a lot of

03:58:32 --> 03:58:36
centralization. As the AI systems get smarter and

03:58:35 --> 03:58:40
smarter, national security concerns will arise, and

03:58:39 --> 03:58:44
you'll centralize the labs, and they'll become super secretive, and there'll

03:58:43 --> 03:58:45
be this whole race

03:58:45 --> 03:58:49
- ...from a military perspective of how do you... between China and the US.

03:58:48 --> 03:58:53
And so all of these fun conversations we're having about

03:58:52 --> 03:58:57
LLMs... the generals and the soldiers will

03:58:56 --> 03:59:01
come into the room and be like, "All right. We're now in the Manhattan

03:59:00 --> 03:59:03
Project stage of this whole thing."

03:59:02 --> 03:59:07
- I think in 2025, '26, '27, I don't think

03:59:06 --> 03:59:11
something like that is even remotely possible. You can make the same argument

03:59:10 --> 03:59:15
for computers, right? You can say, "Computers are capable and we don't want

03:59:14 --> 03:59:22
he general public to get them." Or chips, even AI chips, but you see how Huawei

03:59:21 --> 03:59:26
makes chips now. It took a few years, but... and I

03:59:25 --> 03:59:30
don't think there is a way you can contain knowledge like that.

03:59:29 --> 03:59:33
I think in this day and age, it is

03:59:33 --> 03:59:38
impossible, like the internet. I don't think this is a possibility.

03:59:37 --> 03:59:42
- On the Manhattan Project thing, I think that a

03:59:41 --> 03:59:46
Manhattan Project-like thing for open models would be pretty reasonable, because

03:59:45 --> 03:59:49
it wouldn't cost that much. But I think that will come.

03:59:48 --> 03:59:53
It seems like culturally, the companies are changing. But I agree with Sebastian

03:59:52 --> 03:59:59
on all of that. I don't see it happening nor being helpful.

03:59:58 --> 04:00:03
- Yeah. The motivating force behind the Manhattan Project was

04:00:02 --> 04:00:09
civilizational risk. It's harder to motivate that for open-source models.

04:00:08 --> 04:00:11
- There's no civilizational risk.

04:00:10 --> 04:00:15
- On the hardware side, we mentioned NVIDIA a

04:00:14 --> 04:00:19
bunch of times. Do you think Jensen and NVIDIA will keep winning?

04:00:18 --> 04:00:23
- I think they have to iterate and manufacture a lot.

04:00:22 --> 04:00:27
And I think they probably... what they're doing,

04:00:26 --> 04:00:31
they do innovate, but I think there's

04:00:30 --> 04:00:35
always the chance that someone does something fundamentally

04:00:34 --> 04:00:38
different, gets very lucky, and then does something. But the

04:00:38 --> 04:00:42
problem is adoption. The moat of NVIDIA

04:00:42 --> 04:00:46
is probably not just the GPU. It's more like the CUDA ecosystem, and that

04:00:45 --> 04:00:50
has evolved over two decades. Even back when I was a

04:00:49 --> 04:00:54
grad student, I was in a lab doing biophysical simulations,

04:00:53 --> 04:00:58
molecular dynamics, and we had a Tesla GPU back then just for the

04:00:57 --> 04:01:02
computations. It was about 15 years ago now. And

04:01:01 --> 04:01:06
they built this up for a long time, and that's the moat, I think.

04:01:05 --> 04:01:10
It's not the chip itself, although they have the

04:01:09 --> 04:01:13
money to iterate, build, and scale. But then it's really

04:01:13 --> 04:01:18
about compatibility. If you're at that scale, why would

04:01:17 --> 04:01:20
you go with something risky where

04:01:19 --> 04:01:22
there are only a few chips they can make per year?

04:01:21 --> 04:01:26
You go with the big one. But then I do think with LLMs

04:01:25 --> 04:01:31
now, it will be easier to design something like CUDA.

04:01:30 --> 04:01:34
It took 15 years because it was hard, but now that we have LLMs, we can

04:01:33 --> 04:01:36
maybe replicate CUDA.

04:01:35 --> 04:01:40
- And I wonder if there will be a separation of training and inference

04:01:39 --> 04:01:48
compute as we stabilize, and more compute is needed for inference.

04:01:47 --> 04:01:52
- That's supposed to be the point of the Groq acquisition. And that's why

04:01:51 --> 04:01:53
part of what Vera Rubin is-

04:01:52 --> 04:01:56
where they have a new chip with no high-bandwidth memory, which is one of the-

04:01:55 --> 04:02:00
or very little, which is one of the most expensive pieces. It's

04:01:59 --> 04:02:04
designed for pre-fill, which is the part of inference

04:02:03 --> 04:02:08
where you essentially do a lot of matrix multiplications. And then you only need

04:02:07 --> 04:02:11
the memory when you're doing this autoregressive generation, and you have the KV cache

04:02:11 --> 04:02:15
swaps. So they have this new GPU that's designed for that specific

04:02:15 --> 04:02:20
use case, and then the cost of ownership per FLOP

04:02:19 --> 04:02:23
or whatever is actually way lower. But I think that

04:02:22 --> 04:02:27
NVIDIA's fate lies in the diffusion of AI still. Their biggest clients

04:02:26 --> 04:02:31
are still these hyperscale companies. Like, Google obviously

04:02:30 --> 04:02:35
can make TPUs. Amazon is making Trainium.

04:02:34 --> 04:02:41
Microsoft will try to do its own things. And so long as the pace of AI progress is

04:02:40 --> 04:02:45
high, NVIDIA's platform is the most flexible and people will want that. But if

04:02:44 --> 04:02:50
there's stagnation, then creating bespoke chips, there's more time to do it.

04:02:50 --> 04:02:55
- It's interesting that NVIDIA is quite active in trying to

04:02:54 --> 04:02:56
develop all kinds of different products.

04:02:55 --> 04:03:02
- They try to create areas of commercial value that will use a lot of GPUs.

04:03:01 --> 04:03:06
- Mm-hmm. But they keep innovating and they're doing a lot of

04:03:05 --> 04:03:07
incredible research, so...

04:03:06 --> 04:03:11
- Everyone says the company's super oriented around Jensen and how

04:03:11 --> 04:03:16
operationally plugged in he is. And it sounds so unlike many other big

04:03:15 --> 04:03:19
companies that I've heard about. And so long as that's the culture, I think that

04:03:19 --> 04:03:23
we can expect that to keep progress happening. And it's like he's still in

04:03:22 --> 04:03:27
the Steve Jobs era of Apple. So long as that is how it

04:03:26 --> 04:03:31
operates, I'm pretty optimistic for their

04:03:31 --> 04:03:35
situation because it's like, it is their top-order problem, and I don't

04:03:35 --> 04:03:39
know if making these chips for the whole ecosystem is the top goal of all

04:03:38 --> 04:03:44
these other companies. They'll do a good job, but it might not be as good of a job.

04:03:43 --> 04:03:48
- Since you mentioned Jensen, I've been reading a lot about

04:03:47 --> 04:03:52
history and about singular figures in history. What do you guys think about the

04:03:51 --> 04:03:55
single man/woman view of history? How important are

04:03:54 --> 04:03:59
individuals for steering the direction of history in the tech sector?

04:03:58 --> 04:04:03
So, you know, what's NVIDIA without Jensen? You mentioned

04:04:02 --> 04:04:07
Steve Jobs. What's Apple without Steve Jobs? What's xAI

04:04:07 --> 04:04:12
without Elon or DeepMind without Demis?

04:04:11 --> 04:04:17
- People make things earlier and faster, whereas scientifically,

04:04:17 --> 04:04:22
many great scientists credit being in the right place at the right time and still making

04:04:21 --> 04:04:25
the innovation, where eventually someone else will still have the

04:04:25 --> 04:04:33
idea. So I think that in that way, Jensen is helping manifest this GPU revolution

04:04:33 --> 04:04:38
much faster and much more focused than it would happen

04:04:37 --> 04:04:42
without having a person there. And this is making the whole AI build-out faster. But I

04:04:41 --> 04:04:46
do still think that eventually, something like ChatGPT would have

04:04:45 --> 04:04:49
happened and a build-out like this would have happened, but it probably would not have

04:04:48 --> 04:04:56
been as fast. I think that's the sort of flavor that is applied.

04:04:55 --> 04:04:59
- These individual people, there are people who are placing bets on something.

04:04:58 --> 04:05:03
Some get lucky, some don't. But if you don't have these people at the helm, it would be more

04:05:02 --> 04:05:06
diffused. It's almost like investing in an ETF versus individual

04:05:06 --> 04:05:11
stocks. Individual stocks might go up or down more

04:05:10 --> 04:05:14
heavily than an ETF, which is more balanced. It will eventually go up over time. We'll

04:05:14 --> 04:05:18
get there. But it's just like, you know, the focus I

04:05:17 --> 04:05:20
think is the thing. Passion and focus.

04:05:19 --> 04:05:24
- Isn't there a real case to be made that without Jensen, there's not a

04:05:23 --> 04:05:27
reinvigoration of the deep learning revolution?

04:05:26 --> 04:05:31
- It could've been 20 years later, is what I would say.

04:05:30 --> 04:05:36
Or like another AI winter could have come if GPUs weren't around.

04:05:35 --> 04:05:39
- That could change history completely because you could think of all the other

04:05:39 --> 04:05:41
technologies that

04:05:40 --> 04:05:45
could've come in the meantime, and the focus of human civilization would get...

04:05:44 --> 04:05:49
Silicon Valley would be captured by different hype.

04:05:48 --> 04:05:53
- But I do think there's certainly an aspect where it was all

04:05:52 --> 04:05:56
planned, the GPU trajectory. But on the other hand, it's also a lot of lucky

04:05:55 --> 04:06:00
coincidences or good intuition. Like the investment

04:05:59 --> 04:06:04
into, let's say, biophysical simulations. I mean, I think it

04:06:03 --> 04:06:08
started with video games and then it just happened to be good at linear algebra

04:06:07 --> 04:06:12
because video games require a lot of linear algebra. And then you have the biophysical

04:06:11 --> 04:06:16
simulations. But still, I don't think the master plan

04:06:15 --> 04:06:20
was AI. I think it happened to be Alex

04:06:19 --> 04:06:24
Krizhevsky. So someone took these GPUs and said, "Hey, let's

04:06:23 --> 04:06:27
try to train a neural network on that." It happened to work really well, and

04:06:26 --> 04:06:31
I think it only happened because you could purchase those GPUs.

04:06:30 --> 04:06:34
- Gaming would've created a demand for faster processors if

04:06:33 --> 04:06:38
NVIDIA had gone out of business in the early days.

04:06:37 --> 04:06:39
That's what I would think. I think that

04:06:39 --> 04:06:47
he GPUs would've been different, but I think GPUs would still exist

04:06:46 --> 04:06:50
at the time of AlexNet and at the time of the Transformer. It was just hard to

04:06:49 --> 04:06:54
know if it would be one company as successful or multiple smaller

04:06:53 --> 04:06:58
companies with worse chips. But I don't think that's a

04:06:58 --> 04:07:02
100-year delay. It might be a decade delay.

04:07:01 --> 04:07:06
- Well, it could be one, two, three, four, five-decade delay. I just can't

04:07:05 --> 04:07:09
see Intel or AMD doing what NVIDIA did.

04:07:08 --> 04:07:12
- I don't think it would be a company that exists.

04:07:11 --> 04:07:14
I think it would be a different company that would rise.

04:07:13 --> 04:07:16
- Like Silicon Graphics or something.

04:07:15 --> 04:07:19
- So yeah, some company that has died would have done it.

04:07:19 --> 04:07:23
- But just looking at it, it seems like these

04:07:22 --> 04:07:27
singular figures, these leaders, have a huge impact

04:07:27 --> 04:07:32
on the trajectory of the world. Obviously, there are incredible teams behind them.

04:07:31 --> 04:07:38
But, you know, having that kind of very singular, almost dogmatic focus-

04:07:37 --> 04:07:41
is necessary to make progress.

04:07:40 --> 04:07:45
- Yeah, I mean, even with GPT, it wouldn't exist if there wasn't a person,

04:07:44 --> 04:07:48
Ilya, who pushed for this scaling, right?

04:07:47 --> 04:07:51
- Yeah, Dario Amodei was also deeply involved in that.

04:07:50 --> 04:07:55
If you read some of the histories from OpenAI, it seems wild thinking about

04:07:54 --> 04:07:58
how early these people were like, "We need to hook up 10,000 GPUs and take all of

04:07:57 --> 04:08:02
OpenAI's compute and train one model." There were a lot of people who didn't want

04:08:01 --> 04:08:03
o do that.

04:08:02 --> 04:08:07
- Which is an insane thing to believe. To believe in scaling

04:08:06 --> 04:08:10
before scaling has any indication that it's going to

04:08:09 --> 04:08:18
materialize. Again, singular figures. Speaking of which, 100 years from now, this

04:08:17 --> 04:08:22
presumably post-singularity, whatever singularity is.

04:08:21 --> 04:08:25
When historians look back at our time now, what

04:08:24 --> 04:08:29
echnological breakthroughs would they really

04:08:28 --> 04:08:33
emphasize as the breakthroughs that led to the singularity?

04:08:32 --> 04:08:37
So far we have Turing to today, 80 years.

04:08:36 --> 04:08:41
- I think it would still be computing, like the umbrella term "computing."

04:08:40 --> 04:08:45
I don't necessarily think that in 100

04:08:44 --> 04:08:48
or 200 years it would be AI. It could still very well be

04:08:47 --> 04:08:52
computers. We are now taking better advantage of them, but

04:08:51 --> 04:08:54
he fact of computing remains.

04:08:53 --> 04:08:58
- It's basically a Moore's Law discussion. Even the

04:08:57 --> 04:09:02
details of CUDA and GPUs won't even be remembered, nor

04:09:01 --> 04:09:08
will all this software turmoil. It'll just be, obviously, compute.

04:09:07 --> 04:09:12
- I generally agree, but is the connectivity of the

04:09:11 --> 04:09:18
internet and compute able to be merged? Or is it both of them?

04:09:17 --> 04:09:22
- I think the internet will probably be related to communication.

04:09:21 --> 04:09:26
It could be a phone, the internet, or satellites.

04:09:25 --> 04:09:30
Compute is more like the scaling aspect of it.

04:09:29 --> 04:09:33
- It's possible that the internet is completely forgotten-

04:09:32 --> 04:09:38
that the internet is wrapped into phone networks, like communication networks.

04:09:38 --> 04:09:43
This is just another manifestation of that, and the real breakthrough

04:09:42 --> 04:09:47
comes from increased compute, or Moore's Law, broadly defined.

04:09:46 --> 04:09:51
- Well, I think the connection of people is very fundamental to it.

04:09:50 --> 04:09:55
it's like, you can talk to anyone. You want to find the

04:09:54 --> 04:09:59
best person in the world for something, they are somewhere in the world. And being able to have

04:09:58 --> 04:10:03
that flow of information—the AIs will also rely on this.

04:10:02 --> 04:10:07
I've been fixating on when I said the dream was dead about

04:10:06 --> 04:10:11
he one central model. The thing that is evolving is people having many agents

04:10:10 --> 04:10:15
for different tasks. People already started doing this with different clouds.

04:10:14 --> 04:10:19
It's described as many AGIs in the data center

04:10:18 --> 04:10:23
where each one manages and they talk to each other. And that is

04:10:22 --> 04:10:26
reliant on networking and the free flow of information.

04:10:26 --> 04:10:30
on top of compute. But networking, especially with

04:10:29 --> 04:10:34
GPUs, is such a part of scaling of compute. The

04:10:33 --> 04:10:37
GPUs and the data centers need to talk to each other.

04:10:36 --> 04:10:41
- Anything about neural networks will be remembered? Like, do you think

04:10:40 --> 04:10:45
there's something very specific and singular to the fact that it's neural

04:10:44 --> 04:10:48
networks that's seen as a breakthrough, like a genius, that you're

04:10:47 --> 04:10:52
basically replicating, in a very crude way, the human mind? The

04:10:51 --> 04:10:55
structure of the human brain, the human mind?

04:10:54 --> 04:10:59
- I think without the human mind, we probably wouldn't have neural

04:10:58 --> 04:11:03
networks, because it just was an inspiration for that. But on the

04:11:02 --> 04:11:07
other end, I think it's just so, so different. I mean, it's digital

04:11:06 --> 04:11:11
versus biological, that I do think it will probably be more

04:11:10 --> 04:11:12
grouped as an algorithm.

04:11:11 --> 04:11:16
- That's massively parallelizable... ...On this particular kind of compute?

04:11:15 --> 04:11:20
- It could have been like genetic computing; genetic algorithms just

04:11:19 --> 04:11:24
parallelized. It just happens that this is more efficient and works better.

04:11:23 --> 04:11:27
- And it very well could be that the LLM, the neural networks, the way

04:11:26 --> 04:11:31
we architect them now is just a small component of the

04:11:32 --> 04:11:34
system that leads to singularity.

04:11:33 --> 04:11:38
- If you think of it in 100 years, I think society can be

04:11:37 --> 04:11:42
changed more with more compute and intelligence because of

04:11:41 --> 04:11:46
autonomy. But looking at this, what are the

04:11:45 --> 04:11:49
things from the Industrial Revolution that we remember? We remember the engine, which is

04:11:48 --> 04:11:52
probably the equivalent of the computer in this.

04:11:51 --> 04:11:56
But there's a lot of other physical transformations that people are aware

04:11:55 --> 04:12:01
of, like the cotton gin and all these things, these

04:12:01 --> 04:12:05
machines that are still known: air conditioning, refrigerators.

04:12:04 --> 04:12:08
Some of these things from AI will still be known.

04:12:08 --> 04:12:13
The word "transformer" could still be known. I would guess that deep

04:12:12 --> 04:12:17
learning is definitely still known, but the transformer might be evolved away

04:12:16 --> 04:12:25
from in 100 years with AGI researchers everywhere. But I think deep

04:12:24 --> 04:12:29
learning is likely to be a term that is remembered.

04:12:28 --> 04:12:33
- And I wonder what the air conditioning and refrigeration of the future is that AI brings.

04:12:32 --> 04:12:37
If we travel forward 100 years from now, we transport there right now,

04:12:36 --> 04:12:41
what do you think is different? How do you think the world looks different?

04:12:40 --> 04:12:45
First of all, do you think there are humans? Do you think there are robots

04:12:44 --> 04:12:47
everywhere walking around?

04:12:46 --> 04:12:50
- I do think specialized robots, for sure, for certain tasks.

04:12:49 --> 04:12:51
- Humanoid form?

04:12:50 --> 04:12:55
- Maybe half-humanoid. We'll see.

04:12:54 --> 04:12:58
I think for certain things, yes, there will be humanoid robots because it's

04:12:57 --> 04:13:02
just amenable for the environment. But for certain tasks,

04:13:01 --> 04:13:06
it might make sense. What's harder to imagine is how we interact

04:13:05 --> 04:13:10
with the devices and what humans do with devices. Well, I mean,

04:13:09 --> 04:13:14
I'm pretty sure it will probably not be the cellphone or the laptop.

04:13:13 --> 04:13:17
Will it be implants?

04:13:16 --> 04:13:19
- I mean, it has to be brain-computer interfaces, right?

04:13:18 --> 04:13:23
I mean, 100 years from now, given the progress we're seeing now—

04:13:22 --> 04:13:29
there has to be... unless there's legitimately a complete

04:13:29 --> 04:13:33
alteration of how we interact with reality.

04:13:33 --> 04:13:37
- On the other hand, cars are older than 100 years, right? And

04:13:37 --> 04:13:41
it's still the same interface. We haven't replaced cars with

04:13:40 --> 04:13:45
something else. We just made them better, but it's still a steering wheel,

04:13:44 --> 04:13:46
still wheels, you know?

04:13:45 --> 04:13:49
- I think we'll still carry around a physical brick of compute

04:13:48 --> 04:13:53
because people want some ability to have a private... Like, you

04:13:52 --> 04:13:57
might not engage with it as much as a phone, but having private information

04:13:56 --> 04:14:00
that is yours as an interface between the rest of the

04:13:59 --> 04:14:04
internet, I think that will still exist. It might not

04:14:03 --> 04:14:07
look like an iPhone and it might be used a lot less, but I still expect

04:14:06 --> 04:14:09
people to carry things around.

04:14:08 --> 04:14:12
- Why do you think the smartphone is the embodiment of private?

04:14:11 --> 04:14:16
There's a camera on it. There's—

04:14:15 --> 04:14:20
- Private for you, like encrypted messages, encrypted photos...

04:14:19 --> 04:14:21
know what your life is.

04:14:22 --> 04:14:26
I guess it's a question of how optimistic on brain-machine interfaces you are.

04:14:26 --> 04:14:30
Is all that just going to be stored in the cloud? Your whole calendar?

04:14:30 --> 04:14:34
It's hard to think about processing

04:14:33 --> 04:14:38
all the information that we can process visually

04:14:37 --> 04:14:42
through brain-machine interfaces presenting something like a

04:14:41 --> 04:14:45
calendar or something to you.

04:14:44 --> 04:14:50
It's hard to just think about knowing, without looking, your email inbox.

04:14:49 --> 04:14:54
Like you signal to a computer and then you just know your email inbox.

04:14:53 --> 04:14:57
Is that something that the human brain can handle being piped

04:14:56 --> 04:15:04
into it non-visually? I don't know exactly how those transformations happen.

04:15:03 --> 04:15:06
Humans aren't changing in 100 years.

04:15:05 --> 04:15:10
I think agency and community are things that people actually want.

04:15:09 --> 04:15:11
- A local community, yeah.

04:15:10 --> 04:15:15
- People you are close to, being able to do things with them

04:15:14 --> 04:15:23
and being able to ascribe meaning to your life and being able to do things.

04:15:22 --> 04:15:27
In 100 years, I don't think that human biology is

04:15:26 --> 04:15:31
changing away from those on a time scale that we can discuss.

04:15:30 --> 04:15:35
And I think that UBI does not solve agency.

04:15:34 --> 04:15:39
I do expect mass wealth, and I hope that it has spread

04:15:38 --> 04:15:43
so that the average life looks very different in 100 years.

04:15:42 --> 04:15:47
But that's still a lot to happen. If you think about countries

04:15:46 --> 04:15:51
that are early in their development process to getting

04:15:50 --> 04:15:56
access to computing and internet, to build all the infrastructure and have

04:15:56 --> 04:16:01
policy that shares one nation's wealth with

04:16:00 --> 04:16:05
another is... I think it's an optimistic view to see

04:16:04 --> 04:16:06
all that happening in 100 years-

04:16:05 --> 04:16:10
...while they are still independent entities and not just

04:16:10 --> 04:16:14
like absorbed into some international order by force.

04:16:13 --> 04:16:18
- But there could be just better, more elaborate, more effective

04:16:17 --> 04:16:22
... social support systems that help alleviate

04:16:21 --> 04:16:26
some levels of basic suffering from the world. You know, the

04:16:25 --> 04:16:30
transformation of society where a lot of jobs are lost in the short term, I

04:16:29 --> 04:16:33
think we have to really remember that each individual job that's lost

04:16:33 --> 04:16:37
is a human being who's suffering. That's like a

04:16:37 --> 04:16:42
... When jobs are lost, the scale is a real tragedy. You can

04:16:41 --> 04:16:46
make all kinds of arguments about economics or how it's all going to

04:16:45 --> 04:16:50
be okay. It's good for the GDP, there's going to be new jobs

04:16:49 --> 04:16:54
created. Fundamentally at the individual level for that

04:16:53 --> 04:16:58
human being, that's real suffering. That's a real

04:16:57 --> 04:17:02
personal sort of tragedy. And we have to not forget that as

04:17:01 --> 04:17:06
the technologies are being developed. And also my hope

04:17:05 --> 04:17:09
for all the AI slop we're seeing is

04:17:08 --> 04:17:14
that there will be a greater and greater premium for the fundamental

04:17:14 --> 04:17:18
aspects of the human experience that are in-person.

04:17:17 --> 04:17:23
The things that we all... Like seeing each other, talking together in-person.

04:17:22 --> 04:17:27
- The next few years are definitely going to be an increased value on

04:17:26 --> 04:17:33
physical goods and events— ...and even more pressure on slop.

04:17:32 --> 04:17:36
So it'll be... the slop is only starting.

04:17:35 --> 04:17:39
The next few years will be more and more diverse ...versions of slop.

04:17:38 --> 04:17:41
- They would be drowning in slop. Is that what—

04:17:40 --> 04:17:45
- So I'm hoping that society drowns in slop enough to snap

04:17:44 --> 04:17:49
out of it and be like, "We can't deal with it. It just doesn't matter."

04:17:48 --> 04:17:54
And then, the physical has such a higher premium on it.

04:17:53 --> 04:17:58
- Even like classic examples, I honestly think this is true,

04:17:57 --> 04:18:02
and I think we will get tired of it. We are already kind of tired of it.

04:18:01 --> 04:18:06
I mean, even art. I don't think art will go away. You have paintings,

04:18:05 --> 04:18:10
physical paintings. There's more value, not just

04:18:09 --> 04:18:14
monetary value, but just more value appreciation for the actual

04:18:13 --> 04:18:17
painting than a photocopy of that painting. It could be a perfect digital

04:18:16 --> 04:18:21
reprint, but there is something when you go to a museum and you look at that art and you see the

04:18:20 --> 04:18:25
real thing and you just think, "Okay. A human." It's like a craft.

04:18:24 --> 04:18:29
You have like an appreciation for that. And I think the same is true for writing, for

04:18:28 --> 04:18:33
talking, for any type of experience...

04:18:32 --> 04:18:37
I do unfortunately think it will be like a dichotomy,

04:18:36 --> 04:18:41
like a fork where some things will be automated.

04:18:40 --> 04:18:44
Like, you know, there are not as many paintings as there used to be, you know, 200 years ago.

04:18:43 --> 04:18:48
There are more photographs, more photocopies. But at the

04:18:47 --> 04:18:52
same time, it won't go away. There will be value in that.

04:18:51 --> 04:18:56
I think the difference will just be, you know, what's the proportion of

04:18:55 --> 04:19:01
that. But personally, I have a hard time reading things where I obviously see

04:19:00 --> 04:19:05
it's obviously AI generated. I'm sorry. It might—it

04:19:04 --> 04:19:09
might be really good information there, but I'm just like, "Nah, not for me."

04:19:08 --> 04:19:13
- I think eventually they'll fool you, and it'll be on platforms that

04:19:12 --> 04:19:17
give ways of verifying or building trust. So you will trust

04:19:16 --> 04:19:22
hat Lex is not AI generated, having been here. So then you have trust in this

04:19:21 --> 04:19:26
channel. But it's harder for new people who don't have that trust.

04:19:25 --> 04:19:30
- Well, that will get interesting because I think fundamentally it's a

04:19:29 --> 04:19:34
solvable problem by having trust

04:19:33 --> 04:19:38
in certain outlets that they won't do it, but it's all going to be trust-based.

04:19:37 --> 04:19:42
There will be systems to authorize, "Okay, this is real. This is not real." There will be some

04:19:41 --> 04:19:46
telltale signs where you can obviously tell this is AI generated and

04:19:45 --> 04:19:50
this is not. But some will be so good that it's hard to tell, and

04:19:49 --> 04:19:55
then you have to trust. And well, that will get interesting and a bit problematic.

04:19:54 --> 04:19:58
- The extreme case of this is to watermark all human content.

04:19:57 --> 04:20:03
So all photos that we take on our own have some watermark until they are edited

04:20:02 --> 04:20:07
or something like this. And software can manage communications with the

04:20:06 --> 04:20:12
device manufacturer- device manufacturer to maintain human editing—

04:20:11 --> 04:20:16
which is the opposite of the discussion to try to watermark AI images. And then

04:20:16 --> 04:20:21
you can make a Google image that has a watermark and use a different Google tool to remove it.

04:20:20 --> 04:20:24
- Yep. It's going to be an arms race, basically.

04:20:23 --> 04:20:27
- And we've been mostly focusing on the positive aspects of

04:20:26 --> 04:20:31
AI. All the capabilities that we've been talking

04:20:30 --> 04:20:35
about can be used to destabilize human civilization with even just

04:20:35 --> 04:20:42
relatively dumb AI applied at scale, and then further, superintelligent

04:20:41 --> 04:20:46
AI systems. Of course, there's the sort of doomer take

04:20:45 --> 04:20:50
that's important to consider as we develop these

04:20:49 --> 04:20:54
technologies. What gives you hope about the future of human

04:20:53 --> 04:20:59
civilization, given everything we've been talking about? Are we going to be okay?

04:20:59 --> 04:21:03
- I think we will. I'm definitely a worrier, both

04:21:02 --> 04:21:11
about AI and non-AI things. But humans do tend to find a way. I think that's what

04:21:10 --> 04:21:14
humans are built for: to have community and find a way to figure out

04:21:13 --> 04:21:18
problems. That's what has gotten us to this point. And to think that

04:21:19 --> 04:21:23
he AI opportunity and related technologies is really

04:21:22 --> 04:21:28
big. And I think that there's big social and political problems to

04:21:28 --> 04:21:33
help everybody understand that. And I think that's what we're staring at a

04:21:32 --> 04:21:36
lot of right now, is like the world is a scary place, and AI is a very

04:21:35 --> 04:21:39
uncertain thing. And it takes a lot of work that is

04:21:40 --> 04:21:44
not necessarily building things. It's like telling people and

04:21:43 --> 04:21:47
understanding people, that the people building AI are historically

04:21:46 --> 04:21:51
not motivated or wanting to do.

04:21:50 --> 04:21:54
But it is something that is probably doable. It just will

04:21:54 --> 04:21:58
take longer than people want. And we have to go through that

04:21:57 --> 04:22:02
long period of like hard, distraught AI

04:22:01 --> 04:22:05
discussions if we want to have the lasting benefits.

04:22:04 --> 04:22:08
- Yeah. Through that process, I'm especially excited that we get a

04:22:07 --> 04:22:12
chance to better understand ourselves,

04:22:12 --> 04:22:16
us at the individual level as humans and at the civilization

04:22:15 --> 04:22:20
level, and answer some of the big mysteries, like what is

04:22:19 --> 04:22:23
this whole consciousness thing going on

04:22:22 --> 04:22:27
here? It seems to be truly special. Like, there's a real miracle in

04:22:26 --> 04:22:31
our mind. And AI puts a mirror to ourselves and we get

04:22:30 --> 04:22:35
o answer some of the big questions about like, what is this

04:22:34 --> 04:22:36
whole thing going on here?

04:22:35 --> 04:22:40
- Well, one thing about that is also what I do think makes us very different

04:22:39 --> 04:22:44
from AI and why I don't worry about AI taking over

04:22:43 --> 04:22:48
is, like you said, consciousness. We humans, we decide what we want to

04:22:47 --> 04:22:52
do. AI in its current implementation, I can't see it

04:22:51 --> 04:22:56
changing. You have to tell it what to do. And so you

04:22:55 --> 04:23:00
have still the agency. It doesn't take the agency from you because

04:22:59 --> 04:23:03
it becomes a tool. You can think of it as a tool. You tell it what to

04:23:02 --> 04:23:07
do. It will be more automatic than other previous tools.

04:23:06 --> 04:23:11
It's certainly more powerful than a hammer, it can figure things out, but

04:23:10 --> 04:23:15
it's still you in charge, right? So the AI is not in charge, you're in

04:23:14 --> 04:23:18
charge. You tell the AI what to do and it's doing it for you.

04:23:17 --> 04:23:22
- So in the post-singularity, post-apocalyptic war between

04:23:21 --> 04:23:26
humans and machines, you're saying humans are worth fighting for?

04:23:27 --> 04:23:32
- 100%. I mean, this is... The movie Terminator, they made in

04:23:31 --> 04:23:35
the '80s, essentially, and I do think, well,

04:23:35 --> 04:23:40
the only thing I can see going wrong is, of course, if

04:23:39 --> 04:23:44
things are explicitly programmed to do the thing that is harmful, basically.

04:23:43 --> 04:23:50
- I think actually in that, in a Terminator type of setup, I think humans win.

04:23:49 --> 04:23:56
I think we're too clever. It's hard to explain how we figure it out, but we do.

04:23:56 --> 04:24:03
And we'll probably be using local LLMs, open source LLMs to help fight the machines.

04:24:04 --> 04:24:08
I apologize for the ridiculousness. Like I said,

04:24:07 --> 04:24:12
Nathan already knows I've been a big fan of his for a long time. Been

04:24:11 --> 04:24:16
a big fan of yours, Sebastian, for a long time, so it's an honor to finally meet

04:24:15 --> 04:24:20
you. Thank you for everything you put out into the world. Thank you for the excellent books you're

04:24:19 --> 04:24:27
writing. Thank you for teaching us. And thank you for talking today. This was fun.

04:24:26 --> 04:24:31
- Thank you for inviting us here and having this human connection, which is actually-

04:24:30 --> 04:24:33
Extremely valuable- human connection.

04:24:33 --> 04:24:38
Thanks for listening to this conversation with Sebastian Raschka and Nathan

04:24:37 --> 04:24:41
Lambert. To support this podcast, please check out our sponsors in the

04:24:41 --> 04:24:45
description, where you can also find links to contact me, ask

04:24:44 --> 04:24:48
questions, give feedback and so on. And now

04:24:48 --> 04:24:53
let me leave you with some words from Albert Einstein.

04:24:52 --> 04:24:57
"It is not that I'm so smart, but I stay with the

04:24:56 --> 04:25:05
